J. Christopher Giancarlo

# Foreword



There was a time, not so long ago in Washington and on Wall Street, when crypto was more often dismissed than understood, framed as a risk to be contained rather than a technology to be explored.


As I began focusing my reporting on digital assets in 2022, conversations with policymakers and financial leaders fell into three camps: skepticism, dismissal, and at times, open resistance to a technology that threatened incumbent financial institutions.


That attitude was not without cause. Crypto was a new, decentralized technology that did not fit neatly into existing securities laws designed for centralized entities, putting the industry at odds with regulators seeking to apply traditional frameworks. Combined with its libertarian roots and preference to operate outside legacy systems, it often struck regulators and securities lawyers as difficult to categorize and, at times, easy to dismiss.


This, along with a series of high-profile market downturns tied to exchange collapses and bank failures, reinforced the perception that the sector was highly volatile and posed significant risks to retail investors. The result was years of enforcement crackdowns and a broader push by Biden-era regulators that effectively shut much of the crypto industry out of the traditional financial system.


Four years later, the narrative has changed dramatically. Crypto and blockchain technology now sit at the center of consequential debates over market structure, banking systems, monetary sovereignty, and the future of the global economy.


In his second term, President Donald Trump has repeatedly called for making the United States the “crypto capital of the world” and has moved to establish a national strategic Bitcoin reserve. Congress has passed legislation establishing a legal framework for stablecoins, an asset class now exceeding $300 billion. As of this writing, it is also considering broader market structure legislation to bring regulatory clarity to market participants. The Securities and Exchange Commission, once known for sweeping enforcement actions against crypto firms, is now working directly with the industry to shape rules that could bring US capital markets on-chain. BlackRock CEO Larry Fink, whose firm launched Wall Street’s most successful Bitcoin ETF, has described tokenization as the next frontier for finance.


Crypto, as they say, is having a moment. But how did we get here and where are we headed?


In these pages, Chris Giancarlo, aka “CryptoDad,” sets out to answer those questions by framing crypto in a broader historical context, drawing on themes that long predate modern finance and trace back to the founding principles of the United States. He positions the rise of digital assets as part of a recurring tension between centralized authority and decentralized innovation, a dynamic that has shaped everything from political revolutions to technological breakthroughs and sits at the heart of debates over financial freedom.


As Giancarlo writes, “… the American Revolution was an initial attack on centralized power by a decentralized network, followed by a synthesis of the two. This synthesis has provided a durable political foundation for 250 years.”


From his role as chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) and his wide-ranging work since, Giancarlo brings a rare vantage point shaped by his experience crafting policy during the first Trump administration, working within institutions once resistant to digital assets while also engaging directly with innovators driving change. The result is a perspective that is both grounded and forward-looking, acknowledging the risks of this nascent technology while making a principled case for its potential.


The New Adventures of CryptoDad is not a story told from the sidelines. It brings you inside the events that defined the treatment of digital assets under the Biden administration and the forces now shaping its next chapter under the Trump administration and beyond. Along the way, Giancarlo offers a firsthand account of pivotal moments, including the run-up to the 2024 presidential election, when the crypto industry emerged as a major political force backing pro-crypto candidates; the first White House Crypto Summit hosted by President Trump; and the confirmation hearing of his former CFTC law clerk, Michael Selig, as chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission.


It also offers an insider’s perspective on the evolving partnership between the SEC and the CFTC, the “sister” agencies that until recently regulated US public markets with little coordination and often clashed over jurisdiction, along with the CFTC’s expanding role in crypto and prediction markets.


Reading it has given me a fresh lens and a deeper understanding of many of the events I’ve covered in real time, from my days at FOX Business to my current role as cofounder and host of the Crypto In America podcast and newsletter.


As a journalist, I’m always trying to answer the question: what comes next? This book offers a window into what lies ahead. If it’s any guide, crypto won’t simply be reduced to green and red numbers on a screen, but it will evolve into something much bigger.


“This challenge to traditional finance is ultimately more transformative than the first wave of the internet,” Giancarlo writes. “In the coming decades, crypto will provide a new foundation for the global financial system.”


It’s hard to think in decades when you’re covering an industry that moves at breakneck speed. Let’s see what the next four years bring.


by Eleanor Terrett,


Cofounder, Crypto in America

# Preface



It was perhaps inevitable, with a father dubbed “CryptoDad,” that I would end up here writing the preface to his second book. I am often asked what it was like being raised by him; the best way to paint that picture is through our daily 7:00 a.m. car rides to middle school. For that 30-minute drive, I received my first course of the day: remedial financial markets from the Chris Giancarlo brain trust. Over his thermos of coffee and my Pop-Tart, I learned the basics of derivatives, mortgage-backed securities, and credit default swaps.


When he mentioned his children’s interest in Bitcoin during his 2018 congressional testimony—the moment that earned him the “CryptoDad” moniker—he wasn’t using a metaphor. My siblings and I were truly fascinated by digital currencies. I eventually pursued this fascination as a career, joining a leading blockchain professional services practice after college. It is fitting, then, that while driving to Maryland to celebrate my college graduation and his departure from the CFTC, we conceived the idea for the Digital Dollar Project.


Throughout my career, I have advised many of the world’s leading financial institutions on digital asset strategies, designing the tokenized currencies and settlement platforms that underpin them. Leading the New York FinTech Innovation Lab has allowed me to work at the edge of AI and tokenization alongside industry leaders, navigating a tectonic shift in finance. Consequently, our current car-ride conversations have evolved into professional debriefing sessions.


As sons often do, I share many of my father’s interests but appreciate them through a different lens. We share a love for cars, but where he recounts the design history of a rare Italian sports car, I am interested in what is under the hood. While you may know J. Christopher Giancarlo as a leading mind in global markets and regulation, my experience has pushed me deeper into the technology and operational utility. When he asked me to join him on this book, it felt like a natural extension of our collaboration—an invitation to complement his policy insights with a technical lens to demystify digital assets.


Finance is among the oldest of human activities, evolving from carved stones to punch cards. Distributed ledger technology represents the next chapter—a transformation that will define the financial system for my generation. I have had the privilege of helping build this next wave of finance in parallel with my father’s stewardship of that transformation. What a great ride!


I hope the stories in this book inspire similar conversations in your own family’s car journeys.


—Luke Giancarlo


Hudson Yards, New York City


April 10, 2026

# Author’s Note



This book is a work of non-fiction—a narrative of my personal recollections, observations, and opinions. Some details reflect my own subjective memory and interpretation of events. In expressing my personal views and opinions, this book does not necessarily reflect the positions of any organization, institution, or individual with which I am affiliated, or which I mention herein including the U.S. Commodity Futures Trading Commission.


I write from the perspective of a participant in and advocate of financial innovation and apt regulation—not a disinterested observer of public affairs. Mine is a point of view developed over four decades of professional engagement with the financial services, financial technology, and digital asset industries, and from serving in senior federal regulatory positions overseeing them. I count many of the people and institutions discussed in this book as colleagues, clients, and friends. I was also, at various points described herein, under consideration for senior appointments in the administration whose financial regulatory policies this book often—but not always—recounts favorably. Readers should weigh my assessments of people, policy, and events with that perspective, and those relationships, in mind. Others who were present for these same events may recall them differently, and I welcome that as the nature of any honest account written by someone who was in the room.


This book is a sequel of sorts to my 2022 book, CryptoDad: The Fight for the Future of Money, which recounted my experience serving on the U.S. Commodity Futures Trading Commission. Readers of that book will find familiar themes carried forward here, while those encountering this material for the first time need no prior familiarity with it to follow this narrative.


The events recounted span my CFTC Commission (2014-2019) through the digital asset regulatory landscape as it stands in the first half of 2026—a landscape that has continued to move even as this book went to press, and I have tried to be precise about when events occurred. Where I render conversations, hearing exchanges, or meetings as dialogue, that dialogue is drawn from contemporaneous notes, public transcripts, published accounts, and my own recollection, reconstructed and condensed to the best of my ability; it is not offered as verbatim except where drawn directly from the official record.


Readers will also encounter terms—“crypto,” “digital assets,” “virtual currency,” and others—that are sometimes used interchangeably in public discourse but often carry distinct meanings in law and regulation. I have endeavored to use these terms precisely, though at times I follow the language of the source or era under discussion.


Before we begin, one more quick note: This book is meant to inform, educate, and spark discussion, not to provide investment, financial, legal, or tax advice. If you’re making important financial decisions, you should consult the appropriate professional for guidance specific to your circumstances.

# Introduction: A Midnight Ride




“Listen, my children, and you shall hear of the midnight ride of Paul Revere.”




The opening lines of this poem1 were written by nineteenth century Harvard professor Henry Wadsworth Longfellow.


But what of the “midnight ride” to which he refers? What was that?


It happened in the spring of 1775, one year and two and a half centuries ago. Very early that April morning, more than 700 well-trained and carefully organized soldiers from more than a half dozen British army regiments rowed from Boston across the Charles River. Ashore, they mustered in the Massachusetts village of Cambridge a few hundred yards from then-modest Harvard College. The troops then set out under the cover of darkness to the surrounding countryside to search private homes and public buildings for stores of arms and ammunition.


The British soldiers marched on the orders of military governor General Thomas Gage, sovereign ruler of the Massachusetts Colony, representative of the British crown. That British army set out powerful and confident of success. Yet, within 24 hours, it retreated back to Cambridge, wounded, frightened, and defeated.


How could that be? How was that possible?


Well, setting out that same dark night were two solitary riders. One was Paul Revere, a well-connected and socially prominent craftsman. The other was leather tanner and militia member William Dawes.


The two men journeyed widely in different directions but with the same mission: to sound the alarm.


As explained by Professor Paul Hackett Fischer,2 rather than the romantic, solitary ride depicted in Longfellow’s poem, Paul Revere’s task was to activate a decentralized, regional network of loosely acquainted, but fiercely independent, militiamen, farm owners and farm hands, merchants and tradesmen, church and town leaders, and even college students, rugged individualists all.


A successful silversmith, Revere was also a skilled orator, courier, and intelligence agent who played a vital role in Massachusetts’ Committee of Correspondence. His famous ride on the night of April 18, 1775, followed months of surveillance and planning, designed to alert patriot militias of British troop movements in and out of Boston. Revere was not, in fact, a lone rider but a component in an extensive and coordinated patriot network.


By daybreak, that loose citizen network put into action thousands of opponents to the armed incursion. They stood up to powerful British regiments in the towns of Lexington and Concord and then decimated them militarily along the road back to Boston. In just one day, a centralized royal military force was defeated by a collective but decentralized network of free and independent citizens, setting in motion the American Revolution and political independence from Britain.3





## Hierarchies and Networks



In a masterful 2018 work, The Square and the Tower: Networks and Power, from the Freemasons to Facebook,4 historian Niall Ferguson looks at the historical interplay between centralized power and decentralized social networks and the threat that each has often faced from the other. He examines two core organizational structures of human life: hierarchies of official governmental, organizational, military, and corporate authority (symbolized by the tower) and unofficial social networks connected by mutual occupations and interests, family ties, and other bonds of loyalty (symbolized by the square).


Ferguson uses historical examples to show how networks have often challenged and sometimes toppled hierarchies and continuously shaped society and the human experience. He examines the fifteenth-century development of mobile type printing that allowed for individual interpretation of the Bible that challenged the European Catholic Church’s medieval hierarchy on knowledge and led, ultimately, to the Age of Enlightenment. He also looks at the twentieth-century rise of Silicon Valley and its challenge to traditional communication channels with the invention of modern social media and the increasing news-sharing dominance of X (formerly known as Twitter).5 Ferguson challenges orthodox views that top-down hierarchies like states, churches, and corporations alone shape human evolution by showing how often networks, such as New England’s yeoman militia and the Colonial Committee of Correspondence, have periodically disrupted hierarchical systems, enabling revolutions, social movements, and technological breakthroughs.


Yet, Ferguson also notes that networks are not inherently liberating or democratic. While insurgent networks can and often do democratize information and empower individuals, they also undermine traditional institutions and guardrails, posing new risks to global stability and human liberty. Thirteen years after America’s Declaration of Independence, France toppled its royal hierarchy, leading to years of social instability, bloody mayhem, and financial ruin. Another later revolution, the Russian one, led to a seven-decade experiment in brutal censorship, forced starvation, and human misery—the Soviet gulag’s so-called “warmth of collectivism.”


Hence, Ferguson warns that understanding the interplay between networks and hierarchies is crucial for navigating the future of government, society, and human existence. That understanding is also necessary to understand the evolution of modern finance.


It is now two and a half centuries after the momentous events that began in Massachusetts. Humanity again finds itself amidst the tension, perhaps even a clash, between centralized control and decentralized networks. The early twenty-first century internet is doing to finance, banking, and money what the late twentieth-century internet did to information gathering and dissemination. That is, increase speed, efficiency, and automation, lower costs, include more people and unlock new business models, challenge traditional market structures, displace intermediaries, and, in many cases, create new ones. Just as the first wave of the internet democratized information, this new wave of the internet, an Internet of Value, is revolutionizing the transfer of things of value—making money and property instantly accessible and globally movable, as easily as sending a text message. It is expanding money from fungible cash bills and electronic notations on the balance sheets of central and commercial banks to unique digital tokens on new digital infrastructure that are operated by decentralized, global collectives, national sovereign governments, and private sector entities. In time, the internet of value will likely change everything we know about how we record who owns what and who transfers what to whom.


As this book will soon explain, at the heart of this decentralization is Bitcoin, a tokenized representation of value on a distributed digital ledger (called the blockchain) maintained by a network of computers (called nodes) that is transparent and nearly impossible to tamper and through which people all over the world who do not know or work for each other can send, receive, and store without needing a bank or centralized authority.


The Bitcoin revolution is also raising the question whether this new Internet of Value will truly enhance economic liberty and financial freedom or, in time, rob free citizens of liberty in a similar way to how the current phase of the internet centralizes information and speech in the hands of a few BigTech giants.


So, what you will hear over the course of this book is a challenge and, indeed, a call to action, not unlike that of Paul Revere. That is, to balance the interplay between networks and hierarchies in the burgeoning Internet of Value to make the future of digital finance one that is worthy of free society and true to New World values of human liberty and financial freedom.


But first, let’s consider at how we got here.







## Makings of CryptoDad



My earlier work, CryptoDad: The Fight for the Future of Money, recounted my Wall Street experience in the early 2000s, helping steer an upstart hybrid electronic trading platform through a series of private capital financings and then take it public, eventually onto the New York Stock Exchange. I shared my experience in the 2008 financial crisis and how my friend and law school classmate, Paul Atkins (today, chairman of the US Securities and Exchange Commission) brought me to the attention of Congressional leadership and, ultimately, the Obama administration, resulting in my appointment to the Commodity Futures Trading Commission. There, I gave a green light to the developed world’s first fully regulated trading of crypto in the form of Bitcoin derivatives, which provided clarity and spurred institutional entry into crypto. It also led to the realization that, as I will further explain in this book, crypto is beyond a new class of tradeable assets, but a whole new architecture—an Internet of Value—of banking, finance, and money itself with both promise and peril for human liberty.


That earlier book left off just as the new Biden administration was taking shape in 2021 and formulating policies that were initially promising yet ultimately destructive of crypto innovation. This book will pick up with an explanation of the crypto phenomenon and some of the key crypto digital networks, the emergence of stablecoins, and two “killer apps” for crypto, and how they will revolutionize the way we secure the past and anticipate the future. This book will also recount the building of one of the first digital asset law practices at a traditional “Big Law” firm, including the challenges of counseling participants in the quarrelsome crypto industry, advising the now notorious Sam Bankman-Fried, my controversial 2020 law review argument that the crypto XRP was not a security subject to SEC jurisdiction, and the SEC’s lawsuit challenging that position and its subsequent vindication in federal court. This book will also review the work of the Digital Dollar Project in addressing three essential questions: how to “future-proof” the dollar for a world of competing digital networks; how to maintain the dollar’s world reserve currency status, and, perhaps most importantly, how to preserve the universal values of economic freedom for which the dollar has historically stood.


Turning to the political scene, I will describe the Biden administration’s policy of antipathy to digital network innovation, the possible ideological reasons behind it, and its detrimental impact on US innovation. I will share my insights into backroom efforts in 2024 by candidates Biden/Harris and Trump to attract the growing cadre of crypto voters and why one attempt was way too late, and the other came just in time. Along the way, I will recount an Inauguration Day detour to a duty-free zone near Lake Lugano in Italy to contemplate the nature of youth, art, and gullibility.


I will provide an eyewitness view of the White House’s inaugural 2025 Crypto Summit and recount an appearance before a Congressional body urging policymakers to follow my leadership as CFTC chairman and put in place sound crypto regulatory structures that serve democratic values, especially individual liberty. I will further review the second Trump administration’s current crypto initiatives and the prospects for Congressional crypto clarity. I will conclude by showing how fraud and frenzy have frequently accompanied technological transformation and that the Internet of Value revolution will be no different.


Through it all, I wish to present an insider’s account of the struggle of crypto—new digital architecture of finance—to emerge from the crossfire of different political visions of how to allocate capital to participants in the digitally networked economy of the twenty-first century. I will explain how the outcome of this debate will either empower or inhibit humanity in the digital future of finance and money itself.







## Victory and Defeat



As this book is published, the United States is celebrating its sestercentennial anniversary: 200 and 50 years of independence from the British crown. Two and a half centuries ago, British political and military power in North America stood as a powerful, but narrow, centralized hierarchy. Its would-be challengers were a decentralized network of North American yeomanry, merchants, tradesmen, and landowners.


The American War of Independence began in the eastern shires of Massachusetts when a decentralized colonial network challenged and threatened the British governing hierarchy. Yet the war ended only when that centralized British hierarchy was soundly defeated seven years later by not one, but two, highly centralized hierarchies: Washington’s trained Continental Army and French King Louis XVI’s well-ordered French army and navy.


There may have been something inevitable, even “absurd” (in the words of Thomas Paine), “in supposing a continent to be perpetually governed by an island.”6 Perhaps it was also inescapable that the initial catalyst for the downfall of the British status quo was a decentralized colonial network. But the actual demise of British power in North America was not the work of decentralized warriors, but of opposing highly organized and hierarchical armies. What began as decentralization ended in hierarchy.


Yet, it was a hierarchy steeped in the values of decentralization: local government, separation of powers, individual rights and liberties, freedom of speech, assembly, and private property: key individual and states’ rights that checked the power of the hierarchy. In short, the American Revolution was an initial attack on centralized power by a decentralized network, followed by a synthesis of the two. This synthesis has provided a durable political foundation for 250 years.


As we look forward, we see a dramatic package of technological and social innovation coming over history’s horizon. It most powerfully enables the decentralization of finance and the recording and holding of everything of value. It challenges established hierarchies of powerful political and commercial interests that control the custody and allocation of capital, value, and money.


In fact, the battle between these new decentralized networks of value and mature hierarchies of politics and finance is already being waged, drawing blood and claiming its victors and its vanquished. It looked for a time that the hierarchical forces of traditional finance in Washington and Wall Street had the support of the Biden White House and thus the upper hand in this battle. The past four years were ones of restrictive thinking about American financial markets: risk aversion rather than risk taking, particularly so at the SEC, where the intended policy was suppression of financial innovation, especially regarding digital assets—to the detriment of US economic growth and American consumers and investors.


In the past year and a half, however, the tide has changed dramatically. Stirred by the second Trump administration, many old financial hierarchies are yielding and quickly adopting the new, more decentralized innovations. The technological breakthrough that spurred the decentralizers is now being championed by Washington and accommodated and adopted by traditional hierarchical institutions. The Trump policy direction is clear: support for technological innovation to make the US the crypto capital of the world.


The question, however, is whether the traditional hierarchies of value custody and allocation will be displaced by decentralized networks. Or will they be supplanted by entirely new centralized hierarchies that have mastered the new technology, but recentralized it among themselves? Or perhaps there will be a third outcome? One that based on the historical record is more than likely: a future like the past, an uneasy but ever-present tug-of-war between hierarchies and networks, taking place this time in a future where value is stored and exchanged on global, digital networks.


That is what we will examine together in this book.

# Chapter 1 What Is Crypto?




Taken from the author’s lecture series, an explanation in simple terms of what crypto is and why it will soon change everything we know about finance, banking, and money itself




Since completing my public service as CFTC chairman seven years ago and publishing my book, CryptoDad, I have delivered numerous talks on crypto and digital assets to large and small audiences in corporate boardrooms, government agencies, industry conferences, and college classrooms. The scope of my lectures (and what I will offer here) is that crypto—a shorthand I use for all manner of innovations in digital assets, tokenization, and distributed ledger technology—is the next inevitable wave of the internet, an Internet of Value. It is no more optional or delayable than the earlier wave of the internet—the Internet of Information that changed everything we knew about data gathering, social networking, online commerce, and so much more. This next wave is coming and coming fast, and this time it has its sights on how things of value are recorded, held, and transferred, and, with it, the way we organize and compensate ourselves for helping to do so.


What I do not try to explain in my lectures (and will not try to explain here) is every technical aspect of crypto, digital assets, tokenization, and blockchain technology. For one thing, those topics have been thoroughly covered by other, more technically proficient authors.1 Moreover, such a comprehensive explanation is not the primary purpose of this book, which is to recognize the challenge of crypto and distributed ledger innovation to the established hierarchy of traditional finance. That challenge calls for sensible public policy and well-crafted law and regulation consistent with personal liberty. I intend to present and, in some cases, give eyewitness accounts of crypto’s struggle to emerge and thrive, despite the global crossfire of differing political visions of how best to allocate capital to participants in the United States and world economy.


I will leave textbook writing to others. I’m a storyteller with stories to tell.





## Digital Network Architecture



Nevertheless, to describe that challenge, I must first provide a basic understanding of crypto and digital asset innovation. At each of my public lectures, I start off by asking for a show of hands from the audience to the broad question, “What is crypto?” I do this to get a sense of the audience and to lay a foundation for the rest of my remarks. I begin by asking: “Is crypto a new class of investment assets?” and generally, a few hands go up. Next, I ask, “Is crypto a faster and more efficient means of payment?” and I usually get an equal measure of raised hands and bemused shrugs. After that, I ask if crypto is “the first form of digital commodity?” and I see a few more hands in the air, along with more than a fair share of puzzled looks. I then ask, “How about a form of gambling for young and naive investors?” and the greatest number of hands shoot up into the air. Now, I can wrap up with one final option: “Perhaps crypto is all of the above?” At this point, I see nearly all hands raised.


I tell the audience that yes, crypto is “all of the above.” It is a new investment asset class, a growing means of instantaneous borderless payments, some of the world’s first digital commodities and securities, and, no doubt, a form of gambling for young and naive investors. I then say if that were all crypto was, then it would likely not be worth much of our attention, certainly not mine. Rather, crypto deserves serious consideration because it is much more than just a speculative asset. It is an entirely new architecture—an internet-based architecture—of banking, finance, and money.


As I have been talking about crypto over the past decade, many people I respect, especially professional people at the highest levels of financial services and regulation, told me that they see crypto as, at worst, a passing fad and, at best, a solution searching for a problem to solve.2 To many, the existing financial system just requires ongoing maintenance and periodic upgrades. It does not need wholesale and risky reengineering. Their satisfaction with the status quo ignores the fact that the existing system is one of Byzantine complexity, masking concentrated fragilities and sharp conflicts of interest. It induces enormous transactional friction, benefitting preserved monopolies and rent-collecting intermediaries. Worse still, the incumbent financial system excludes large segments of the global population, limiting access to those with credentialed identities—something more than 10% of the world’s eight billion people lack.3


To the argument that crypto is a solution in search of a problem, my response is that crypto and the distributed ledger technology behind it directly address problems the financial system has repeatedly failed to solve—namely, high costs, slow and inefficient execution, and social exclusion. Crypto does this by powering round-the-clock and round-the-world internet connectivity. It replaces rent-collecting middlemen with trustless consensus mechanisms. It allows participants to agree on the validity of transactions without needing to trust each other, a central authority, or an organized hierarchy.4 In essence, crypto replaces the tower with the square, rigid hierarchy with flexible decentralization. In this light, crypto, tokenization, and distributed ledger technologies are the next wave of the internet doing to banking, finance, and money what an earlier wave of the internet did to information gathering, social networking, retail shopping, and so much more. The same internet that changed Madison Avenue, Fleet Street, and the public square is now changing not only Wall Street, but the entire branch banking system on Main Street.


Even critics of crypto cannot dismiss the extraordinary investment performance of the innovation. For 8 of the 12 years from 2014 to 2025, Bitcoin has provided the highest calendar-year price return among major asset classes (stocks S&P 500, gold, oil, US 10-yr Treasury, and Bitcoin).5 Another leading crypto, Ethereum, has seen a 140,000%+ return in the decade since its launch in 2015.6 This performance might seem unsustainable, but it is rooted in a fundamental belief in crypto’s potential.


In June 2020, Tesla became the world’s most valuable automaker7 and remains so today.8 This milestone came despite the fact that Toyota and other automakers produce far more cars and have substantially greater manufacturing and distribution capacity.9 Rather, Tesla’s current $1.4 trillion market cap reflects future expectations. If investors believe that the future of transportation is electric (as more and more people do), then purchasing Tesla stock is a way to express that conviction. Similarly, if one believes (as an increasing number of people, especially young people, do) that the future of finance, banking, and payments will be digitally networked, then the way to express that conviction is to own crypto.


Still, to fully understand crypto, one needs to move beyond seeing it as something to buy in the hopes of getting rich. I tell audiences that, to me, whether the price of Bitcoin or other cryptos goes up or down is somewhat irrelevant. It is irrelevant to me, in part, because I do not directly own Bitcoin and other major cryptocurrencies. Yes, I actively invest in crypto infrastructure companies and serve as an advisor and independent director in numerous businesses that build crypto applications and that sometimes hold cryptocurrencies in their corporate treasury. But I do not personally speculate in crypto tokens. Much to my financial detriment, this position is one I first adopted in 2015 when I served in Washington, DC making US regulatory policy for crypto. I believed it would be wrong for me to benefit financially from rules and regulations put in place by the regulatory agency I oversaw. It is a position that I have maintained since I continue to serve as an outspoken advocate for crypto innovation. Financially speaking, I would be better off if I had speculated in crypto a decade ago. But choices have consequences, and I am satisfied with mine. Hopefully, it engenders your confidence in my impartiality as you read this book.


However, the more important reason why I believe that the rise or fall of cryptocurrency prices is irrelevant is that, whether high or low, the price does nothing to undermine or further the enormous promise and power of the architecture itself. Think about artificial intelligence (AI), for example, which is arguably the most powerful and disruptive new technology of the twenty-first century, with the potential to transform almost all aspects of our lives. Ask yourselves if the social impact of AI will be any greater or lesser if the average daily trading price of the leading AI company, Nvidia, rises or falls on any given day. It doesn’t. The same is generally true about Bitcoin and other crypto.


So, what do I mean when I say that the value of crypto lies in its service as architecture? Well, to do that, I need to first tell you briefly how I got here and what qualifies me to try to explain crypto.







## Down the Rabbit Hole



I am not a technologist or “digital native” who started on the engineering or coding side of crypto technology. In fact, I am like many proponents of crypto: I came to appreciate the power and the possibility of the technology based upon my personal experience. For some, that means expanding financial services to the underserved across the world, and for others, it means creating a new financial order that is faster, cheaper, and more efficient than legacy infrastructure. For me, the “ah-ha!” moment came when I realized that crypto held the power to make the financial world safer and more resilient.


I go into detail about my career path in CryptoDad, so I will only summarize it here. In brief, I am a trained lawyer, having graduated from Vanderbilt Law School in Nashville, Tennessee, in the somewhat notable class of 1984.10 I practiced finance and business law in New York and London until 2000, when I went into business on Wall Street11 with a group of entrepreneurs who were building some of the first global electronic trading systems for trading complex financial instruments between large investment and money-center banks. Our platform, GFI Group,12 was a global exchange for sophisticated products that didn’t trade on traditional exchanges. It was especially adept in handling trading in credit default swaps or “CDS,” a type of tradable instrument somewhat similar to insurance that provides protection in the event a commercial institution could not pay back money it borrowed.


GFI Group was very successful. The hybrid electronic platforms that it built quickly expanded around the world. We raised private equity capital, and in January 2005, the company went public on the Nasdaq stock market and, later, listed its shares on the New York Stock Exchange. By 2008 and the stirring of the great financial crisis, GFI Group handled roughly two-thirds of global trading in credit default swaps.


GFI Group was in the eye of the storm during the 2008 financial crisis. As the leading exchange platform for the world’s largest banks to trade the risk of default by other banks in the form of CDS contracts, we saw the growing fear on Wall Street that collapsing US home values and mortgage failures would impact bank balance sheets. The panic of investors and finance leaders was compounded by the opacity and disconnectedness of the records of the banks’ CDS exposures. It was a panic that could only be quelled by highly controversial federal government loans and bailouts.


As the financial contagion spread in late summer 2008, CDS became a big area of concern. At one point, I remember a call from the US Federal Reserve Bank of New York that went something like this:


“Hello. This is <name redacted> from the New York Fed. I’m interested in learning more about what you guys do and what you are seeing in the market.”


“Thanks for the call. You might recall that our platform handles about two-thirds of the credit default swap market.”


“So, tell me, what’s happening in the market now?”


“Well, to be honest, it’s pandemonium. You understand that individual credit default swap contracts trade at a premium to Treasury bonds: the higher the premium, the greater the risk. Right now, Lehman Brothers is trading 800 basis points over Treasuries. In other words, they are in seriously troubled territory.”


“Wow, that’s interesting. Is it really that bad?”


“It’s worse than bad. Right now, few banks are willing to trade with Lehman, fearing it can’t deliver. At this point, Lehman is basically a dead man walking.”


Ten or so days later, Lehman Brothers collapsed, and the US government scrambled to put together the $700 billion Troubled Asset Relief Program (TARP).13 The purpose of the program was to calm the markets by providing liquidity to systemically important banks in the same way that a fire brigade hoses down houses surrounding the one on fire to keep them from also going up in flames. Critically, the worry was not that CDS instruments would fail, but that they would work as intended and cause banks to pay out lots of money pursuant to their CDS contracts, forcing them, in turn, into bankruptcy.


The problem was that no one, including my GFI colleagues and me, fully understood the exact scope of the problem at the time because of a lack of transparency. Upon Lehman Brothers’ collapse, the US government calculated that $400 billion in gross national outstanding CDS contracts were written against the failure of Lehman to pay its debts.14 Lehman failing, the government reckoned, would cause the surviving banks to make good on those contracts, potentially destabilizing the entire global banking industry.


What was unclear then to everyone was the actual or “net” payout of Wall Street to cover the failure of Lehman Brothers. I suspected that the “net” position of CDS exposure had to be much, much less than the $400 billion that was generally referred to as the “gross” position. I thought so because CDS trades handled by GFI were usually hedged by the parties involved to reduce the net exposure of the firms writing CDS contracts. Through such hedging activity, the Wall Street banks and other large financial firms offset their potential losses by entering into contracts by which they would receive payment if they had to pay out on a failure of Lehman Brothers. The problem was that we and the marketplace didn’t know how well they had hedged their Lehman exposure.


Lehman’s debt was trading at 8.6 cents on the dollar,15 indicating that the marketplace had little confidence that Lehman could remain solvent. Many were frightened by the prospect that sellers of that CDS protection would soon have to pay buyers of protection a total of $400 billion, less the 8.6 cents for the heavily discounted debt, or about $365 billion in total.16 The worry was that other banks could not afford to meet their CDS obligations, which, in turn, would cause a cascade of bank failures. Hence, the launch of the $700 billion TARP program.


Years later, in 2015, Professor Bruce Tuckman, clinical professor of finance at New York University’s Stern School of Business, carefully analyzed the Lehman failure.17 He calculated that because of those offsetting trade “hedges,” a default by Lehman would not have resulted in payouts of $400 billion or even $365 billion. Rather, the aggregate payouts would have been less than $8 billion!18


Had US government officials clearly understood in 2008 that a failure of Lehman Brothers would not have resulted in $400 billion in losses or even $364 billion, but a loss of less than $8 billion, they would have had a far wider range of policy options than they had: they could have let Lehman fail, let it be sold, or bailed it out. None of those options, by themselves, would have triggered a Wall Street collapse. Moreover, if the broader financial markets knew that a failure of Lehman could have only triggered $8 billion at most in net payments by the other banks, it is quite unlikely that there would have been the kind of market panic that precipitated the 2008 market crash.19 Tuckman’s research revealed that American taxpayers probably didn’t need to fund a $700 billion TARP program at all, a fact that was proven when the program was later estimated to have only cost taxpayers somewhere between $27 billion and $41 billion.20


This experience stuck with me and led to an “a-ha!” moment in the coming years as I started to get an in-depth understanding of Bitcoin and the underlying blockchain technology that drives cryptocurrencies. At heart, the 2008 financial crisis was caused by a lack of clarity into the true exposure of Wall Street banks to one another. Yet, if the architecture of the financial market, the architecture of Wall Street, had been written on a single digital ledger or a network of ledgers to which a broad range of appropriate transacting and regulatory parties could have full visibility and the ability to confirm transactions, we might have had sufficient clarity to end the panic. The root of the problem in 2008 was largely one of financial system fragmentation and opacity.


A few years later, after reading the 2008 manifesto21 by a pseudonymous writer named Satoshi Nakamoto, I wondered if distributed ledger technology could hold the answer to the last crisis and to the future of finance itself. The paper proposed the creation of a decentralized network in which unrelated computers around the world could reliably reach agreement with each other on questions of ownership and transfer (e.g., who owns what and who transfers what to whom). As described, the Bitcoin mining algorithm would ensure that a network of computers that don’t know each other will, in fact, reach agreement with each other in a reliable manner. And that this agreement or consensus—what today we call a blockchain—would be immutable and verifiable.


It struck me that if all those CDS contracts and the offsetting trades that my firm had brokered and the remaining trades not handled by us had been recorded on a distributed ledger or blockchain or a network of digital ledgers back in 2008, then the world would have known that the failure of Lehman would not have brought down Wall Street.


I wondered if it might be possible for blockchain technology to usher in a new era of transparency, inclusion, efficiency, and scalability for financial assets of all kinds. That question stuck with me as I later undertook public service, first as a commissioner and then as the chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC). There, I dug into blockchain technology to solve the previous financial crisis, but I wound up furthering crypto regulatory structures to help mitigate the next one.







## Becoming CryptoDad



Settling in as a CFTC commissioner in 2014, I advocated for a “do no harm” policy toward blockchain and distributed ledger technology generally.22 I became chairman of the agency in January 2017 and immediately began a deep dive into digital assets. In May 2017, at a gathering of New York’s renowned FinTech Innovation Lab23 with the support of my thoughtful Democratic colleague, Sharon Bowen (now chair of the New York Stock Exchange), we launched LabCFTC, a dedicated government office to understand and apply financial innovation and the first federal office of its kind. We recruited Adjunct Georgetown Law Professor Daniel Gorfine to run it and serve as the CFTC’s chief innovation officer, another first. We also created a CFTC market intelligence unit and published primers on crypto innovation and trading. We even got to the point of simulating Bitcoin “mining” in the CFTC Chairman’s boardroom in the late summer of 2017 just so that we could understand what Bitcoin was and how it was generated.


That painstaking analysis paid off in December 2017 when my administration greenlighted trading of the first crypto derivative product: Bitcoin futures. Not long after, I was summoned to appear before the Senate Banking Committee. As I detailed in my first book, CryptoDad, I was called to publicly justify giving that greenlight despite enormous opposition from Wall Street banks, several overseas regulators, and the futures industry itself. The opposition expressed itself in derisive articles in the Washington beltway press and full-page advertisements in The Wall Street Journal. I knew I was in for a grilling by a gang of grillmasters that had flayed many a proud regulator, bank CEO, and hedge fund grandee.


The day before the Senate hearing, I submitted my carefully prepared, written testimony providing chapter and verse of the CFTC’s legal authority to oversee trading of futures products on Bitcoin and other crypto. That morning, I took my seat at the low hearing table under bright lights before the Senators’ raised dais. When it was my time to speak, I made a show of pushing away my papers and looking up. Clearing my throat, I acknowledged that I was appearing as the head of an important market regulator. Yet, with permission, I wanted to speak simply as a “dad.” I explained that I was a father of three college-age children and had tried for years to generate their interest in the stock market. Yet, something had changed, and they were enormously interested in Bitcoin, probably in a similar way to the Senators’ own children and grandchildren. As I saw a few heads nod, I dove in:



It strikes me that we owe it to this new generation to respect their enthusiasm about virtual currencies with a thoughtful and balanced response, not a dismissive one.24





Becoming CryptoDad Before the US Senate Banking Committee



I went on to use the hearing to make an impassioned plea for Congress to support my agency’s initiatives and respond to a new generation’s interest in cryptocurrencies, not with derision and disdain, but with sensible and apt regulatory frameworks. I entered the hearing chamber with about 600 Twitter followers. By the end of the week, I had 50,000 supporters giving me all sorts of nicknames, including “CryptoDad,” a moniker I am proud to have earned and embraced, not grasped or proclaimed.


Since that hearing, the US-regulated Bitcoin futures market has experienced dramatic growth and stability.25 Now centered on the Chicago Mercantile Exchange (CME), trading in Bitcoin futures assures US dollar pricing of the world’s first digital commodity. It is a fact that many of the world’s key natural commodities (e.g., oil, gold, wheat, corn, soybeans) are priced in dollars. Dollar pricing of key world commodities is one of the underpinnings of the US dollar and its reserve currency status. Dollar pricing is not established in everyday physical or spot commodity markets. Rather, it is determined in large, organized futures markets, where both institutional and retail buyers and sellers negotiate wholesale prices upon which physical prices are based. The price of gold is set in gold futures markets, not at the jewelry store. The price of oil is set in oil futures markets, not at the gas pump. Many of those wholesale futures markets are in Chicago and New York, priced in US dollars and regulated by the CFTC. By establishing a regulated US futures market for Bitcoin, we made sure that the global price for Bitcoin was similarly set in US dollars, not euros, Swiss francs, or Chinese yuan. Eight years on, I am proud that the US marketplace for Bitcoin futures is liquid, transparent, and well-regulated by the CFTC. It is proof that US regulators can engage successfully with crypto innovation…if they have a mind to do so.







## Software vs Hardware



Alright, now that you know how I got here, let’s turn back to my contention that crypto is less about “number go up?” and, more importantly, about financial architecture. Let’s talk about the history of digital network technology.


Undoubtedly, the twentieth century was an era of great technological marvels, from airplanes to moon landings and from telephones to nuclear energy. Perhaps one of the greatest technological breakthroughs of the century was the 1947 invention of the transistor at Bell Labs in New Jersey.26 It allowed the replacement of giant mainframe computers with their hundreds of vacuum tubes and miles of hard wiring located on college campuses and army installations to be reduced to the size of desktop personal computers,27 decentralizing computational power from hierarchical control to individual personal use.


But as important as was the breakthrough in transistor and other semiconductor hardware, the real breakthrough was in the development of key algorithms and protocols like TCP-IP, HTTP, and HTML. These software protocols enabled personal computers to “talk” to each other, forming a communication network of worldwide users, and giving birth to the internet as we know it—an Internet of Information. The protocols enabled all manner of information sharing and, in turn, enormous social changes in information gathering (Wikipedia), social networking (Facebook), entertainment (Netflix), retail shopping (Amazon), ride sharing (Uber), video sharing (TikTok), business meetings (Zoom), and so forth.


Over the decades, computer hardware became ever smaller and more powerful in accordance with Moore’s law (the number of transistors on a microchip doubles every two years).28 Computing power increased, and costs decreased. Desktop personal computers gave way to smartphones and wearable computing devices as the development and proliferation of modern wireless communications through cellular and other mobile technologies made personal communications a ubiquitous, around-the-clock phenomenon.


Yet, perhaps more important than the improved computer hardware were the new software protocols like Bluetooth and Wi-Fi that allowed ever smaller portable devices to also interact with each other. Development of these protocols led to a new wave of the internet called the “Internet of Things” (IoT). It saw the digitization of more and more devices from car dashboards to health-tracking jewelry to home doorbells, stereos, and heating systems that can be networked and remotely monitored and controlled.


In consumer finance, IoT transformed the front-end experience, making it possible to move money and stocks with the click of a computer mouse or a screen swipe on a mobile device. Venmo and Zelle are good examples. Yet, the back-end infrastructure remained unchanged, running on message transmission technology built upon antiquated account-based architecture.


So, where are we headed? Well, predictably, computing capability is ever greater and more compact. But it is breakthroughs in software that are again spurring on this next wave of the internet, an “Internet of Value.” In this new era, computers and smart devices are going beyond “talking” to each other; they are now “transacting” with each other over native digital networks. Advancements in distributed ledger technology, combined with AI, are performing services on those networks that up to now have been conducted with substantial human assistance on antiquated backend infrastructure. And it is once again the software protocols or “consensus mechanisms” that stand out. This time around, these consensus mechanisms have names like Bitcoin, Ethereum, and Tether.


Let’s take a look at them.
