# Chapter 2 Digital Networks of Value




A review of some prominent cryptocurrencies and the economies they create




In this new wave of the internet—the Internet of Value—things of value are represented, stored, and transferred as digital tokens of two kinds: identical, fungible tokens (think of digital nickels, dimes, dollars, or euros) and unique “non-fungible” digital tokens or “NFTs,” such as art or collectibles. Rather than existing in analog form—paper stock certificates, plastic driver’s licenses, metal coins or paper currency—each token encodes a unique series of 0s and 1s. These digital tokens can represent virtually anything of value: investment instruments like bonds and equities, physical assets like real estate properties, and intangible valuables like identity and personal data, and even rare works of art and historical artifacts like paintings and precious violins.1


The internet, as we’ve known it, operates a “message and reconciliation”-based model: data are managed and shared by transmitting instructions to update records, then reconciling with counterparties to verify accuracy. The Internet of Value works differently. Distributed ledgers and blockchains enable shared networks that can be updated and viewed simultaneously by all relevant participants with a single shared network serving as the authoritative source of truth, rather than each participant maintaining its own separate records. The analogy is familiar: just as a driver’s license is a physical token that proves your identity, a digital token proves your ownership of that data set—instantly, without intermediaries—on a shared distributed network for everyone to see. The implications for how things of value are represented, stored, and transferred across various industries are profound.


There are tens of thousands of cryptocurrencies, with the aggregate value topping $3 trillion as of January 2026.2 The innovation started with Bitcoin and then other cryptocurrencies, but it is now spreading to all types of money and financial assets. It’s often hard to comprehend the staggering success of cryptocurrencies because they do not link to a traditional asset. Cryptocurrencies leverage a reward system where the participants of the network provide their compute power to process network transactions to earn tokens that can be used to transact within the network. The most prominent cryptocurrencies have familiar names like Bitcoin (BTC), Ethereum (ETH), Solana (SOL), BNB Coin (BNB), and XRP.


As the industry has evolved, these networks are now framed as “Layer 1s” where additional networks or “Layer 2s” are built on top of them to help handle demand on the network. These so-called L2s are not able to fully operate apart from the underlying L1s, but they have become sizable. Some of the largest L2 projects are Polygon, Base, Arbitrum, Optimism, and Mantle. These networks serve as the foundation to further network innovations such as decentralized applications or “dApps,” and decentralized finance networks (DeFi) that provide additional features.


Let’s now look at various prominent cryptocurrencies.





## Bitcoin



Bitcoin is the founding father of all cryptocurrencies. It was conceived and announced on Halloween night 2008 by its pseudonymous creator, Satoshi Nakamoto. To this day, the identity of this individual (or group of people) has never been revealed or discovered, adding to the mystery and utilitarian ethos of Bitcoin and blockchain technology.3


Bitcoin was not the first attempt at creating a form of digital currency,4 but it was the first one to work. The concept of Bitcoin was set out in a paper published online, entitled: Bitcoin: A Peer-to-Peer Electronic Cash System.5 Whether coincidental or not, the timing of the paper’s release—just a few weeks after the 2008 collapse of Lehman Brothers—was certainly propitious for Bitcoin’s adoption. Confidence in the financial system and the institutions that administered and regulated it had sharply diminished. A currency that offers to circumvent and obviate all those authorities is alluring.


Software for the Bitcoin system—in an open-source format—was released in January 2009. Bitcoin transaction activity grew slowly over the next few years. For a while, it flew under the radar of much of the public and, indeed, of financial regulators (except for law enforcement). Until 2017. Then it suddenly became front-page news and has remained so.


As I explain in my book, CryptoDad, Bitcoin is transacted on the blockchain—a form of digital ledger technology.6 Blockchain is, as the name suggests, a chain of conceptual blocks. Each block is a digital vault that contains information. That information could be, for instance, an amount of money and who it belongs to or a receipt for a shipment of fresh salmon arriving at the Fulton Fish Market. The blockchain provides for the confirmation of the origin and ownership of things of value, such as ship cargoes, agricultural and mineral commodities, contracts, stock certificates, property titles, art and music, votes in an election, and even personal identities.


When entering a new transaction, the user waits for a sophisticated automated algorithm to verify the entry. Then a new block is created and coded with a hashtag—a digital fingerprint—that is associated with the unique information contained within. Importantly, every block in the chain is marked not only with its own hashtag, but with that of the block immediately preceding it. Once confirmed, the transactions are immutable, meaning they cannot be altered or reversed by anyone.


Bitcoin is said to be “censorship-resistant” due to several core features of its technical architecture.7 First, it operates through a global, decentralized network of computers that is not controlled by any single government or organization. Blockchain transactions are confirmed not by a central intermediary—like a bank—but by the consensus of participants in the system. It thereby allows people who have no particular confidence in one another to collaborate without having to rely on a neutral central authority. Those people conduct transactions free of intervention from third parties that might seek to limit or censor their transactions.


Given this structure, you might wonder what incentivizes participants to do all those crucial confirmations? Certainly, they need to be paid to do so. But if they are paid in US dollars or other sovereign money, then a bank or other central authority would need to be brought in to do so. But that would defeat the whole point of the distributed ledger—the blockchain—to avoid dependence on controlling and, potentially, vulnerable banks and other intermediaries, rather than the global consensus of all network participants to run the system.


The solution that Satoshi Nakamoto hit upon was to create the ecosystem’s own payment mechanism that rewards participants (called “miners”) with the opportunity to receive something of value: Bitcoin tokens. New Bitcoin tokens are created through a process called mining, where networks of specialized computers compete in a series of computational races to solve a difficult math problem for the right to add a new block to the blockchain. This process of validating transactions and creating new blocks is called “proof-of-work” (PoW). Successful miners receive newly minted Bitcoin at each new block, which gets propagated every 10 minutes on average. Today’s “mining reward” is 3.12 Bitcoins per block, which works out to be $182,532.48, at $58,504 per Bitcoin as this manuscript is going to print.


Unlike fiat currencies, which have no hard limit and whose supply can expand and contract based on policy preferences from governments and central banks, the design of the Bitcoin blockchain limits the total amount of all Bitcoin tokens to 21 million units. As of this writing, approximately 20 million Bitcoins have already been created.8 But the asset’s issuance schedule, which reduces its inflation rate by half every four years, will ensure that the final unit will not be created until the year 2140.9


The Bitcoin blockchain is a public ledger, making it accessible to anyone. Each transaction recorded on the ledger includes transaction details such as the amount transferred and the addresses involved. This level of public transparency allows anyone to verify transactions, enhancing trust in the system.


Bitcoin transactions are not anonymous, but pseudonymous. Participants are represented by alphanumeric addresses rather than their real names. These addresses are public, and all transactions tied to them can be seen on the blockchain, while the actual identities of the users behind the addresses remain concealed. This means that, although personal information is not displayed, the transaction history is publicly available and can be analyzed. The transparency of Bitcoin allows for traceability. Blockchain analysis tools can track the flow of Bitcoin from one address to another. This capability can be used to monitor transactions and identify patterns, which is why Bitcoin is often considered more transparent than traditional financial systems.10


One way to look at Bitcoin is through the lens of more traditional forms of money. Economists will say that money serves three main functions: a medium of payment, a unit of account, and a store of value.11 A “medium of payment” simply means that you can pay for things using that particular instrument. The standard medium of payment for groceries at a US supermarket is US dollars, not postage stamps or American Airlines reward miles. A “unit of account” means that things are calculated and priced in that instrument. For example, the produce in American supermarkets is priced in US dollars, not in postage stamps, airline miles, or even in euros or Japanese yen. The annual earnings and financial accounts of French supermarket chains are stated in euros; the earnings of Japanese conglomerates are recorded in yen. Hence, dollars, euros, and yen are “units of account.” All three currencies are also “stores of value.” Most of us have a bank savings or checking account recording holdings of dollars, euros, yen, or other currencies. The purpose of those accounts is to hold the currency assets we consider “stores of value.”


Analyzed by the three functions of money, Bitcoin is not yet widely used as a “unit of account.” Supermarket prices and corporate earnings are not denominated in Bitcoin. Even Bitcoin itself is mostly accounted for in dollars.


As a “means of payment,” Bitcoin works very well as the exclusive means to reward “miners” for confirming blocks and adding them to the Bitcoin blockchain. Other than for work on the blockchain, however, Bitcoin is a much less common form of payment in the everyday economy. Some online retailers, like Overstock.com, accept Bitcoin for payment, as do some service providers, like Expedia.com. However, Bitcoin’s well-publicized price volatility against traditional sovereign currencies reduces its utility as an everyday “means of payment.”


Bitcoin’s price volatility is not a design flaw, however, but a design feature. That is because whenever you have a fixed-supply commodity (limited to 21 million units) like Bitcoin, then there can only be one response to rising or falling demand: price movement. If supply cannot increase to meet rising demand, then the response will be a price increase and a price drop when demand falls.


This leads to considering Bitcoin as a “store of value.” Many investors consider Bitcoin an excellent store of value because of its computationally limited supply compared to the unrestrained government money printing of many sovereign fiat currencies. This scarcity value of Bitcoin is built into the algorithm of the Bitcoin blockchain, making it virtually impossible to alter and thus debase Bitcoin. Since its inception, through every real-world challenge—technical, political, or market—Bitcoin has shown that it works. Its value has remained.


If you compare residential real estate and the US dollar to Bitcoin, you will see that in 2016 the price of the average American home was approximately $235,00012 and the average price of a Bitcoin was $434. If the owner of an average house had sold it and bought Bitcoin, they would have received more than 541 Bitcoins. In 2025, the median price of an average American home rose to $415,000, but the average price of Bitcoin had risen to approximately $92,000. In fact, that 2016 home seller who bought Bitcoin would be holding value in 2025 of approximately $50 million (541BTC × $92,000 = $49,772,000) and could then afford to purchase almost 120 average American homes ($49,772,000 divided by $415,000 = 119). Clearly, despite its current price drop, Bitcoin has been a far superior store of value over the past decade compared to both the US dollar and US residential property. In fact, it is arguable that Bitcoin has settled into a hybrid between a growth stock and a form of digital gold.








## Ethereum



If Bitcoin has settled into its role as digital gold, then Ethereum can be thought of as a global, decentralized supercomputer. It is a decentralized blockchain platform that enables the creation and operation of smart contracts and dApps. Its native currency, ether or “ETH,” powers these applications and transactions on the network. Boomers like me might imagine Ethereum as a distributed ledger engineering platform similar to the “erector sets” that we used as kids to build physical working devices. A younger generation might think of it as the “Raspberry Pi” of distributed ledgers with innumerable, affordable use cases.


Ethereum was launched in the summer of 2015 by Vitalik Buterin and a team of cofounders, including Gavin Wood and Joseph Lubin.13 It has been the world’s second-largest blockchain by market capitalization since its inception. As of this writing, it is worth an aggregate of $188 billion. In terms of competition with other smart contracts, Ethereum dominates in terms of total value locked (TVL) in decentralized finance protocols with 57.60% of the $97.20 billion total.14 As far as stablecoins go, it also has more than 50% of the circulating supply, which is now $322 billion.


Ethereum supports a wide range of applications, including those concerning NFTs, dApps, and decentralized finance, often called “DeFi,” that allows users to lend, borrow, and trade assets directly without bank intermediation. Ethereum accomplishes this using auto-executable software programs called “smart contracts.” Despite the name, these “smart contracts” are somewhat simpler than the name implies. They are fairly sophisticated “if/then” computer algorithms that are preprogrammed to execute certain functions when a set of conditions is met. Consider a decentralized lending platform built on Ethereum whereby somebody deposits $100 worth of ETH as collateral to borrow $75 in a US dollar-denominated stablecoin. If the price of ETH drops to $85 or $80, bringing it below the value of the collateral standing behind the loan, the lending protocol will automatically sell that ETH collateral at the prevailing spot price to cover the position. In traditional finance, an action like this would likely be handled by a human person. However, decentralized applications on Ethereum (and other blockchains that use smart contracts) have preprogrammed instructions to auto-liquidate collateral such as this during times of duress.


dApp tokens first came to prominence in 2017 and 2018 during the boom in so-called initial coin offerings (ICOs),15 in which crypto startups raised millions in mere seconds, attracting often retail investors eager to invest money into any project with a whitepaper and an exciting promise.16 The total amount of funds raised by ICOs in 2017 was a staggering $5.6 billion, a significant leap from the $240 million raised in 2016.17 Projects like EOS raised $4.1 billion, making it the largest ICO of that year.18 Other notable ICOs included Telegram, which raised $1.7 billion, and Dragon, which raised $320 million.19 Nevertheless, the vast majority of these ICO projects failed, and the value of their assets went to zero, which was a reflection of the novelty, hyperbole, and excitement of the space, which I discuss later.


Nonetheless, today numerous dApp tokens have market capitalizations in the hundreds of millions or even billions of dollars and underpin applications with real utility and actual business operations that make money. The most valuable amongst these are DeFi tokens such as Aave, Uniswap, Ethena, Hyperliquid, SushiSwap, Curve, PancakeSwap, and Sky. Most of these are primarily built on the Ethereum Layer 1 blockchain.


Despite this impressive decade of performance, which has never seen the Ethereum blockchain go offline and has allowed it to achieve an unparalleled level of decentralization, Ethereum is not without its critics. For starters, as far as networks go, it is relatively slow. It can generally handle approximately 30 transactions per second.20 Rivals, such as Solana, can process thousands per second.21 Because of these limitations on throughput, which can cause transaction fees to surge from a few cents per dollar to as much as $70 during times of peak stress, the Ethereum network so far has proven unsuitable for particular use cases such as high-frequency trading or gaming, which require thousands of transactions to be written onto a blockchain per second.







## Solana



If Ethereum is the world computer, then Solana is pitched as “Nasdaq on the Blockchain,” a high-performance network boasting a thriving DeFi market, while also acting as an incubator for emerging industries.22 Like Ethereum, Solana is a Layer 1 smart contract platform, but it prioritizes ultrafast performance alongside decentralization, optimizing both rather than treating them as a binary trade-off.


Originally launched in March 2020, Solana is the most prominent of several Layer 1 protocols that were released around the onset of the pandemic, along with Avalanche, NEAR, Polygon, and Polkadot. However, at the time, there was no guarantee that it would achieve the breakout success it has achieved. In fact, one prominent crypto venture capitalist told Steven Ehrlich, a former senior editor at Forbes, that his biggest regret was passing on Solana’s seed round. At the time, he did not see anything about Solana’s pitch that made it stand out from the dozens of others that he was receiving.


Solana’s big break came from signing up FTX founder Sam Bankman-Fried (who we will meet in Chapter 5) as an unofficial spokesperson. In fact, during the pandemic boom, Bankman-Fried was at the height of his celebrity; he called himself a self-described “fanboy of Solana.” But if Solana was nothing more than a common Layer1 blockchain with an uber-famous spokesperson, it would not have survived to reach its lofty perch today, especially not after FTX collapsed and Bankman-Fried went to prison. The price of Solana dropped from a high of $250 in late 2021 to below $10 one year later.


Instead, the team behind the blockchain leaned heavily into use cases that required ultra-cheap and ultra-fast processing to function properly.23 The most prominent was trading of “meme coins” (discussed shortly)—a high-velocity, speculation-driven activity that demanded exactly the kind of cheap, near-instant settlement Solana was built to provide. A meme coin trading boom exploded in late 2024, and because almost all this activity was centered around Solana, it led to a surge in demand for the network, and by extension, the token. Today, Solana has a market capitalization of approximately $42 billion, is still leaning into use cases that require ultra-fast transactions with little to no latency, and is actively courting institutional activity where speed and settlement finality are nonnegotiable.







## XRP



XRP is one of the most prominent, but also controversial cryptocurrencies in crypto today (for reasons that I will explain in Chapter 5). Nevertheless, XRP is the third-largest token by market capitalization (not counting stablecoins), with a total value of $64.05 billion.24 The XRP token and its paired blockchain, the XRP Ledger, are intended to disintermediate the expensive and opaque correspondent banking network. XRP is intended as a “bridge” asset so that users (primarily banks and other commercial entities) can keep excess reserves in one fungible asset rather than having to prefill bank accounts across the world in an inefficient setup of various fiat currencies.


It was a lofty and noble goal for sure. But disrupting entrenched financial networks was never going to be easy, especially so early in the crypto innovation cycle of the mid-2010s. That was especially the case when Ripple Labs, the primary developer behind the XRP Ledger and holder of billions of dollars’ worth of the XRP token, was sued by a reactionary Securities and Exchange Commission (more on this later). With these legal entanglements now in the rearview mirror, Ripple Labs and XRP are aggressively pursuing a multichannel approach to growth. The company has invested billions of dollars in purchasing a prime broker, a corporate treasury platform, a stablecoin payments platform, and multiple custody services to create a full-suite institutionally focused financial services platform.25 It has also sought to evolve the XRP Ledger from focusing exclusively on payments into a full-suite smart contract platform. Ripple Labs launched its own proprietary stablecoin called RLUSD. And there is still plenty of runway left to continue building a full-stack financial services suite.







## SUI



Sui was a late challenger to Ethereum and other L1 blockchains mentioned previously. This blockchain burst onto the scene in 2022 when Mysten Labs, its primary developer, raised $300 million at a $2 billion valuation from an impressive group of investors, headlined by Andreesen Horowitz, Franklin Templeton, and Shark Tank investor Kevin O’Leary (aka, Mr. Wonderful) in August 2022. The Sui blockchain itself went live in May the following year.


In sitting down with Forbes for a profile in the summer of 2023,26 Evan Cheng, Mysten’s cofounder and CEO, was asked whether the world really needed another L1 blockchain. After all, at the time, the market was still recovering from the collapse of FTX and the crypto winter of 2022. Perhaps it made sense to focus more on driving demand than on more infrastructure?


Cheng did not mince words. “Ethereum is completely wrong,” he said at the time. “It’s considered a standard, but there are too many things broken about it.” In fact, Cheng believed that the current wave of crypto companies investing more capital and resources in Ethereum is a big mistake. “Everybody seems to say, ‘Oh well, let’s just keep building on top [of Ethereum], because this is where the users are,’” says Cheng. “But I see a path to nowhere. I don’t see [the] technology being useful for anything at scale.”


Cheng believes Sui’s competitive advantage is its programming language, called Move, an open-source programming language that Cheng helped develop when he worked on Facebook’s notorious Libra project along with the rest of the founding team at Mysten Labs. Move is supposed to be more user-friendly because it allows blockchains to update assets (such as video game characters) in real life and instantaneously. The project is still advancing strategies to achieve breakthrough product-market fit, but it splits its focus between consumer applications requiring a high load of transactions and data (such as payments and finance, including tokenization of real-world assets) and creating a full stack of decentralized tools for developers building large and small-scale applications. Sui is also making a strong play for the AI economy.







## Canton Coin



As I have described, much of my career was spent in institutional financial markets on Wall Street. Unsurprisingly, I am interested in blockchain networks that serve the needs of professional traders, lenders, market utilities, and exchanges. The next network that I will describe—Canton—was designed specifically for institutional financial transactions. It is a public blockchain that enables secure, interoperable, and, crucially, privacy-preserving transactions. It connects different financial systems while ensuring compliance with regulatory requirements. It aims to modernize post-trade processes and enhance the efficiency of financial markets in a fully regulated fashion.


As explained, two foundational aspects of Bitcoin and other early decentralized blockchains are transparency and censorship resistance. The first public ledgers used transparency to catalyze open innovation, enable composability, and lay a new foundation of trust that allowed developers everywhere to build on one another’s advances.27 This transparency is an important first principle, as crypt-native communities value openness and censorship resistance.28


But the same transparency is a primary barrier to adoption by large financial institutions and professional market participants. Having spent much of my professional career on Wall Street and in Washington regulating it, I know that the financial services industry cannot operate under a fully transparent financial architecture. The reasons are as much regulatory compliance as practical reality. The public transparency of Bitcoin and similar open-architecture blockchains is challenging for institutional finance because it exposes sensitive financial data, such as trading strategies and positions, asset portfolios, client information, staff compensation, etc. It also compromises the compliance obligations of regulated financial institutions that must also adhere to strict client confidentiality obligations.


Canton addresses this concern by making privacy a core element of its L1 design. First launched in 2023 and initially developed by the blockchain infrastructure firm Digital Asset,29 the Canton Network is supported by a broad consortium of major financial institutions (including Bank of America, BNP Paribas, BNY, Citadel, Citi, DTCC, EuroClear, Goldman Sachs, and iCapital), traditional exchanges (including Nasdaq and the London Stock Exchange Group), crypto trading firms and platforms (Cumberland DRW, Robinhood, OKX and Kraken), technology firms (Microsoft and Broadridge), and stablecoin providers (Circle and Paxos).


What makes Canton appropriate for use by large financial institutions is that it utilizes a unique architecture that allows each institution to maintain its own ledger while connecting through a shared synchronization layer. In this way, the Canton network is very much like the internet itself: a network of the ledgers of its participants, a network of networks. It allows each participant to have its own privacy model (who can see what), permissions (who can do what), scalability (how many transactions to process), and governance (how rules are set and changed). That is why it is named “Canton,” reflecting the Swiss confederacy of 26 fully sovereign states or cantons that band together for common purposes, but otherwise maintain their unique prerogatives.


With Canton, privacy protection is not an overlay; it is the default operating mode. Only the parties explicitly named on a smart contract—or those given observer rights—can see the contents of that contract and its associated transactions.30 “Privacy is not anonymity. It’s the ability to share information on a need-to-know basis,” according to Yuval Rooz, cofounder and CEO of Digital Asset.31 For example, two institutions transacting with a stablecoin on Canton can keep their activity visible only to themselves and designated observers, with no leakage to the wider network. This “need-to-know” distribution of data is what allows Canton to support regulated use cases that would be impossible on fully transparent public blockchains.32 It is also the only way that Wall Street and professional finance can operate on-chain. Firms cannot run payroll on-chain if competitors can know each other’s salaries. No trading firm can compete if its competitors can see its entire trading positions and asset inventory, executive bonus structure, revenue sharing arrangements, and historic transaction patterns.


On the other hand, Canton’s transactional privacy does not mean regulatory opacity. Rather, the Canton network ensures compliance with regulatory requirements—essential for regulated banks and exchanges and institutional market participants—through a design that allows for selective disclosure of transaction data to meet regulatory requirements without data leakage to the market. Using smart contracts, Canton can include regulators as network observers to provide necessary oversight without exposing sensitive information to competitors.


Canton Coin is used for transaction fees and rewards for network participants. It supports various financial applications, including tokenization of real-world assets, business-to-business payments, on-chain derivatives, and settlement workflows. Canton Coin is not meant for price speculation, but to reward app builders and users and to compensate those who build and run the network.


Unlike the other blockchains mentioned earlier, which are permissionless and allow anyone to observe and participate, Canton allows anyone to connect and build while maintaining granular control over privacy and access. These rules are defined by each application, meaning fully permissionless and highly controlled environments can coexist and interoperate on the same network. Canton doesn’t compete with other blockchains. Its goal is to connect as many on-chain financial assets, applications, and blockchains as possible on a shared network without sacrificing privacy or control.







## System Upgrade



In 2025, a series of new digital tokens were introduced by stablecoin firms like Tether (Plasma) and Circle (ARC), trading exchange Bitfinex, infrastructure provider ChainLink (LINK), and payment specialists like Stripe (Tempo) and Alchemy Pay (Alchemy Chain). The tokens provide a range of functions from payments to utilization of functions on underlying Layer 1 blockchains. According to Citi analysts, their launch is not just about adding more tokens to the mix but about owning the foundational infrastructure for the next generation of payments.33 The objective is to solve for institutional-grade settlement finality while building a base layer capable of supporting global, always-on money movement.34


Separate and quite distinct from the major cryptocurrencies that I have described are digital currencies that, by design, have little or no underlying utility, technology, or economic purpose. “Meme coins” are cryptocurrencies created primarily around internet humor, celebrity culture, or viral social media moments. The original meme coin was Dogecoin, launched in 2013 as a joke based on the popular “Doge” internet meme. It became the template for thousands of imitators.


Often meme coins are pump-and-dump schemes—insiders accumulate coins cheaply, hype them on social media, and sell into the retail buying frenzy, leaving latecomers with worthless tokens. They have no earnings, no technology, and no intrinsic value. In short, meme coins are the digital equivalent of lottery tickets sold at a carnival—occasionally someone wins big, but the house nearly always wins, and the carnival moves on quickly to the next town.


Considering all these different tokens and blockchains, what the exactly is crypto? The answer is “all of the above”! It is an internet-based financial architecture. It is computer protocols using distributed ledger technology and cryptography to record ownership and transfer of intrinsic or implied value on the internet, rather than the way value is currently transferred and stored as assets and liabilities recorded on the siloed and disconnected balance sheets of the world’s roughly 7,000 financial institutions.


To appreciate the potential of blockchain-based technology to revolutionize the legacy financial system, consider the convoluted way we currently record things of value. If you stop to think about it, today’s financial system is a fairly abstract, arcane, and piecemeal system. It may not seem convoluted since we take it for granted. But consider that whatever you have saved up for retirement is not stacks of $100 bills in some physical vault managed by T. Rowe Price or Charles Schwab. The money you have in your bank checking and savings accounts are not being held in cash in some bank’s safe. Rather, what you think of as “your money” is really just an IOU, a liability on the balance sheets of a bank or other financial service firm. The money you deposited in your bank account has already been lent out to other bank customers. You may think you have “money in the bank,” but you really are just a creditor to a series of banks and other financial institutions. When you go to withdraw what you think is your “money,” you are really asking those institutions to make good on their IOUs, not to go and retrieve any actual money they are holding for you in a vault somewhere.


Similarly, when you buy stocks today, you do not actually own them. Your broker owns them. Your custodian holds them. A clearinghouse settles them. You are just the beneficial owner recorded on some ledger. Your salary that is directly deposited becomes another IOU from some large bank. If either Citibank or Chase or some other Main Street bank were to fail this afternoon, you could wait a long time to get your money back. Yes, you may recover pretty quickly a good portion of it (up to $250,000) through the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation (FDIC) and maybe all of it years later when bankruptcy proceedings are finally resolved. Still, what you think of as your “money” is nothing in our current system other than balance sheet liabilities of large and highly centralized institutions, institutions that do indeed go bankrupt, as we saw during the Biden administration when 3 of the 30 largest US bank institutions went belly up.35


And yet, since 2008, Bitcoin has never been hacked or disrupted.36 It has never failed to work as designed. It is true that access to the internet can be disrupted from time to time in certain geographies. Many readers may have been impacted by the widespread Verizon outage that impacted the eastern United States in January 2025. But since Bitcoin and other blockchains are globally distributed, other nodes pick up the slack until everyone comes online. The same principle holds when large crypto miners are driven offline due to weather patterns.


Using another example, your ownership of your home or automobile—even your identity—is nothing more than official records housed in a government registry, at least for government and legal purposes. When the Great Chicago Fire ravaged much of the city in October 1871, including Cook County’s land records, almost all property documentation was destroyed, leading to decades of chaos over lost property ownership rights.37 More recently, in July of 1973, an enormous fire destroyed approximately 17 million US military personnel files housed in the National Personnel Records Center in St. Louis, Missouri. Those records have never been recovered.38


Further, these examples do not even get into the constellation of middlemen that siphon value across the payment ecosystem. Sending an international wire from an institution like Citibank can cost $35,39 and it might take days to arrive. Services such as Western Union can be much more expensive, and you can lose money on the fee as well as a margin charged on the exchange rate from the provider.40 In contrast, using blockchains such as Ethereum or Tron (which is a dominant platform in the stablecoin space) costs less than $0.50.41 And the money gets there instantly.







## Digital Photography



Compared to the arcane and convoluted design of the traditional financial system, the prospect of recording value on immutable ledgers distributed over the internet does not really seem so challenging. The premise of crypto is simple: digital network architecture and asset tokenization. I have described digital network architecture. But what is tokenization? In simple terms, tokenization is the process of converting rights to a real-world asset—such as real estate, stocks, bonds, or commodities—into a digital token recorded on a blockchain of the types I have described. Think of it like this: instead of owning a single share certificate for a piece of property, you hold a digital token that represents your ownership stake. That token is programmable, instantly transferable, and verifiable by anyone on the network without needing a central intermediary like a bank or clearinghouse. In short, tokenization takes the analog world of traditional assets and makes them native to the digital financial system—faster, more accessible, and more transparent.


To appreciate the usefulness of digital tokens, think of digital photography.42 Somewhere in your home (perhaps in your basement or attic), there may be dozens of photo albums stuffed with physical film photographs taken before the advent of smartphones. Sadly, you probably only rarely open those albums to look at those photos. Yet, right now on your smartphone, you have hundreds of digital photos taken over the past decade. Not only do you view these photos often, you can edit them, text and post them, and share them with friends at lunch and colleagues at work. Because of this capability, this usage of digital photos—their “utility”—is exponentially greater than old physical print photographs.


In the coming future, all things of value that are not in the form of digital tokens will be like those old photos in dusty albums—unused, not readily accessible, and forgotten. More importantly, things of value—your money, title to your car, your 401(k)—will be tokenized and on a digital network and will be as useful, shareable, and as readily available to you as the photos on your smartphone.


And this leads to not just “money” but anything of value being tokenized and accessible on global digital networks, including stocks, bonds, commodities, debt instruments, real estate, video game items, digital collectibles, software licenses, birth records, identity, and much more. This new infrastructure will be free to use and run on open-source code, displacing rent-collecting middlemen and providing individuals with more financial sovereignty to engage with a global financial system that is more efficient, transparent, and equitable than what we have today. Just as the digital photo gallery on your smartphone has replaced the leather-bound photo albums in your attic, so will the workings of the digital wallet on your smartphone replace your interaction with bank tellers and online checking accounts. Finance will become in-hand: immediate, constant, and available with just a screen swipe.


There are many benefits of tokenization, starting with efficiency and cost reduction. As mentioned, tokenization reduces the need for intermediaries, paperwork, and manual processes, making asset management and transfer faster and more efficient. It enhances overall operational efficiency by minimizing administrative delays and costs. It enables assets to be bought and sold more easily, increasing market liquidity. Moreover, tokenization allows investors to purchase smaller portions of high-value assets, making them more accessible to a broader range of investors. Tokenization also improves transparency by providing a secure and transparent ledger for recording transactions, reducing fraud and enhancing trust. Additionally, the use of smart contracts with tokens automates transactions and enforces terms, ensuring compliance and reducing the risk of disputes. Tokenization also increases accessibility and global participation by allowing assets to be traded 24 hours a day and seven days a week (24/7) on blockchain platforms. Finally, tokenization will reshape finance by creating secondary markets for assets that were previously difficult to trade, such as real estate, private equity, and luxury goods, and creating a clear provenance of title for luxury watches or ordinary automobiles. Because of all these advantages, it is very likely that everything of value that can be tokenized will be tokenized. Tokenization is going to revolutionize finance.







## Fad or Future?



Sounds pie-in-the-sky, right? Well, so was the promise of internet e-commerce 25 years ago during the bursting of the so-called “dot-com bubble” of the late 1990s. In just a few weeks in March 2000, many previously high-flying online shopping or “e-commerce” businesses went bust, notably Pets.com, Webvan, and Boo.com.43 In 2000, there were only about 360 million people worldwide engaged in e-commerce. Far more people were likely wondering if the “dot-bomb” crash was the end of internet shopping. Some reporters, like those at the Daily Mail, speculated that the internet itself was just a “passing fad.”



Famously, Barron’s magazine ran a cover story in May 1999 that called out one of the new dotcoms and its CEO as “just another middle man” whose claims that he has “pioneered a new business paradigm is silly.” The title of the article was “Amazon.bomb” and the CEO and his company profiled were Jeff Bezos and Amazon.44


In fact, it was not the case in 2000 that people did not want to buy pet supplies and other goods online. They just struggled to do so using dial-up modems, putting their credit cards into dodgy websites, and waiting three weeks for their packages to arrive. It took the arrival of technology like 3G, 4G, and 5G technology, services like PayPal and Apple Pay, and vendors like eBay and Amazon to make e-commerce a practical experience.



In all, e-commerce captured only 1.2% of sales in the United States at the time of the “dot-com” crash, but that number grew to 4.6% within 10 years, a total of $165 billion.45 Today, e-commerce is a $6.8 trillion global industry with close to 3 billion consumers.46 Moreover, the number of people who use the internet is well over five billion, most of the world’s population. There is no going back.


As for “Amazon.bomb,” in February 2026, it surpassed Walmart as the world’s largest company measured by annual corporate revenue47 with a market capitalization of over $2.3 trillion dollars.


In many ways, we are at a similar early stage in the development of crypto as we were 25 years ago with e-commerce. Engaging with crypto technology is still remarkably clunky and daunting. Investing in cryptocurrency presents a range of practical challenges, including high volatility, cybersecurity risks, and the continuing lack of comprehensive regulatory clarity. Additionally, many investors struggle with understanding the technology and market dynamics involved in cryptocurrencies. Setting up accounts on exchanges and managing and understanding trading platforms can be daunting for beginners. That includes managing public and private cryptographic keys and the so-called “seed phrases” that are critical for managing and securing crypto wallets.


But it will get easier, just as it did with e-commerce. Today, one in four Americans—roughly 67 million people—own at least some crypto.48 And, crypto is not just a wealthy person’s asset. 90% of holders earn under $500,000 a year, and nearly a quarter make $75,000 or less. Today’s holders are just as likely to work in construction or manufacturing as in tech or finance. Some use it to invest in their financial future, others for art and games, and still more are simply curious and testing the waters.49


Many Americans are already using crypto to make everyday purchases.50 Half of large US retailers now accept crypto at checkout, including Home Depot, Whole Foods, Starbucks and Steak’n Shake.51 Eighty-two percent of small US merchants report that Gen Z customers were specifically asking to pay in crypto.52 Seventy-three percent of Gen Z and younger said that they want the option to pay with crypto at checkout.53 “When you are paying with credit card, check, debit card, Zelle, Venmo, PayPal, and any of those apps, what’s really happening is it’s just IOUs moving back and forth behind the scenes. But with crypto, it’s the actual asset and the actual store of value that is moving.”54







## Digital Network Economies



In his essential work, Read, Write, Own, renowned venture capitalist Chris Dixon points out that the internet of information has gone through two major design eras.55 In the first, users could access and read internet content but had limited interaction with it (what he calls “read” phase). In the second, users could interact, create, and share increasingly dynamic internet content in return for surrendering control over that content to an oligopoly of data cartels like Facebook, Amazon and Netflix. In this second phase, these cartels extract nearly all the profits that user-generated content generates.56


Dixon says that we are now on the cusp of the third era of democratizing ownership (what he calls the “own” phase).57 In this period, cryptocurrencies themselves become the unit by which value flows directly between users without being siphoned off by intermediaries like banks and money transmitters. Crypto becomes more than a payment mechanism; it is the means for users to borrow, lend, and trade assets directly with each other without middlemen. Crypto further allows for shared access to resources like internet bandwidth or computational power, such as Filecoin’s provision of computer storage58 and Ethereum’s enabling of peer-to-peer lending at lower fees than banks.


Some analysts liken the economic activity that takes place on one or another of the various blockchains to that which takes place in a national economy or other geographical area of production, distribution and trade.59 In this view, a blockchain is akin to a sovereign “digital economy” with its native token, such as Ether (ETH) or Solana (SOL), functioning as its unit of account and sovereign currency to incentivize participants to build in its ecosystem rather than in others.60 Just as the value of a traditional sovereign currency is tied to the strength of the national economy that issues the currency, cryptocurrencies derive their value from explicit demand for the particular goods and services available on their related underlying blockchain.61 And, if you believe that crypto is the future of financial architecture, then the components of that architecture, the digital tokens, are valuable of and by themselves.


According to digital finance visionary Mike Cagney, Crypto tokens have three primary drivers for value: yield, utility, and governance. Yield means the amount the token pays you to hold it on its blockchain. Utility means what benefits come with holding the token, such as reduced fees or greater convenience (such as with digital photos) or better loan rates, etc. Governance means the way in which the token supports decentralization and allows for voting relating to yield and utility.62


Dixon argues that the current internet, dominated by a few giant corporations, is fraught with issues such as data privacy concerns, censorship, and monopolistic practices.63 Web3, a vision of the next evolution of the internet built on decentralized blockchain technology, aims to address these shortcomings by giving power back to the users. He shows how decentralization removes intermediaries and gives individuals direct control over their assets rather than entrusting them to large impersonal banks or centralized platforms.64 It reverses the Big Tech model of YouTube and Facebook, in which you are the product, into one in which users own their own content, launch their own enterprises, monetize their creativity, and participate in the governance of their own communities, moving power away from online data oligarchs to the users themselves.65


In their foreword to CryptoDad, crypto billionaires Cameron and Tyler Winklevoss noted that, “Satoshi’s breakthrough not only made the decentralization of money possible, but it also provided a blueprint for the decentralization of anything.”66 One of those things is the very nature of contemporary corporate enterprises. Decentralization enables the payment of compensation to collaborative group efforts by otherwise unassociated people around the world, a prototype for a new type of corporation where economic value flows to users rather than being distributed out to promoters, financiers, managers, and commercial middlemen. In this new type of decentralized association, individuals may come together for specific tasks and be paid with tokens related to that task. Then, they may separate and regroup with others for other tasks and be paid with tokens related to that task.


Decentralization also furthers decision-making by communities rather than corporate boards through transparent rules that users vote upon, weighted by their contributions to the mission of the enterprise or their holdings in it. This does away with restrictive terms of service, limitations on access to competitors’ “apps,” and hidden algorithms that control access and financial benefits by today’s internet oligarchs. In their place are collaborative communities that can collectively manage platforms, create innovations, offer rewards, and agree on open rules satisfactory to users. Such direct compensation and shared ownership and decision-making are transforming power dynamics on the web, leading to the “own” design era of the internet.67


After a decade of researching crypto, successfully regulating it, and advising many of its most innovative companies, I have come to firmly believe that there will not be any one “winning” crypto, but many. Global economic activity will come to be built upon many different blockchains, not devolved down to a particular one. In part, that is because “no single blockchain network can handle all of the world’s transactions; there will be many.”68 The coming decades of the twenty-first century will see a kaleidoscope of different digital networks of value. Some of them will be fully decentralized like Bitcoin and operated collaboratively by their users, some will be partially decentralized, and others will be highly centralized and run by global banks, tech firms, and even sovereign governments such as China and the European Union. Some will be permissioned, and some will be permissionless; some private and some fully transparent. I have no doubt that the digital future—the Internet of Value—will be as complex as the analog network past.


Let’s now take a look at one of the emerging components of this digital future: stablecoins.
