# Chapter 3 Stablecoins




How a type of cryptocurrency called stablecoins will make it possible for computers to do more than talk to each other, but transact with each other




As mentioned in the previous chapter, stablecoins are tokenized digital assets on distributed digital ledgers specifically designed to address the inherent volatility of crypto. They serve the three functions of money: a means of payment, unit of account, and store of value. They achieve the latter by being backed by other assets with a stable value, like government bonds, gold, or some other stabilizing feature. Stablecoins are differentiated by four different types of price stabilizers: fiat currency and cash equivalents, other cryptocurrencies, algorithmic equations, and bank deposits. All are intended to avoid the dramatic US dollar price volatility of cryptocurrency such as Bitcoin. As a result, stablecoins provide a less volatile, more stable medium of exchange capable of fulfilling daily transactional needs. Hence their name: stablecoins.


From a starting point of just under $10 billion five years ago, the supply of stablecoins in circulation exceeded $320 billion in May 2026.1 The amount of dollars held in stablecoins is greater than the foreign exchange reserves of 95 nations, including developed economies such as the United Kingdom and Canada.2 The annual stablecoin transfer volume reached $33 trillion in 2025,3 surpassing the combined volumes of Visa and Mastercard.4 Walmart and Amazon are both exploring how to launch their own stablecoins.5 This relatively rapid growth and adoption of USD-pegged stablecoins confirms that global demand for the US dollar has been underserved by the American dollar in its current non-digitally networked state.



USDC stablecoin issuance 2018–2025






## The Problem with Legacy Payments



Enthusiasm for stable digital currencies is based on several inefficiencies and limitations of the current system for global payments. Over the past decades, legacy bank payment systems, especially in the United States, have failed to modernize and seamlessly solve the challenges of consumers and businesses.6 Although these systems handle trillions of dollars in transactions each day, transaction costs remain high, speeds are slow, and they are limited to bankers’ hours.


You might think of the current global payment system as an airline route map that only offers connecting flights and no nonstop travel. Stablecoins replace those connecting routes with an all “direct flight” service: faster, cheaper, and available around the clock. The reason why the current global payment system is so slow and cumbersome is because it is based upon the delivery of messages passed between payors, banks, beneficiaries, and intermediaries containing instructions for payments, securities transactions, and foreign exchange dealings. SWIFT, the Belgium-based “Society for Worldwide Interbank Financial Telecommunication,” is a global cooperative that is the backbone of the current system of payment messages.7 SWIFT facilitates these messages using specific formats and standards, which ensure consistency and reduce errors in international transactions. However, message-based payment systems present numerous operational inefficiencies, including slowness (or latency), trapped liquidity caused by the disparate operating hours of banks and financial intermediaries across the globe and the elevated settlement and credit risk incurred when mutual transfer of funds and/or assets does not happen simultaneously.


A recent tweet on X8 humorously described the current process of a wire transfer of money as taking the following steps:




- Fill out this form.



- Provide the home address of the recipient.



- Disclose why you are sending the funds.



- Sign this document.



- Your signature doesn’t match.



- Do it over again.



- Ok, apparently it matches now.



- Confirm via recorded phone line.



- Okay, it is now past 4:00 p.m., so we’ll send tomorrow.



- Wait five days (weekend).



- Ask bank where is the money.



- Something went wrong, so the money is now back in the account.



- No one knows why.



- Get charged anyway.



- Go back to step one!


Compare these 15 steps with a digital currency transaction:




- enter wallet address,



- click send,



- wait a few seconds,



- done.


Clearly, twentieth-century money is too slow, too cumbersome and too human.


According to Chris Dixon:



… why is it still so hard—and expensive—to move money? The early internet promised a future where anyone could publish, build, or transact without permission. Protocols like email and the web were open and neutral – and they sparked an explosion of creativity, innovation and entrepreneurship. But somewhere along the way, we veered off course. Today, the global financial systems resembles a patchwork of corporate networks: centralized, closed and extractive. Behind every transaction is a Rube Goldberg-machine of intermediaries – points of sale, payment processors, acquiring banks, issuing banks, local banks, correspondent banks, foreign exchanges, card networks and others – each taking a cut, adding latency, and imposing rules.9




Stablecoins address many of the drawbacks of the existing global payment system. Their growing popularity arises from their speed, efficiency, and almost instantaneous settlement, lowering payment transaction costs, benefiting consumers and small businesses. For the millions of the world’s foreign workers, stablecoins slash the cost of international remittances. Today, sending $200 from the United States to the Philippines costs between 7% and 17%, a high fee on some of the world’s poorest workers.10 With stablecoins, it costs pennies, is instantaneous, and is available 24 hours a day, seven days a week. Direct flights around the clock.


The friction and latency of the current money transmission system persist despite the United States having the deepest capital markets, the best engineering talent, a large consumer base, and the world’s top technology hubs. Europe, while lagging the US economy overall in new business formation, technological innovation, and overall economic growth, has produced and introduced more new payment and related fintech innovations. The discrepancy is largely the result of outdated US regulations and policies that have entrenched a clear preference for traditional banking models and locked in legacy money movement systems. It is not surprising, then, that researchers affiliated with MIT found that the “US experiences substantial frictions due to legacy infrastructure, market fragmentation, and lack of competition.”11 Demand for stablecoins might be lower had the American banking system been more open to payments innovation and not directed its formidable political lobbying to stifle legislative initiatives for change, a pattern being played out again in 2026 with efforts to pass the Clarity Act.


The initial use case for stablecoins was almost solely for the purpose of facilitating crypto trading and speculation.12 In time, usage broadened to the growing metaverse economy of virtual events and real estate, online gaming, non-fungible NFT collecting, and other activities. In cyberspace, stablecoins have become the primary means of exchange,13 with the US dollar serving as the reference currency. At the same time in the physical world, stablecoins increasingly provide a superior user experience over analog dollars with their ease of uploading to and managing on smartphones.14 USD stablecoins will find an instant application in those countries dealing with high inflation and/or political instability, where the US dollar is already a preferred instrument to the local sovereign currency. In such cases, digital dollar stablecoins are likely to become the chosen means of transacting, a store of relatively more stable value, and a desired means of payment.15 Yet, stablecoin adoption is not just taking place on the global and metaversal periphery. As corporations, financial service providers, and asset managers adopt programmable money, stablecoins will be increasingly used for everyday financial and commercial transactions.16


Let’s look at the leading stablecoins.







## Tether



USDT or Tether is a stablecoin designed to maintain a stable 1:1 value against the US dollar, backed by an equivalent amount of USD held in reserve. Operating on blockchains like Tron and Ethereum, it enables fast, low-cost transactions worldwide and serves as a critical bridge between crypto markets and traditional currency—letting traders and investors hold a stable position without converting back to fiat. A strong argument can be made that Tether, not the giant cryptocurrency trading platforms Coinbase and Binance, is the most important company in crypto today. Its USDT stablecoin has a circulating supply of approximately 186 billion tokens at the end of 2025.


USDT’s ever-growing supply is backed by short-term US treasuries. Tether has become one of the largest purchasers of  T-bills in the world today—as well as a growing investment portfolio focused on everything from bitcoin mining to AI brain implants.17 In fact, Tether is arguably the most profitable company in the industry, generating nearly all of its revenue from the interest on those treasury holdings. The $189 billion invested at 3% interest pays approximately $6.3 billion a year, before accounting for appreciation on other assets on other balance sheet assets, such as gold and Bitcoin. According to the company’s latest quarterly attestation, produced by BDO Italia, the firm held $192.8 billion in assets on its balance sheet against $186.5 billion in liabilities (circulating USDT as of December 31, 2025).18 These figures mean that there was a surplus of $6.3 billion.


On April 14, 2026, Tether officially launched tether wallet—its first self-custodial, consumer digital wallet. This marks a significant evolution: Tether is shifting from being purely an infrastructure provider (the issuer of USDT) to a direct-to-consumer product company. Tether is also turning its attention to the US market, which it has long sidestepped due to regulatory concerns, including a 2021 settlement with the New York Attorney General arising from an earlier claim that USDT was backed 1:1 by US dollars.19 With the GENIUS Act now signed into law (see later), Tether has reengaged the US market with a fully compliant stablecoin, USAT.


It was a long and often precarious road for Tether to reach this position. Until quite recently, a vocal contingent of so-called “Tether Truthers” spread FUD—fear, uncertainty, and doubt—that Tether did not hold sufficient reserves to redeem every USDT in circulation.


No single anecdote better illustrates how sharply perceptions of the company have shifted than this account from a 2022 Forbes profile on Tether, in which the magazine named various Tether executives as billionaires for the first time.20



On Monday, November 7, 2022, Tether executives received an unusual phone call from a long-time business partner, FTX CEO Sam Bankman-Fried.


Bankman-Fried, the youthful moppy-haired crypto executive who rode the crypto wave to a personal fortune of $26.5 billion, sounded desperate. A news story in the trade press five days earlier revealed that underpinning the highly leveraged balance sheet of his trading firm Alameda Research was some $5 billion worth of a token Bankman-Fried had essentially invented called FTT. FTX and Alameda were big customers of Tether. To date Bankman-Fried had already minted $36 billion worth of its US dollar-based stablecoin USDT, almost half of the total amount created.


“He contacted us and asked for economic help,” says [Tether CEO] Ardoino. “He didn’t disclose details or exactly how much he needed, but we categorically refused.”


Ardoino says that something didn’t smell right about the request and that it was an easy decision to say no. “He suddenly asked for something that he had never asked for before, and he wasn’t talking about $10 million. The way he was talking suggested that he had a big issue. His request was in the billions.”




It turned out that Ardoino was right to say, “No.” As I was also right to say “no” to Bankman-Fried, for reasons that I will detail shortly.







## Circle



Circle, which originally started out as a payment product for Bitcoin, moved into the stablecoin space, presenting itself as an antidote to the problematic reputation of Tether. “We like to be boring,” asserted Jeremy Allaire, Circle’s CEO, in a 2023 interview with Forbes.21 Helping Circle achieve that standing are strong and articulate executives: President Heath Tarbert, former CFTC Chairman and my immediate successor in that role, and Dante Disparte, chief strategy officer and veteran (with scars to show for it) of Diem, the permissioned blockchain-based stablecoin payment system proposed by American social media company Facebook that ran into political buzzsaws on both sides of the Atlantic.22


From the beginning of Circle’s journey, it presented itself as a regulatory-compliant stablecoin that was as safe as a bank account. It did not hide who its banking partners were. Long before being required to do so, Circle posted on its website its balances of US treasuries, down to the specific serial numbers.23 And unlike Tether, which for a time avoided the United States and was largely uninterested in regulatory developments in Washington, DC, Circle actively called for legislation to govern the ever-growing industry.


Of course, nothing is ever easy in crypto, and Circle was taught a difficult lesson in 2023 when it faced losing $3.3 billion in collateral held in Silicon Valley Bank (see Chapter 5). But the company has since recovered and is thriving. Its circulating supply is up to approximately $75 billion, and the firm went public in 2025 to a wildly successful IPO that minted Jeremy Allaire as a billionaire. With the US stablecoin industry now regulated under the GENIUS Act, Circle appears well-poised to benefit from the continued convergence of traditional finance and crypto.







## Paxos



Together, Tether and Circle constitute about 85% of the global stablecoin market.24 So far, no one else even comes close. However, their dominance peaked in March 2024 at 91.6% of global stablecoin supply and has been slipping since. The reasons are availability from providers of so-called “private label” stablecoin products, the search for greater interest or “yield” than that offered by the incumbents, and the new regulatory dynamics following passage of the GENIUS Act.25


PayPal’s (PYUSD) is a US dollar-denominated stablecoin on the Ethereum and Solana blockchains redeemable 1:1 for US dollars. PYUSD is operated for PayPal by Paxos Trust Company, a New York–based financial institution specializing in blockchain technology regulated by the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency.26 Founded in 2012, Paxos Trust Company offers other stablecoins: Pax dollar (USDP) and PAX Gold (PAXG), backed by physical gold. Paxos also offers the Global dollar (USDG), part of the rapidly growing the Global Dollar Network, which includes major financial institutions and trading platforms like Kraken, Robinhood, Anchorage, Galaxy, and Bullish, in addition to over a dozen other prominent partners.


Other notable stablecoins to watch include those issued by Ripple (RLUSD) and a stablecoin issued by Donald Trump’s World Liberty Financial (USD1). ZeroHash provides critical white-label infrastructure that enables institutions to embed stablecoin capabilities under their own brand.27 Additionally, executives from many key banks, including Bank of America, JPMorgan, and Citibank, have also expressed interest in issuing stablecoins, either to prevent deposit flight, capture those lucrative economics for themselves, or simply embrace the future.







## Smart Contracts to Smart Money



As noted, stablecoins offer a new way to move money globally with greater speed, transparency, and control.28 The biggest implication is the shift to real-time everything, where cash no longer sits idle in cut-off windows or multi-day settlement cycles.29 In this coming era, corporations will be able to move their cash continuously across subsidiaries, markets, and time zones, allowing treasurers to prioritize working capital on a rolling basis rather than at end of day. This compresses financial cycles: receivables, payables, payroll, and trade settlement will all happen in real time.30 As friction is reduced, the “velocity” of money in the financial system will dramatically increase, stimulating economic growth.


As we move into the digitally networked future, however, an even more impactful feature of stablecoins will be their programmability with smart contracts. This technological capability will not only allow money to move at the speed and simplicity of a text message but will enable innovators to develop suites of stablecoin “apps” and smart contracts for seamless financial transactions and investment, including lending, cross-border money transfers, and e-commerce. Over time, the competitive edge of stablecoins and on-chain money may rely less on narrow transaction cost advantages and more on programmability and real-time integration.31


According to Jeremy Allaire, CEO of Circle, Stablecoins are



…the highest utility form of money ever created. And we are not quite yet at the iPhone moment when developers everywhere realize the power and opportunity of programmable digital dollars on the Internet in the same way they saw the unlock of programmable mobile devices. Soon.32




The higher utility comes from the ability to integrate stablecoins into smart contract applications offering decentralized lending, swaps, automated market making, collateral management, payroll automation (imagine having your salary paid in discrete daily or even hourly increments rather than bi-monthly), cross-currency conversion, and trade finance functions (imagine immediate confirmation that your money arrived no matter where you sent it). Such a possibility to increase the “utility” of money for everyday users may be just on the horizon. It is “Bretton Woods meets Silicon Valley.”33 It is certainly about time.







## Programmable Money



As I explained in the first chapter, a key twentieth-century technological breakthrough was in the development of key algorithms and protocols like TCP-IP, HTTP, and HTML that enabled the first creation of the internet. These software protocols enabled personal computers to “talk” to each other. Once that happened, the internet as we know it was born. Then came the Internet of Things, in which ever smaller devices and apps interact with each other. It was unforeseeable 20 years ago that the birth of the smartphone would lead to the replacement of cameras, maps, GPS devices, calculators, alarm clocks, camcorders, pay phones, yellow pages, answering machines, newspapers, calendars, flashlights, watches, compasses, cookbooks, keys, cash, airplane and concert tickets, money, and (as I have explained) photo albums. And, yes, many of these things, now in app form, are indeed talking to each other.


With programmable stablecoins, we are now enabling computers and smart devices to do more than “talk” to each other, but to “transact” with each other. This is indeed the birth of the next wave of the internet—this Internet of Value that I have been writing about. Just as in the 1990s it was virtually impossible to imagine personal computers “talking” to each other could lead to all manner of innovations from Wikipedia to Facebook to Amazon to TikTok to Uber and to Zoom. Similarly, in the 2020s, it is quite hard to fully envision what will result from computers “transacting” with each other. But let me try.


Consider a typical long-distance flight during which you wish to catch up on email. Connecting to the airplane’s Wi-Fi requires putting credit card information into the airline’s payment portal on your laptop, initiating a traditional bank-to-bank payment while you wait for confirmation. And, whether you respond to one email or a hundred, you pay the same relatively high fee for internet access for the duration of the flight. Now, imagine instead that your laptop computer simply deposited minute by minute a fractional series of stablecoins into the airplane’s Wi-Fi computer for each email or text sent that is proportionate to the amount and duration of bandwidth used. Similarly, to read a Wall Street Journal article—and just that one article—plus that Economist piece that you have been saving to read on the flight. Stablecoins will allow you to pay for just what you want to read without having to buy a year’s subscription. They are ideal for micropayments because of their efficiency and cost-effectiveness. They can do so in tiny fractional increments, say $0.003 per email or article you read. And, with no extra cost, certainly not the $0.20 credit card processing fee that would otherwise be applied to each microtransaction.


Similarly, imagine pulling your electric vehicle up to a charging station, inserting the cable, and allowing your car’s computer to pay the charging station’s computer for each drawn kilowatt without having to take your wallet out of your pocket and swipe a credit card. Now imagine that instead of wasting time at a charging station, you are riding in your autonomous vehicle (probably watching a movie) along a road with embedded wireless technology capable of charging your vehicle’s battery as you ride by drawing from electromagnetic coils laid under the road surface and connected to the city’s power grid.34 Your vehicle’s battery is being charged as you go along through a process known as “inductive charging,” similar to the technology used in wireless charging for mobile phones.35 This is great, right? But now imagine that with today’s banking technology, you have to stop every 30 miles at a toll booth and pay for the electricity with a credit card or cash. Tomorrow, however, with stablecoins and smart contracts, your vehicle computer will simply pay the highway computer per kilowatt mile as you proceed your merry—likely driverless—way along the road.


Automated payments are just the beginning, however. The next step is the combination of stablecoins with “agentic AI,” the adoption of autonomous AI agents capable of making decisions, learning from data, and executing actions across complex financial systems.36 Unlike traditional generative AI, which requires human prompts that are increasingly familiar to users of Claude, Grok, or Perplexity, agentic AI systems serve as assistants or “agents” that are programmed to operate with a high degree of autonomy.37 They combine the intelligence of advanced AI models with access to tools so they can take actions on your behalf and pursuant to your instructions. They can “create a presentation for work, coordinate the family’s schedules, and pick a March Madness bracket, all while it learns our personal preferences, no coding needed.”38 PayPal is creating agentic AI shopping and commerce experiences through adoption of industry-leading systems such as Google’s Agent Payments Protocol. Imagine instructing your PayPal account to watch cruise line fares and when there is a special package deal within your set budget on trips to the Caribbean to book it, pay for it, and record the transaction with the family bookkeeper.39 Among other things, Starbucks is experimenting with AI to anticipate and adjust to customer preferences.40 Soon, the agentic AI smart contract in your car’s digital wallet will speak to Starbuck’s AI the moment you turn into the drive-in lane at any location. Your large, iced decaffeinated brown-sugar shaken espresso with dark caramel sauce and lavender cold foam will be paid for and ready to pick up at the drive-up window counter, while your credit card never leaves your wallet.


Agentic AI is not just about faster checkout, however. It is about delegating payment and payment decisions to the AI agent itself, enabling it to become an economic actor in its own right using stablecoins as its payment tokens.41 There will be limits, of course, auditability, and human override, but nevertheless, these AI actors will have agency. More and more, they will tailor highly personal investment portfolios and execute trades for clients. They will anticipate fraud patterns, rebalance investment portfolios, and adjust risk models in real time—creating entirely new, dynamic client experiences.42


And we are starting to see it happen. In October 2025, Coinbase launched “Payments MCP,” enabling large language models such as Claude and Gemini to perform wallet operations, fiat on-ramping, and stablecoin payments via natural language instructions.43 In March 2026, Visa’s Crypto Labs released an interface that allowed users to send card payments using simple text message commands.44 Before long, you will simply instruct your AI agent by voice through your iPhone, Apple watch, Oura Ring, or Meta’s Ray-Ban Smartglasses.45


So where are we on this road to tomorrow? The answer is we’re moving along ever faster. Stablecoins have advanced over the past three years from mere tools used by crypto traders and exchanges to engage in crypto speculation. Today, stablecoins are increasingly used as a stable store of value in place of failed currencies in bankrupt nation states,46 as immediate and low-cost money transfers around the globe, and in developed and wealthier economies for highly sophisticated consumer and enterprise payments in both the real economy and the growing metaverse. And this is just the start. With agentic AI, we are entering a new era-one where autonomous agents negotiate, manage, and move money on behalf of individuals, businesses, and even institutions themselves.47 In this era, agentic AI will provide the reasoning and decision-making, and stablecoins will provide the execution and flow, surpassing slow-moving twentieth-century, “connecting flight” money.48







## GENIUS



In July 2025, “The Guiding and Establishing National Innovation for US Stablecoins (GENIUS) Act” was passed by both the House and Senate with bipartisan support and signed by President Trump in a White House ceremony attended by key legislators and leaders of the stablecoin industry.


The GENIUS Act is the most significant US financial services legislation to be adopted since the 2008 Dodd-Frank Act. It establishes a US state and federal regulatory framework for USD-pegged stablecoins operated by banks, financial technology companies, and retailers. It requires stablecoin firms to hold one US$ in high-quality liquid assets like US Treasuries for every US$ of stablecoin issued to customers. It also requires stablecoin issuers to undergo audits and meet transparency standards to confirm to stablecoin customers that those liquid US Treasuries securities are held and available for redemption. The GENIUS Act appoints regulators at both the federal and state level to oversee stablecoin issuers and grants sweeping oversight and rulemaking authority to the Department of Treasury.


The origins of the GENIUS Act go back to the early Biden administration. In November 2021, the president’s Working Group on Financial Markets (PWG), along with the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation (FDIC), and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC), issued a comprehensive interagency report that examined the risks and regulatory gaps surrounding stablecoins and provided key recommendations for their oversight.49 The report identified key risk areas, including user protection, redemption runs, payment safety, economic power concentration, and illicit finance.50 It recommended that Congress act promptly to enact legislation requiring stablecoin issuers to be insured depository institutions under comprehensive federal prudential regulation.51 Nellie Liang, under secretary of the Treasury for Domestic Finance, ably led the White House efforts to steer legislation in Congress. While Senate action stalled, a House measure, the “Clarity for Payment Stablecoins Act,” made significant progress prior to the end of the 2023–2024 Congressional term.


Stablecoin legislation moved quickly in 2025 under Republican leadership in both chambers. In early April, the “Stablecoin Transparency and Accountability for a Better Ledger Economy (STABLE) Act” was introduced in the House of Representatives. Four weeks later, the GENIUS Act was introduced in the Senate and skillfully led by Tennessee Senator Bill Hagerty with support from Senators Tim Scott (R-SC), Chairman of the Senate Banking Committee Kirsten Gillibrand (D-NY), and Cynthia Lummis (R-WY). Following a series of amendments, including prohibiting stablecoin operators from lending activities and paying interest to stablecoin holders to assuage concerns about harm to commercial banks’ deposit taking and consumer lending, GENIUS passed the Senate in July 2025.52 Despite differences in the bills passed by the House and Senate, the House passed the Senate’s GENIUS version and it was promptly signed by President Trump.53


Upon Senate passage of the GENIUS Act, I tweeted:



And, upon its subsequent House passage of the GENIUS Act, I tweeted:



Passage of the GENIUS Act is intended to support the US dollar as the world’s primary reserve currency by satisfying unmet global demand. It recognizes that the global call for dollars is increasingly underserved by a low-utility, analog currency network.54 It does so from both the supply side through greater ease of digital access and on the demand side for the higher utility of tokenized and programmable money compared to traditional analog money. US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent estimated in July 2025 that USD-based stablecoins could hold as much as $3.8T in US Treasury securities within a few years.55


“Crypto-dollarization” of the globe through US dollar-based stablecoins could effectively supplant weak national currencies in places like Venezuela, Zimbabwe, and Bolivia. This will happen not through US government intervention but by a spontaneous “bottom up” process driven by consumers engaging in currency substitution in states with harsh currency controls, corrupt black markets, or failed domestic currencies. Such dollar dominance will be much more aggressive than conventional dollarization, which is often limited by the availability of physical banknotes. Stablecoins will eliminate the power of borders in currency choice and will be almost impossible to stop. Wherever a native currency is broken, a nation’s people will be able to replace it with a better one—with a swipe of the screen of their smartphone.


The most striking validation of the GENIUS Act’s promise came in late April 2026, when Meta—the social media giant behind Facebook, Instagram, and WhatsApp—quietly began paying select content creators in USDC stablecoins over the Solana and Polygon blockchain networks, with Stripe providing the payments infrastructure. The pilot launched first in Colombia and the Philippines, precisely the markets where legacy cross-border payment rails are most costly and exclusionary, offering a powerful demonstration of stablecoins’ practical utility for the financially underserved. Meta was emphatic that it was not issuing its own token—a deliberate departure from its ill-fated Libra project, which regulators on both sides of the Atlantic had killed four years earlier. This time, Meta threaded the needle: leveraging regulated, third-party infrastructure rather than seeking to become a monetary authority unto itself. The move carried significance well beyond Meta alone. With over three billion users across its platforms and nearly three billion dollars paid annually to creators, the potential scale of Meta’s stablecoin pipeline dwarfs most existing crypto payment networks combined. More broadly, it signaled that the major technology platforms—Shopify, Western Union, and others moving in the same direction—had concluded that the GENIUS Act’s regulatory clarity had made dollar-backed stablecoin payments not merely permissible, but strategically essential. What regulators once killed as Libra had returned, chastened and compliant, as something more durable: not a challenger to the dollar, but its digital champion.


It will be interesting to see what impact digital dollar stablecoins have on intact currencies of functioning states. It may be that all currencies, especially key reserve currencies, will need to adopt a digital network format, lest they lose out to those that do. Other major reserve currencies will likely seek to avoid being at a competitive disadvantage and lose market share to a high-utility, borderless digital US dollar. The GENIUS Act clearly caught the European Union off guard, pushing the European Central Bank to speed up its digital euro plans and consider issuing it on public blockchains like Ethereum or Solana, instead of the EU’s previously favored closed, centralized system.56 I expect that every major reserve currency economy will likely look to put its currency on digital networks, whether operated by its sovereign government, its central bank, or by private sector actors (stablecoins) or both.







## Run Prevention



The GENIUS Act does not apply to so-called algorithmic stablecoins that rely on complex smart contracts to maintain dollar parity and demand for their tokens. Assets of this type have collapsed in the past, with the most infamous example being the drop of TerraUSD all the way to zero in early 2022.57 Its dramatic fall erased $45 billion in value and in many ways precipitated the crypto bear market in 2022, which sparked the collapse of Sam Bankman-Fried’s FTX exchange in November of the same year.58


Instead, the GENIUS Act applies regulatory clarity to US dollar-based stablecoins. Yet, it will not likely prevent stablecoin runs.59 A recent MIT study found that while the GENIUS Act substantially improves reserve quality and disclosure, it implicitly treats stablecoin stability as a balance-sheet problem, resolvable through conservative asset holdings and supervision without regard to the functioning of redemption mechanisms, markets, and operational infrastructure.60 Otherwise deemed “safe” stablecoins may still experience instability when market liquidity, dealer balance-sheet capacity, or technical systems are strained.61 Stablecoins vary in transparency, with many issuers providing regular audits and reserve reports to demonstrate their backing assets. A recent Brookings Institution Research Report makes a series of thoughtful recommendations to enhance public trust in stablecoins and ensure financial stability.62


Even Circle, which prides itself on its regulatory compliance, faced a crisis in March 2023 when the collapse of Silicon Valley Bank63 left it with $3.3 billion in unsecured deposits. In the ensuing panic, the price of USDC dropped to $0.88. It took an extraordinary act by the FDIC, Federal Reserve, and Office of the Comptroller of the Currency to calm investor fears by guaranteeing all deposits, regardless of the FDIC limits of $250,000.


“It was pretty astonishing to have $3.3 billion in uninsured deposits,” says Sheila Bair the former FDIC chair, former independent board director of Paxos, to Forbes in a contemporaneous interview. “Circle is absolutely right in its disclosures that they’ve been very transparent, but nonetheless, the amount of uninsured funds was pretty remarkable.”64 This latter story is not to pick on Circle. It is meant to show that no law, even one as well-conceived as GENIUS, can protect consumers against every possible eventuality. Nothing in law or financial regulation can assure the one component ever necessary for the financial stability of stablecoins: public trust. In the view of Josh Lipsky, chair of international economics at the Atlantic Council. “There’s nothing that stablecoins can do that get to the foundations of the dollar, which are trust, fiscal processes, rule of law and the independence of monetary authorities.”65


As one astute commentator noted, “… trust in money can’t be outsourced to code. It is created by institutions, audits, and rules. The irony is that a technology born of rebellion against financial oversight now seeks legitimacy through the same disclosures and supervision it once tried to escape.”66


The biggest disappointment in the GENIUS Act, however, is that it infringes on Americans’ financial privacy by imposing upon stablecoin issuers and their customers the existing financial surveillance regime under the US Bank Secrecy Act. I will discuss this in greater detail in Chapter 12.


Let’s first examine two “killer apps” for crypto: tokenized land records and event prediction markets.

# Chapter 4 Crypto Killer Apps: Digital Land Records and Prediction Markets




An examination of two “killer aps” for crypto: digital land records and event prediction markets, and how they will revolutionize the way we secure the past and anticipate the future




Innovators have begun to envision an enormous amount of practical uses for tokenization and digital asset technology, though far less than what will be realized in the years and decades to come. In 2015, the Global Blockchain Business Council identified 101 examples from A to Z.1 They range from farming and agriculture, where blockchain technology is being used to individually identify cattle in domesticated herds, more carefully distribute meals in African Zambia, and count wild jaguars in central American rain forests. In healthcare, blockchain is being used to protect the integrity of medical research and the security of human genomic data. In education, blockchain technology is being used to support and increase the efficiency of student loan financing; in the arts, it helps prove the provenance of artistic creation and ownership; and in sports, it enhances fan engagement. Law enforcement is using blockchain to track illicit drug trafficking and prevent money laundering and sanctions evasion.


Let’s have a look at two increasingly proven applications for blockchain and crypto: municipal land records and event prediction markets. One is a centralized network run by local governments, and the other is priced and valued by the participants themselves. Together, they will revolutionize the way we secure the past and anticipate the future. Both are happening now.





## Land Records



Twenty-seven years ago, my wife and I, with three small children in tow, purchased a ramshackle 1897 Shingle Style2 house in a small town in northern New Jersey. Wanting to know more about the history of this unique house, I spent a day in the land records department of the Bergen County Clerk’s office. There in a file drawer were several hundred aging pieces of paper of various original and copied deeds, mortgages, subdivisions, and conveyances relating to my house and adjacent properties. With the benefit of my legal training and a fair amount of fortitude, I was able to more or less work out the chain of title from the original area Bergen-Dutch farmers to the Victorian-era builder of my house, a later subdivision of the surrounding property, and over a hundred years of transfers of ownership.


American land records are commonly recorded in this manner and kept by a combination of local, state, and federal government agencies. Many municipalities and counties still rely on fragmented, paper-based systems that are more than just inconvenient. They are hard to navigate, expensive, slow, and vulnerable to mistakes and fraud. My paper records excursion is a too common experience for ascertaining real estate title history in municipalities and counties across the United States.


Right now, Bergen County, New Jersey is advancing into the digital future by embarking on the largest blockchain-based land record management project ever launched in the United States.3 Working with Balcony, a blockchain-technology firm,4 Bergen County will digitize more than 370,000 property deeds, representing around $240 billion in real estate value, and migrate those records onto the Avalanche blockchain platform.5 The project will cover all municipalities in Bergen County and serve nearly one million county residents.


Turning property records into digital tokens on a blockchain offers multiple advantages, including enhanced security, greater accessibility, and reduced processing times by as much as 90%.6 To improve security, Balcony’s use of the Avalanche blockchain puts land records into an immutable digital ledger, rendering them secure and verifiable. This is a critical improvement over decades-old municipal database systems frequently plagued by cyber hacks.


Once the tokenized system is implemented, deed settlement time will drop from 90 days to just a few days, dramatically streamlining real estate transactions. Moreover, digitized records will become instantly searchable by both property owners and municipal officials. Tamper-proof transactions will be more trustworthy. “This initiative is about improving the lives of our residents,” said John Hogan, county clerk of Bergen County. “By digitizing property records, we are making the process simpler, faster, and more secure for homeowners, businesses, and future generations.7


From the perspective of government, tokenization of land records is also expected to improve municipal revenue collection. In one New Jersey municipality, the platform uncovered nearly $1,000,000 in tax income that had been buried in outdated and incomplete records.8 With on-chain tokenized land records, hundreds of New Jersey towns and tens of thousands of municipalities in other states will be able to identify overlooked taxable property and eventually collect the full amount of revenue to which they are entitled under law.


By leveraging blockchain technology, local governments and their citizens can enjoy more efficient and reliable systems for recording and managing land assets. Bergen County and New Jersey may be just the start of a much larger shift to on-chain tokenized land records and the certainty and efficiency of an asset class recorded on some of the most antiquated systems still in existence. Bergen County’s land tokenized land records initiative demonstrates that distributed ledger technology and crypto are not just simply the new architecture of finance, but an innovation that unlocks billions in lost value while enhancing financial sovereignty and, potentially, individual liberty.







## Predicting the Future



In March 2021, 20 months after completing my term at the CFTC, I was introduced to Shayne Coplan, the founder of Polymarket, a blockchain-based prediction market platform.9 I was familiar with prediction markets from my earlier interactions with Joey Krug, the brilliant cofounder of Augur, the pioneering decentralized event contract market built on the Ethereum blockchain.10 I knew that prediction markets were not new; they were common throughout history,11 including predicting future Popes during papal conclaves in Renaissance Italy, estimating the number of downed German bombers from underground rail terminals in blitz-torn London, and forecasting presidential election outcomes reported daily in The New York Times in the run-ups to US presidential elections in the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries.12


Shayne and I spent several hours on the phone one early spring afternoon as my wife and I drove up Maryland’s Route 301 after a weekend in St. Michael’s. I was impressed with his exuberant intelligence and sponge-like absorption of information and advice. I was also taken by his steely determination and courage, two character traits that are essential for successful entrepreneurs. Shayne Coplan would be tested on both qualities in the next few years.


Twenty-three years old at the time, Shayne shared his enthusiasm for market-based finance. “I believe in markets,” he said as we shared admiration for the work of Friedrich Hayek, the classically liberal economist, whose Nobel prize-winning work recognized that market prices communicate information essential to satisfy human needs and aspirations.13 We talked about Polymarket and its decentralized network business model. Shayne drew the distinction between journalism reporting on what has happened and prediction markets predicting what will happen. He also drew a distinction between public opinion polls that offered no incentive to be right and prediction markets that penalized participants for being wrong. Shayne added that polling hardly reflected “the wisdom of the crowds,” since the average sample size for most polls was between 500 and 1,500 people. He pointed out that many of Polymarket’s top event contracts had tens to hundreds of thousands of participants. Unlike public opinion polls, participants were drawn to prediction markets because they could benefit by being right about the outcome of future events. The benefit for the public at large was the information derived from a marketplace of ideas—information about what would happen in the future, often important information that affected human life.


Shayne briefed me on his then-recent engagement with the CFTC’s Division of Enforcement. My former agency was asserting that Polymarket had offered off-exchange, binary options contracts relating to common political and social events without being appropriately licensed by the CFTC as a “Designated Contract Market” or DCM, a type of CFTC-regulated trading exchange.14 The upshot is that without a CFTC license, Polymarket was not allowed to take on US clients.


Shayne explained how the business he had started the year before had simply grown too fast, and he had been too naïve to engage properly with regulators. My advice was simple: “The only way out is through.” I encouraged Shayne to fully cooperate with the CFTC and reach a settlement. Meanwhile, I urged him to become an expert in the agency’s rules and regulations.


Shayne learned fast and, sure enough, 10 months later, Polymarket settled with the CFTC for a modest penalty and an agreement not to offer its services to US persons until it obtained the necessary licenses.15







## How It Works



Polymarket is a decentralized, peer-to-peer prediction market where users can compete with each other on all sorts of future events such as “Will the Federal Reserve chairman announce an interest rate cut in June?”, “Will Russia and Ukraine agree upon a ceasefire in 2026?” and “Will the Reform Party will win control of the British House of Commons?”


Each question is its own market on Polymarket. Users can buy “yes” or “no” shares priced between $0.01 and $1.00. Let’s say that the question is “Will Democrats win a majority of seats in Congress in November 2026?” and the share price is 60¢ that they will win and 40¢ that they will not. That share price reflects the market’s collective belief in that outcome.


Let’s also say that you think that the market is undervaluing the chance of Democrats’ victory and you buy 500 contracts to win at a cost of $300 (500 × $0.60). Now, let’s say that the Democrats do indeed win the election. In that case, each share you purchased becomes worth $1.00, and you will have earned $200 ($500 minus your original $300 investment). If instead, you had bought 500 contracts that the Democrats would not obtain a majority, you would have lost your $300 investment. Now, let’s say that sometime before the election is decided, the odds of Democrats winning go up from $0.60 to $0.80. You can sell your 500 contracts for a total of $400 (500 × $0.80) and lock in $100 profit on your original $300 investment.


Polymarket operates hundreds of marketplaces (hence, the name “Polymarket”) where buyers and sellers meet to agree or disagree on market-established prices. In this way, Polymarket operates a business similar to the business that I helped build on Wall Street 20 years before: a marketplace for market participants where our platform served only as a neutral intermediary, not a market participant itself.


Polymarket is said to be a “money-Lego,” in that its open architecture is freely available to developers who want to build on top of it.16 In this regard, Polymarket is more than a trading platform; it is an infrastructure layer for others to build their own prediction markets. Polymarket is highly decentralized and built upon blockchain technology that is highly transparent. According to Shayne, “You can see the activity, you can see the top holders, you can see the order book and liquidity and the price trending over time. You don’t need to trust me.”17 With Polymarket, you also don’t need to trust the casino or a bookie.


Polymarket’s participants interact with their own contracts, providing them with full control of their funds at all times. Because it is a blockchain system, Polymarket uses the USDC stablecoin as transaction currency. Independent third parties also supply funds to Polymarket’s prediction markets through automated “market makers”18 to foster smooth, liquid trading in a similar manner to how traditional exchanges routinely use market makers to provide consistent and adequate trading liquidity. Odds on Polymarket adjust in real time based on changing market sentiment and a decentralized means of resolution—all without intermediaries. Markets resolve once the outcome is verifiably known, such as following the certification of a political election. Verification data are provided by data pools called “oracles” that provide external data to the Polymarket network. Polymarket’s decentralized settlement process has seen its share of disputes and controversy,19 which Polymarket is working to improve.20


Participants on the Polymarket platform pay a modest trading fee, which goes to the third-party liquidity providers and not Polymarket (though there are certain blockchain transaction costs that are passed through to customers in these fees). Polymarket earns revenue by selling its prediction odds to newsfeeds and market participants, including through an exclusive partnership with Dow Jones, the publisher behind prominent financial news outlets like The Wall Street Journal and MarketWatch. This collaboration, announced in January 2026, aims to integrate Polymarket’s real-time prediction market data into Dow Jones’ platforms. Other partnerships are in place with CNN, Major League Soccer, the National Hockey League, the Ultimate Fighting Championship, and, in March 2026, exclusively with Major League Baseball.21 In 2025, Polymarket became X’s (Twitter) official prediction market partner22 and was named one of Time Magazine’s “100 Most Influential Companies.”23







## Weather Forecast



What makes Polymarket so remarkable is the accuracy with which it gauges public sentiment about future outcomes.24 Research conducted by data scientist Alex McCullough noted that Polymarket is around 90% accurate in predictions made a month in advance and can achieve 94% accuracy just hours before an event.25 This is notably higher than the average performance of opinion polling, which often struggles with accuracy on speculative or complex subjects.


This forecasting accuracy comes by drawing on the “wisdom of the crowd.”26 That is, by incentivizing the myriad of Polymarket participants who have voluntarily entered a market and put up real money (in the form of stablecoins) to make sure their opinion is correct. This accuracy contrasts with social media, where people are rewarded for being extreme and wrong with little accountability. With Polymarket and other decentralized prediction markets, on the other hand, people are rewarded for being right and punished for being wrong. Prediction markets also contrast with public opinion polling, which too often is a collection of random opinions from somewhat involuntary sources with little “skin in the game” as to the subject matter.27 Respondents to traditional telephone polls have no disincentive to tell pollsters what they “want” to happen rather than what they “think” will happen on any given question. With Polymarket, however, participants have something to lose if they take a position on what they “want” to happen rather than what they truly believe will happen.


The year 2024 was known as the “year of elections,” in which more people around the world went to the polls than in any other year in recent memory. In total, 78 countries and territories held elections and referenda on 110 separate election days.28 In that year, Polymarket’s decentralized prediction markets were remarkably accurate, often more so than public opinion polling.29 In Canadian politics, Polymarket showed that Liberal Party leader Mark Carney had a stronger lead over Conservative leader Pierre Poilievre compared to traditional polling aggregators.30


In the United States, many polls in late October and early November gave Kamala Harris a narrow edge of 1–4 percentage points over Trump nationally. The final major forecasts, including FiveThirtyEight, Nate Silver, and the New York Times/Siena poll, showed Harris either slightly ahead or tied with Trump.31 Yet, Trump won the electoral college 312–226, and the popular vote by roughly two and a half million votes, not quite what the headline polls would have anticipated.32 The odds on Polymarket, however, were closer to the mark. In the weeks leading up to the election, the Polymarket odds of a Trump victory were greater than 50/50. They grew steadily throughout election day, and by midnight, they indicated a 97% chance of his winning, well ahead of traditional news media like CNN and The New York Times that delayed calls on key swing states.33 While mainstream news networks continued their advertiser-sponsored broadcasts without proclaiming a winner, Polymarket had effectively declared the race and accurately so.34 The accuracy of its forecast rested on the “skin in the game” of $3.6 billion in election trades on the Polymarket platform.


I think about the social utility of prediction markets the way I think about weather forecasts. One of the most trusted news sources is the Weather Channel, not only for me but for millions of viewers. It has an enormous audience, often putting it in the top rankings for most-watched programming, especially during severe weather events. The reason the Weather Channel is so popular is that it provides some degree of predictability about a category of external events—the weather—that can have an impact on daily human life.


Yet, think for a moment of all the other categories of external events that can have enormous, longer-term impacts on human life for which there is little accurate predictability: economic conditions, political outcomes, environmental impacts, social events, and so forth. For example, changes in mortgage rates may have an impact on whether to buy or sell a home, the impact of a new virus may impact hospital crowding, expected gasoline prices may affect summer vacation plans, and changes in public policy may affect the decision of what career to follow or job to pursue. All these external events have been mostly unpredictable throughout most of human history, until now. Today, the predictability of real world event has been made far greater through the advent of prediction markets.


I predict that event prediction contract markets will soon be as popular as the Weather Channel and for the same reason: their accuracy. Prediction markets have been shown to have significantly greater accuracy than traditional news media. That is not to discredit journalism, which is meant to report on the results of events, not predict them—to say what happened, not what is likely to happen. Rather, prediction markets provide a complement to less dynamic media coverage and public opinion polling, especially at a time when faith in traditional media is dismal.35 Prediction markets’ wide user bases—their ability to draw on the wisdom of crowds—allow them to move nimbly without the influence of advertiser-driven, network narratives, and too frequent media bias, prioritizing outcome accuracy over extended dramatization and limited sample public opinion. Tens of millions of Americans—over a third of the population—already use prediction markets—whether to check forecasts or trade—and they want them regulated but not banned.36 There is no turning back. Prediction markets will be the weather report for everything other than the weather.


According to one observer, “What makes [prediction] markets so powerful is that they are a mechanism for combining trillions of points of localized, ever-changing knowledge and data, and producing a single actionable price. … Friedrich Hayek explained that the price system is the marvel that allows industrial society to function. “Man has been able to develop that division of labor on which our civilization is based because he happened to stumble upon a method”—the price system—“which made it possible,” Hayek observed. By expanding prices to predictions, many economists believe we can open a new way of planning for the future.”37 It is certainly Shayne’s vision.







## Room for Improvement



Polymarket and its competitors in the emerging event contract industry are not without their challenges and challengers. Critics cite the potential for market manipulation, including “wash trading,” where individuals buy and sell the same contracts to create a false impression of activity.38 Others cite the impact of large positions that suddenly move odds in one or other directions39 and gaps in liquidity that weaken price signals and could facilitate manipulation and restrict the opportunity for effective hedging.40 Some cite the limitations of Polymarket’s decentralized “oracle”-based outcome resolution mechanism.41 Another concern is that “the same structural features that make these markets efficient prediction tools also make them vulnerable … to the transmission of sensitive signals to foreign adversaries.”42


None other than Congresswoman Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez criticizes prediction markets for their potential to promote gambling, which can lead to addiction, debt, and social issues.43 The criticism is fair. Yet, the congresswoman does not appear to have similarly criticized sports books that not only actively promote gambling through ubiquitous broadcast and social media campaigns, but take large percentages of participants’ winnings and ban them when they win too much.44 While AOC’s concern for addiction is admirable and valid, the gambling genie seems to be well out of the bottle in American society, at least since the 2018 US Supreme Court ruling legalizing interstate gambling that I will discuss shortly. I am not sure that American society now wants to ban it.


Another concern is alleged insider trading, especially in light of highly profitable last-minute bets before recent announcements of the 2025 winner of the Nobel Peace Prize and Papal Conclave and the early 2026 capture and extraction of Nicholas Maduro from Venezuela. The CFTC has enforcement authority to investigate and prosecute violations of the Commodity Exchange Act related to insider trading based on “misappropriation of confidential information in breach of a pre-existing duty of trust and confidence to the source of the information.”45


The CFTC uses a variety of tools to combat insider trading, including market surveillance programs that monitor trading activities for signs of manipulation and fraud. Among those tools are collaborations with major US sports franchises, including Major League Baseball and the national football, hockey and basketball leagues, to obtain data on suspicious activities and potential market manipulation in professional sports. Other tools include enforcement of existing laws and regulations. Using classified information as the basis for taking positions on prediction markets is already against the law and fully punishable, as confirmed by the US Justice Department’s April 2026 indictment of a soldier who allegedly profited from the capture of Venezuelan dictator Nicolás Maduro.46 Tampering with an airport weather sensor to make a prediction on daily high temperature is already illegal, as a French criminal complaint recently made clear.47 Enforcing existing law is essential before considering new restrictions on the emerging field of prediction markets.


In fact, the first line of defense in preventing insider trading is the licensed prediction markets themselves that are responsible for making sure that the markets they offer are not readily susceptible to manipulation and are not, in fact, manipulated. Polymarket reports that it has referred over 90 accounts to law enforcement and regularly cooperates with relevant authorities in ongoing investigations, keeping details confidential while investigations are open.48 In early 2026, Polymarket and Kalshi each introduced enhanced measures to curb insider trading,49 including deployment of Solidus Labs’ HALO platform event contract surveillance infrastructure that provides machine-learning sentiment analysis across Reddit, X, Telegram, and news sources, elastic scalability to absorb sudden spikes around elections, economic releases, court rulings, and sports finals, and real-time alerting across thousands of outcome-based markets.50


Interestingly, suspicious last-minute bets on global events are not limited to prediction markets but continue to take place in traditional futures markets like Brent crude oil futures,51 proving that misbehavior takes place through both old and new technologies. Clearly, the work of regulatory enforcement is never done, regardless of financial innovation.


Certainly, the heat is on prediction markets to justify whether “the Hayekian benefits of centralized pricing justify the social costs (if any) of gambling of this sort.”52 I disagree with assertions that prediction markets have outpaced the legal frameworks designed to govern them.53 Rather, I expect that many of these concerns about prediction markets will be addressed and resolved as the industry grows and evolves into a fully-fledged and dependable network of decentralized prediction platforms. Polymarket demonstrates that distributed ledger technology provides a new and promising means to garner the opinion of wide swathes of the global population to predict the outcome of future events. Decentralized events contract markets can augment the sometimes-biased narratives and limited sampling of traditional media and pollsters. In a world where elections, financial conditions, and global events have consequences, the forecasting accuracy of decentralized prediction markets becomes increasingly essential to commercial risk mitigation and investment decision-making. In the words of the current CFTC chairman, “We’re seeing trust in centralized gatekeepers being replaced with trust in decentralization and market systems,” as a result of widespread debanking and misreporting in the news. “It’s essential that we create a regulatory framework that does not quash this trust revolution.”54







## Wake Up Call



Polymarket’s remarkable success in calling the US elections led to events that would severely test Shayne’s innate determination and courage, which I discerned in him when we first met. A week after Polymarket first declared Donald Trump the winner of the 2024 Presidential election, FBI agents conducted a predawn raid on Shayne’s Manhattan apartment, shaking him out of a sound sleep and seizing his electronic devices. Yet, Shayne was not charged with any crime.55 I spoke to him shortly afterward. He was still in shock from the FBI’s crack-of-dawn raid of his apartment, but was determined to stay on course. He later tweeted: “It’s discouraging that the current administration would seek a last-ditch effort to go after companies they deem to be associated with political opponents.” And “we are deeply committed to being non-partisan, and today is no different, but the incumbents should do some self-reflecting and recognize that taking a more pro-business, pro-startup approach may be what would have changed their fate this election.”56


Neither the FBI nor the Biden Justice Department provided any information as to what precipitated the raid, though ever-ready “anonymous sources” alleged that it was driven by a CFTC criminal referral that US persons had successfully traded on Polymarket in violation of Polymarket’s 2022 CFTC settlement. If so, then the FBI’s involvement marks an attempted criminalization of alleged lapses in otherwise ordinary regulatory compliance that would otherwise routinely be resolved in an administrative proceeding. I would hate to think that senior staff at the CFTC would have made a criminal referral to federal law enforcement for such alleged civil rule violations. It would be infinitely worse if a criminal referral were driven through the agency by confederates of Polymarket’s commercial competitors. It would be unprecedented and unacceptable if the raid was driven by the Biden White House to punish Polymarket for its accuracy in picking the presidential winner.


Remarkably, eight months later, Polymarket received formal notice from the Trump Department of Justice and the CFTC that they were ending any investigation.57 Nevermind!


Responding to the news that the FBI had dropped its investigation, Coinbase’s CEO, Brian Armstrong, tweeted:



This was one of the most egregious examples of lawfare from the last administration, that should never have been possible in America. Imagine having your door broken down for predicting an election. The onus was on the government to prove there was something worth pursuing here, and they failed to do that. This is how you lose trust in institutions.58




Later that same year, Polymarket acquired QCEX, a CFTC-licensed derivatives exchange and clearinghouse.59 Not long after, the CFTC approved Polymarket to relaunch in the United States.60


On July 2, 2025, I spoke on the phone with Jeff Sprecher, chairman of the New York Stock Exchange and founder, chairman, and CEO of its parent company, the Intercontinental Exchange (ICE). Sprecher and I have known each other in business for over 20 years. He asked me to introduce him to Shayne Coplan. I called Shayne right away and told him to drop everything he was doing and meet with Sprecher. I said, “You need a big brother, and Jeff might be it. He can change your life.” Shayne followed up immediately.


Three months later, on October 7, Jeff Sprecher and ICE announced an investment of $2 billion in Polymarket,61 valuing the company at over $8 billion. Less than a year after the FBI bashed down his door, Shayne Coplan was the world’s youngest billionaire!62



Left to right: Jeff Sprecher (ICE), the author and Shayne Coplan (Polymarket)



Sports Talk: Regulation


In 1992, Congress passed a law called the Professional and Amateur Sports Protection Act (PASPA) that prohibited state-sanctioned sports gambling. Twenty years later, following a New Jersey state referendum in favor of sports gambling (alas, I voted “no”), the state legislature enacted a law that allowed sports wagering at casinos and racetracks. That law went through a series of challenges from professional sports leagues contending that the state violated the PASPA. New Jersey’s then-Governor Chris Christie appealed a Federal Circuit court decision in favor of the federal ban to the US Supreme Court, which reached a decision under his gubernatorial successor, Phil Murphy. The Supreme Court 2018 decision, Murphy v. National Collegiate Athletic Association, ruled that the federal law prohibiting states from authorizing sports gambling was unconstitutional. The Murphy decision allowed states to take control of their own gambling laws and to legalize sports betting.


Prior to the Murphy decision, sports gambling was localized and mostly unseen in the United States. Since the decision, you cannot watch a sports event without ads for sports betting flooding the screen. In just a short while, sports betting has become a huge business, producing enormous profits and generating large state and local tax revenues. In 2025, nearly $150 billion was wagered through industry operators, up nearly 25% compared to 2023.63 Sports betting in contemporary America is both ubiquitous and socially acceptable. From a public policy perspective, it seems to be just a matter of who gets to regulate and tax it.


And sure enough, with the explosion in Polymarket’s events contracts, as well as those offered by popular competing platforms like Kalshi, PredictIt, Crypto.com, and others, a complex and evolving legal battle is underway. The dispute is over whether prediction market contracts should be regulated under federal law by the CFTC or fall under state gambling regulations. This question has produced sharply conflicting judicial outcomes across multiple federal and state courts, aggressive state enforcement actions, a landmark CFTC amicus brief asserting federal supremacy, comprehensive new rulemaking initiatives, and legislative proposals that could fundamentally reshape the regulatory landscape. More than 20 federal lawsuits are currently pending, with states like Ohio and Tennessee, among others, taking active measures.64 Recently, a federal judge in Tennessee ruled that Kalshi operates within federal jurisdiction, allowing it to continue while the case proceeds.65 Conversely, a judge in Ohio stated that Kalshi must comply with state gambling regulations.66 This inconsistency highlights the differing interpretations of what constitutes gambling versus a financial market.







## Sports Talk: Horses for Courses



Congressional hearings will be held, and entire law treatises could and may well be written analyzing CFTC statutory authority, federal preemption, state gaming law, and the current national litigation landscape. I will not try to summarize those here. However, in April 2026, I was again called to Capitol Hill to appear before the US House Agriculture Committee for a roundtable discussion on the current state of prediction markets.


In my testimony, I told the Committee that I thought of my role in public service as one of finding solutions, not animating controversy. I wanted to help the Committee find middle ground in the prediction market debate. I said that, in my view, much of the current debate is flawed by trying to distinguish federal from state jurisdiction according to the underlying subject matter of the event contracts, such as sports, current events, politics, entertainment, or economics. That is like trying to cut a seven-layer cake sideways by the layer. The right distinction is from the top to the bottom by determining whether event contracts are offered to customers by retail establishments or traded by participants in marketplaces.


In traditional gambling, the casino, bookie, or “house” is the counterparty to each trade. The house sets the odds and the price, on which it has a monopoly under license by state, municipality, or tribal authority. Every player, customer, or gambler is a price “taker,” who can take or leave the price, but cannot set it. It makes sense that these casino-like operations should be regulated at the state level, since it is the casino itself—often a local operation serving area demand—that must be overseen to ensure it treats its customers fairly and forthrightly. In these circumstances, the casino or “house” is akin to a local retail merchant subject to appropriate local, state, or tribal licensure and regulatory compliance.


In event contract markets, however, participants are not wagering against the “house,” and there is no casino to set the odds and take a spread. Rather, there is a trading platform that merely puts forth a yes/no proposition. The platform leaves it to its participants to set the price through their bids and offers. Participants in event contract prediction markets are both price “makers” and “takers.” They contest against each other, not against the house that does not earn a spread or monetize its sportsbook. Thus, prediction platforms are marketplaces, not casinos. It is their functioning as global marketplaces that makes event contract markets’ predictive capability so remarkably effective.


Federal law unequivocally designates derivative contract marketplaces operating across state lines under exclusive CFTC jurisdiction. The Commodity Exchange Act grants the CFTC “exclusive jurisdiction … with respect to accounts, agreements … and transactions involving swaps or contracts of sale of a commodity for future delivery … traded or executed on a contract market ….” [emphasis added] Clearly, Congress meant to put the CFTC in charge of marketplaces.







## Cutting the Cake



Undoubtedly, US states, municipalities, and tribal authorities are highly capable of regulating gambling establishments. They provide specialized oversight to ensure the integrity, honesty, and financial stability of the casino industry by licensing operators, enforcing compliance, and managing technical standards for gambling within their borders. There is also no doubt that the CFTC is uniquely competent to oversee national and global markets for event prediction contract markets. CFTC-regulated trading exchanges and clearinghouses are among the most robust and resilient in the world. The one piece of Sam Bankman-Fried’s global house of cards that did not fail its customers was the piece overseen by the CFTC. Federal regulation of national marketplaces is consistent with public policy that recognizes that no state has the capacity to oversee and police nationwide trading activity. Only the CFTC has the surveillance tools and data collection capability to police interstate markets for fraud and manipulation.


Some litigants have questioned the CFTC’s operational capability to regulate event contract prediction markets.67 Derivative contract markets overseen by the CFTC are measured by “open interest,” that is, the number of active, unsettled contracts. Traditional futures markets, such as those for US Treasuries, WTI crude oil, gold, and Eurodollars, are global giants where open interest is measured in the tens of millions of contracts, representing trillions of dollars in notional value.68 The open interest of event contract prediction markets, on the other hand, is minuscule compared to traditional futures markets: 10,000 to 100,000 times smaller.69 With the capability to oversee the world’s largest financial marketplaces, the CFTC certainly has the capability to oversee unleveraged prediction markets that are a minor addition to its world-leading regulatory activity.


The numerous current lawsuits involving states and tribes likely will resolve in time with rulings in which applicable state, municipal, and tribal regulations are applied to traditional casinos, and federal regulation is confirmed for interstate event prediction contract markets. It is the only outcome that makes sense. It will reflect both the existing law and sound public policy because a patchwork of states, municipalities, and tribes are not well-positioned to regulate interstate markets and the CFTC has no ability or desire to regulate gambling houses, sportsbooks, and casinos. Yet, together, we all have an interest in regulating prediction markets with the right slice of the cake—giving the right job to the right regulator.


Such a resolution seems akin to the emergence of ride-sharing provided by Uber and Lyft. That innovation had to overcome disconnected yet vigorous opposition from hundreds of state, county, and municipal taxi and limousine commissions. Ride-sharing eventually succeeded because the American public liked and demanded the service. Yet, ride-sharing did not displace ride-hailing, but rather became a complement to it. I suspect the same will happen with event prediction contract markets that will eventually be found to be under federal CFTC supervision. Yet, casino-like gambling will remain as a complement to event contracts under the careful and proven supervision of local authorities.


In fact, the CFTC continues to step up to its regulatory authority over prediction markets. On Thursday, March 12, 2026, it announced two critical steps. It first issued instructions to registered exchanges on compliance and product listing requirements for event contracts.70 It also published an advance notice of proposed rulemaking inviting public comment on whether it needs to write new rules or amend existing ones for prediction market oversight.71 Taken together, these agency actions are a prompt for the industry and the public to provide input into how prediction products and the platforms on which they are transacted are classified, reviewed, registered, overseen, and regulated. The CFTC is readying its aim before firing, doing things in the right order. According to the current CFTC chairman, “Prediction markets are here to stay.”72


Let’s hope so. In March 2026, Democratic senators Jeff Merkley and Elizabeth Warren and Representative Jamie Raskin introduced legislation to ban prediction market bets on sports, war, elections, and government actions.73 Perhaps a ban on warfare makes sense. But a ban on the outcomes of elections and government activity seems self-serving. It is not surprising that some politicians do not want to have the wisdom of the crowds judge their governmental actions. They would not want to allow consumers of prediction market data to bypass the news filter of the traditional news media. They would not want the unvarnished truth of political opinion down in the village “square” to be discoverable and challenge the mighty power of the “tower.”


Ultimately, it is not about sports or even gambling. It’s about discovering truth, something society deserves more of and not less. It is also not a question of whether prediction markets survive—they are too innovative and accurately predictive not to. The real question is whether they enable professional and ultimately corporate participants to hedge real-world risk in orderly and robust markets. To do that, the current cadre of iconoclast founders needs to mature into seasoned and responsible operators. That’s a tough transition in any industry—many intrepid founders create valuable enterprises only to surrender control to leaders who expand the franchise to serve professional markets. That remains to be seen for this cadre of founders, Shayne included. One test will be whether he and the other prediction market founders, including the leadership of Kalshi can put aside their personal rivalry and cooperate in setting and adhering to industry standards and best practices that protect consumers and the public. There is still some way to go.74 If the current cadre of prediction markets entrepreneurs cannot make the leap, seasoned financial service leaders will eventually step in and steer prediction innovation through the storms of public opinion in the direction of broad adoption.
