# Chapter 5 Taking a Stand




An account of the author’s work in building one of the first digital asset practices at a “Big Law” firm, engagement with the fractious crypto industry, and his controversial 2020 law review argument that the crypto XRP was not subject to SEC jurisdiction, the subsequent SEC lawsuit challenging that position, and the ultimate vindication in federal court




As I recounted in my earlier book, I left the CFTC without much of a plan for what I would do next. Press stories ran that I might replace Mark Carney (current Canadian prime minister) as governor of the Bank of England.1 I was approached but declined to be considered to run the Financial Conduct Authority, the UK’s primary regulator of financial services firms and markets. After five years toiling in Washington, DC, on a government salary, I was ready to return to the fast-paced world of business and finance back in New York City, where I had built my career.


More than anything, I wanted to focus my energies on furthering digital asset innovation. Believing strongly that distributed ledger technology is the future architecture of finance, I wanted to help build it. I wanted to act as both private investor and professional advisor by serving in various capacities as outside legal counsel, strategic advisor, and non-executive director to a range of businesses engaged in digital asset transformation. I also embarked on an active writing spree, not only with my first book, but in dozens of op-eds and articles with many fine coauthors, as well as an active schedule of conference and podcast appearances. Then, of course, I would continue work with the Digital Dollar Project, which, as I will explain later, is both an endeavor and a passion.





## Legal Eagle



First, I needed to find a place to hang my hat. As I explained in CryptoDad, returning to the practice of law with the esteemed law firm of Willkie Farr & Gallagher has been one of the most pleasant surprises since leaving Washington, DC.2 Founded in 1888, Willkie’s practice is exceptionally strong in the intersection of financial markets, law, and technology, in which I have worked over the course of four decades.


At Willkie, I set out to build and lead an expert digital asset practice dubbed Willkie Digital Works.3 I helped recruit some remarkable talent, from breezy but brilliant Kristy Littman, former chief of the Crypto Assets and Cyber Unit in the Enforcement Division of the US Securities and Exchange Commission, to Kari Larsen, one of crypto’s true legal pioneers.4 I brought on board two of my former CFTC law clerks, Mike Selig and Chelsea Pizzola, each of whom had developed into a leading expert in digital asset law and regulation (more about each of them later). As of this writing, the number of Willkie lawyers working with the Digital Works practice is several dozen and spread out across the United States and Europe, advising some of the most successful builders and investors in crypto innovation. Although I recently retired from Willkie after six years at the firm, I trust that the Digital Works practice will serve as a foundation for the second century of Willkie’s service to international commerce and the global economy.


From my work and advocacy, there is one feature of the emerging crypto industry that disappointingly speaks to its youth and lack of sophistication: the industry’s fractious partisanship. Crypto seems to constantly divide itself into chauvinist fights, pitting “true believers” of one L1, stablecoin sponsor, or prediction market against another, all championed by five separate trade associations, each with unique backers and supporters. This is not the way that mature industries operate. In my experience, the banking and derivatives trading industries came together before and after passage of the Dodd-Frank Act to hammer out their common interests and then speak to policymakers in a mostly unified fashion. The crypto industry, on the other hand, approaches policymakers in a disjointed fashion. Industry players, especially in the case of prediction market platforms and their champions, too often use discussions with policymakers to call out other industry players for regulatory censure, hoping to use regulatory action as a competitive advantage. This is not the means to achieve good overall policy. It is time for the crypto industry, including stablecoin issuers and prediction market platforms, to mature and work together to address its common policy interests.







## Ripple in Still Water



Early in 2020, Willkie Digital Works was retained by Ripple, a company that provides crypto and blockchain solutions to business enterprises to process financial transactions in a wide range of fiat currencies and cryptocurrencies. Ripple uses the open-sourced blockchain, the XRP Ledger described earlier. The digital asset, XRP, is the native crypto token of the XRP Ledger. Ripple sometimes also licenses use of its proprietary RippleNet network to its customers as an efficient payment mechanism for payment network transactions.


After research and consideration, I concluded that XRP should not be classified as a security under applicable law, but as a payment token, akin in function to a currency. In June 2020, my Willkie Digital Works colleague, Conrad Bahlke, and I published that view in an international law review.5 Our argument was generally well-received and widely covered.6 I was confident that view would be sustained in time.


Six months later, in December 2020, in the waning days of the first Trump administration, to my surprise and dismay, the SEC sued Ripple and two of its senior executives, Brad Garlinghouse and cofounder Chris Larsen, accusing them of unlawfully offering XRP as an unregistered security.7 This legal battle was then taken up by the Biden administration and its newly appointed SEC chairman, Gary Gensler. Over the next several years, it became one of the most closely watched cases in the cryptosphere, an aggressive campaign by the SEC to bring digital assets to heel. It symbolized Gary Gensler’s quite audacious attempt to get the federal courts, rather than Congress, to rule that XRP and virtually every digital token were securities under the SEC’s exclusive authority.


The SEC’s legal theory rested on a seminal 1946 Supreme Court decision, SEC v. W.J. Howey Co.,8 that established the scope of what constitutes a security subject to SEC jurisdiction. Under the Howey decision, an investment contract exists and is subject to US securities laws if there is an “investment of money in a common enterprise with an expectation of profits to be derived from the efforts of others.”9


Over the next three and a half years, the SEC and Ripple fought it out in the Federal District Court in New York City. The SEC argued that not only was the XRP token itself a security, but all sales of XRP, including on public exchanges, were unregistered securities transactions. Ripple, on the other hand, argued that XRP, by and of itself, did not meet the requirements of a security under the Howey test, since XRP is not primarily intended for investment. One argument made by Ripple, among many others, was that XRP had a real-world use case: cross-border payments. This was the same position that I had taken in June 2020. The litigation attracted many “friend of the court” amicus briefs from crypto law experts and even industry rivals, expounding on the SEC’s assault on the idea that a digital asset itself could be “the embodiment” of a security and, therefore, provide the SEC with an almost unlimited legal berth to regulate every sale, even on secondary markets. Among the amici was John E. Deaton, the indomitable attorney and Massachusetts senatorial candidate who represented a group of XRP holders.10


On July 13, 2023, Federal Judge Analisa Torres ruled that “XRP, as a digital token, is not in and of itself a ‘contract, transaction or scheme’ that embodies the Howey requirements of an investment contract.”11 She determined that XRP was not a security when sold to the public on an exchange, but could be when sold to institutional investors based on contractual promises and obligations with those institutional investors and obligations and that that portion of the case could continue.12 The partial decision was a remarkable victory for Ripple, its principals, and the industry. It also confirmed our 2020 law review argument that cryptocurrencies and digital assets generally are not inherently securities subject to SEC jurisdiction unless they are transacted in a manner that otherwise comes under US securities laws.13


In October 2023, the SEC dismissed with prejudice its claims against Ripple’s CEO and co-founder but continued its action against the company.14 Both the SEC and Ripple eventually filed appeals in the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit. In a November 2024 appearance on Fox Business, I predicted that the SEC would ultimately drop the case.15 In May 2025, as predicted, after a four-and-a-half-year legal battle, the SEC reached a settlement with Ripple Labs and its principals.16 On August 7, 2025, the case was dismissed by the US Second Circuit Court of Appeals.17 In March 2026, the SEC formally and definitively declared that XRP was NOT a digital security under its jurisdiction.18 Thanks a lot!


In public remarks, Ripple General Counsel Stu Alderoty likened the legal saga between the SEC and Ripple to classic stories, saying the case has always felt like “Moby Dick with [SEC Chair Gary] Gensler starring as Captain Ahab,” but now feels more like “Moby Dick meets My Cousin Vinny.”19 He also kindly noted my unwavering support for Ripple’s legal position. “Five years ago, while many were rooting against us, even those who stood by us chose to do so silently. You were smart and brave enough to call it as you saw it. Thank you,” he said.20


For better or worse, calling it as I see it has been my approach to crypto (and life) from the start, especially with some of the more notorious characters in the crypto community.







## If You Fall, You Fall Alone



In June 2021, I headed down to Florida with a colleague to speak at that year’s Bitcoin Conference, one of the world’s largest gatherings of bitcoin supporters. With more than 12,000 conference attendees, Bitcoin 2021, held in the commercial Wynwood section of Miami, was raucous by day and across the bay on South Beach, wildly celebratory at night.21


One bright “Magic City” morning, I met a 20-something-year-old man at an agreed outdoor coffee spot just south of the Brickell Avenue Bridge. Sam Bankman-Fried had been introduced to me on a Zoom call a few weeks before. I was intrigued by the wiry, unshaven guy I greeted. Bankman-Fried wore cargo shorts and a T-shirt that must have been slept in not only the previous night but the entire week before. He said that he was in Miami to speak at Bitcoin 2021, but also to celebrate his $135 million renaming of the Miami Heat’s basketball arena to that of his burgeoning trading platform, FTX.


This would not be my last meeting with Sam Bankman-Fried or “Sam,” as he was known to people who worked with him and “SBF” to people who wanted to. Sam explained that he intended to relocate FTX out of Hong Kong to the Bahamas. Going forward, he would focus his attention on better serving a global customer base. He understood that FTX would need to be licensed by the CFTC and SEC and sought my advice in taking the necessary steps to offer customers a full suite of crypto trading products.


I told Sam about SEC Chairman Gary Gensler’s oft stated invitation for crypto firms to simply “come in and register.”22 This was long before the pattern was established that firms that accepted the dubious invitation discovered no path to registration, but instead often found themselves grilled for incriminating information and under scrutiny by the SEC’s enforcement staff.23 Over the next few months, SBF and his FTX team retained Willkie Digital Works and drew upon my services for US regulatory advice and, among other things, attend an October 2021 meeting with the SEC.


Later that summer, Sam asked me in my personal capacity to join his board of directors. At that time, Sam was a rising star, and FTX seemed to be headed to the moon. I asked a lot of questions and got a lot of somewhat compelling answers, with one exception. I asked about the relationship between FTX as the trading platform and Alameda, his affiliated proprietary trading firm. I asked how they interacted with each other since, under CFTC oversight, trading platforms were generally independent of their proprietary trading customers. I knew that GFI Group, which I helped build almost two decades before, would never enter its own trades against its clients on the trading platform it operated.


Sam’s reaction was both evasive and dismissive. He didn’t want to talk about it. I thought that he simply found my question to be too prying. I reckoned that my inquisitiveness as an independent board member would eventually get on his nerves. And his lack of transparency would annoy me. As we spoke further, my spider senses were elevated. I felt an instinctual aversion to getting further involved. Yet, I hardly suspected the level of double-dealing that would eventually come to light. A week or so later, I politely declined the seat on his board of directors. I never thought twice afterward about the decision.


The introductory meeting with Sam and SEC Chairman Gensler, which I attended by Zoom, took place in October 2021.24 It was the first of other meetings between SBF and the SEC Chairman,25 to none of which was I invited, nor did I attend. My professional engagement with SBF ceased at this point.


I did, however, continue to run into SBF at various crypto industry events and get-togethers. In April of the following year, I flew to Nassau in the Bahamas to attend and speak about US crypto regulation at “Crypto Bahamas 2022.” The event was billed as an “invitation-only event featuring collaboration and networking among leading players in the crypto and traditional finance industry,” featuring discussion and “unique experiences in the Bahamas.”26


In fact, Crypto Bahamas 2022 was a four-day coming-out party for SBF and his FTX platform. No expense was spared in putting on a great show with big celebrities (including American football star Tom Brady and his then-wife, Gisele Bundchen). For me, the highlight was sitting alongside “Mr. Wonderful” Kevin O’Leary in the audience of an enormous ballroom of the Baha Mar hotel and hearing Sam interview former British Prime Minister Tony Blair and former US President Bill Clinton. On an enormous stage in front of hundreds of attentive participants, Clinton and Blair sat side by side, dressed in suits and collared shirts befitting former heads of state.27 On a stool in front of them bobbed SBF in dirty Converse sneakers, limp white socks, a worn T-shirt, and his customary cargo shorts. Sam’s simple questions (“Um, what do you think of crypto?”) were not particularly penetrating and were easily answered by the two world leaders with cultured panache and gravitas. At one point, I caught sight of Clinton’s well-known squint as he studied SBF. I imagined that Clinton was thinking to himself, “This guy is worth $26 billion?28 You have got to be kidding me! I should have asked for more than a quarter of a million dollars to fly down here.”29 Sitting there, I could feel Bill Clinton’s pain.


I saw Sam again in October 2022 along with Kristy Littman at a luncheon hosted in a fancy Union Square restaurant by one of the large New York investment banks. He was his same twitchy self, pitching the prospects of his FTX platform and seeking new investors. Less than two weeks later, the whole thing unraveled spectacularly.30 FTX suffered a rapid liquidity crisis as account holders withdrew and closed trading accounts upon the news that the trading firm, Alameda Research, also owned by Bankman-Fried, was borrowing heavily from FTX to cover large trading losses, putting up as collateral a speculative cryptocurrency issued by FTX, called FTT.31 Both companies and most of their affiliates filed for bankruptcy. In December, the US government filed civil and criminal charges against SBF for misappropriating more than $8 billion in customer funds (exclusive of customer funds with FTX’s CFTC-regulated subsidiary that were safely segregated with third-party custodians under CFTC rules). In March 2024, thirty-two-year-old Sam Bankman-Fried was convicted on criminal charges and sentenced to 25 years in prison. I now knew there was a reason why he didn’t want to answer my questions two years before.


The fall of FTX caused enormous financial harm to its millions of customers and investors, with billions of dollars in deposits reportedly used to cover Alameda’s trading losses, driving a cascade of bankruptcy filings. Online libraries are now filled with all that has been written about the curious Sam Bankman-Fried and his sudden rise and spectacular fall amidst allegations of stealing, misusing, and misappropriating client money. They include popular magazine profiles and full-length books by no less than the best-selling author Michael Lewis.32 They recount in detail his disheveled appearance, jittery manner, and frenetic pace, all of which I also observed. What I recall the most about Sam, however, was his fundamental aloofness and distance. When Sam spoke, his mouth and body moved, but his eyes never did. They stared at the floor, the wall, the ceiling, but rarely at the person in front of him. I couldn’t tell whether Sam’s empty eyes betrayed superior intelligence, boredom, or maybe sadness. Yet, I feel sadness for Sam, for what happened and what should never have happened.


In a later chapter, I will explain how the implosion of SBF had an enormous and devastating impact on Congressional Democrats, the Biden administration’s crypto policies, and Kamala Harris’ electoral defeat.


But first, let’s talk about the prospects for a digital US Dollar and the importance of steering it in the direction of financial freedom.

# Chapter 6 The Digital Dollar Project




A review of the work of the Digital Dollar Project in posing three essential questions: how to “future-proof” the dollar for a world of competing digital networks; how to maintain the dollar’s world reserve currency status, and, perhaps most importantly, how to preserve the universal values of economic freedom for which the dollar has historically stood




In early spring 2024, some colleagues and I headed off to an important meeting in an innocuous office campus north of Washington, DC. The location was the National Counterterrorism Center, operated by the Office of the Director of National Intelligence. After passing through a series of security checkpoints, depositing our electronic devices in a locked cabinet, and undergoing individual electromagnetic screenings, we were escorted into a featureless conference room and seated at a long table under a large viewing screen. Our escort remained and was soon joined by a few others.


Our escort declared the meeting ready to start. The viewing screen came to life with a checkerboard of images of various unfamiliar faces. The overall scene reminded me of the television quiz show, Hollywood Squares, in which contestants compete in a game of tic-tac-toe to win cash and prizes by asking questions of Hollywood celebrities seated in a walled stack of three-by-three open-faced cubes. The contestants weighed the truth of the celebrities’ answers in order to claim the square and a chance to win the game or, in the game’s parlance, the “circle gets the square.”


Now, deep within the labyrinth of a US government national security center, the people in the boxes were not celebrities. Rather, they avoided any recognition and simply gave their names as “Fred,” “Rosemary,” “James D.,” and “James P.” Half of them did not even turn on their cameras, so the box was marked with a first initial. Only a few of them told us what branch of national security employed them, using acronyms such as NSA, CIA, DOD, and FBI. Unlike the TV program where the audience asked questions to those in the squares, here, after our short presentation, we were on the receiving end of questions about the work of the Digital Dollar Project. Now, I felt as if the truthfulness of our answers was under review!





## The Project



In CryptoDad, I described my cofounding of the Project,1 a privately funded, nonprofit, nonpartisan organization devoted to catalyzing research, exploration, and real-world experimentation of digital modernization of the US dollar. The Project operates a forum to consider how best to modernize the US dollar and preserve its reserve currency status and the values it represents in a coming world of both decentralized and centralized digital currency networks.


When it was founded in 2020, the Digital Dollar Project foresaw the coming twenty-first-century kaleidoscope of digital networks of money: some decentralized and others centralized, some sovereign and many commercial. Since then, the Project has consistently considered three straightforward but difficult questions: How should the dollar be “future-proofed” for the digitally networked twenty-first century? How can the dollar best maintain its status as the world’s reserve currency in the coming world of competing digital networks? And, perhaps most importantly, how can the universal values of economic freedom for which the dollar has historically stood be preserved in this radically different, digitally networked future?


Importantly, the Digital Dollar Project does not advocate for the deployment of a US Central Bank Digital Currency or “CBDC.” A CBDC is a digital form of fiat currency issued by a sovereign state or its central bank that is the liability of the central bank or sovereign state rather than a commercial bank or corporation. It is different than a stablecoin that may be backed by debt or other forms of sovereign money but is itself not a sovereign obligation. CBDCs, on the other hand, are themselves obligations of central banks or sovereign nations.


The project asserts that the United States should only consider issuing a CBDC if key design elements of privacy, security, distribution, and economic stability are properly understood and democratically addressed. Yet, the Project does encourage the United States and other free societies to assert principled leadership in digital currency experimentation at home and standard setting abroad that is consistent with civic norms and values. The Project believes that if the United States doesn’t set global standards for digitally networked money—sovereign and nonsovereign—China will.2


The Project serves as a research platform that takes a deep and thoughtful look at the topic of dollar digital modernization, from the good to the bad, with all relevant voices. The Digital Dollar Project provides a venue where thoughtful consideration can take place, away from the polarization of contemporary politics and social media. It is an effort in the proud tradition of public/private partnerships that was the vehicle to launch a free and fair internet3 that has spurred growth, opportunity, and insight of benefit to the whole world.


To ensure that expert and diverse voices and perspectives are represented, the Digital Dollar Project is supported by an extraordinary advisory group that includes a broad array of economists, business leaders, technologists, innovators, lawyers, academics, and consumer advocates.4 The members and their points of view cover the social and political spectrum.


Over the past five years, the Project’s engagement with both the public and private sectors has delved into critical topics of digital currency privacy, risk, security, cross-border payments, and interoperability. From hosting roundtables at leading educational institutions and sold-out conferences to publishing a series of risk and privacy working papers, the Project has been engaged in understanding and exploring the implications and necessary next steps for thinking about the future of money. The Project conducted the first-ever US wholesale CBDC pilot study with the Depository Trust and Clearing Corporation and the first-ever US retail CBDC pilot study with Western Union and BDO Unibank.5


In the United States, there are currently two types of central bank money: physical currency issued by the Federal Reserve and digital balances held by commercial banks at the Federal Reserve.6 Should it ever be deployed, a US CBDC would serve as a third format—a digitally networked format—of central bank money. Unlike stablecoins or tokenized bank deposits, a US CBDC would enjoy the full faith and credit of the US government.


There could be two kinds of US CBDC: a retail CBDC, a form of central bank digital currency that is accessible to the public and used domestically or in cross-border transactions ranging from purchases of goods and services to vendor payments and payroll, and a wholesale CBDC designed for use by financial institutions to conduct interbank payments, settle securities, and foreign exchange (FX) transactions.7


While contemporary Americans hold most of their money in bank accounts in digital form (as in digital notations on some bank’s internal computer ledgers), their money is not recorded on digital networks operating across the financial system. CBDCs differ from existing digital money because a CBDC would be a liability of the Federal Reserve, not of a commercial bank. In a financial crisis, it would be generally considered safer than commercial forms of money, including stablecoins.


US exploration of CBDC has been relatively limited, primarily involving tests and limited prototypes, such as Project Hamilton (testing the architecture of a wholesale CBDC)8 and Project Cedar (testing the use of wholesale CBDC)9 led since 2020 respectively by the Federal Reserve Banks of Boston and New York. In 2024, the Fed participated in Project Agorá (testing wholesale CBDC and tokenized deposits).10 However, an early 2025 Executive Order from the Trump White House prohibits US executive branch agencies from undertaking any action to establish, issue, or promote CBDCs, so any progress on these initiatives has halted.11


But Washington’s current antipathy to CBDCs will not stop their global development. The Atlantic Council regularly publishes statistics on the global adoption of various forms of central bank digital currency. As of this writing, 137 countries and currency unions, representing 98% of global GDP, are exploring CBDCs.12 In May 2020, that number was only 35. Today, three countries have fully launched a digital currency—the Bahamas, Jamaica, and Nigeria—all focused on expanding the reach of their CBDCs domestically.13 There are 49 CBDC pilot projects around the world.14 That includes 19 of the G-20, with China placing its digital yuan—the eCNY—in over 180 million digital wallets and used for 7 trillion yuan worth of transactions in its short life—an amount equivalent to almost $1 trillion in US dollars.15 The European Union is also in advanced development of its digital euro for deployment by the end of the decade.16


Whether or not the US deploys a sovereign digital dollar, American tourists will soon have foreign sovereign CBDCs in their digital wallets alongside crypto and stablecoins. American multinational corporations will accept foreign sovereign CBDCs for payments, reserving CBDCs in their corporate treasuries and remitting CBDCs in foreign taxes. Thus, global citizens soon will be dealing with CBDCs, stablecoins, and crypto. The sometimes fashionable debate between CBDCs, stablecoins, and crypto is a false choice. The twenty-first-century future will certainly be all of the above. That was the topic about which America’s law enforcement Hollywood Squares wanted to discuss with us.







## The Work



The Project’s inaugural publication in May 2020 was “The Digital Dollar Project: Exploring a US CBDC,”17 a comprehensive white paper that introduced the Project to the world and called for thoughtful US research, experimentation, and leadership in the global digital currency landscape. The paper set out several guiding principles for any potential US digital dollar, should it ever be introduced. First, it should be a digital bearer instrument in the form of a token, providing secure and efficient peer-to-peer transactions without undue reliance on intermediaries. Second, it should operate alongside physical cash and existing digital bank reserves, not replace them. Next, it should be distributed through commercial banks and regulated financial intermediaries, minimizing disruptions to the current two-tier financial system. Then, it must balance individual and consumer privacy with regulatory compliance requirements, ensuring protections against undue surveillance while safeguarding against illegal activities. Also, it should not alter the Federal Reserve’s monetary policy actions or its ability to curb inflation, and finally, it should spur innovation, coexist with stablecoins, and foster competition within the financial ecosystem.


The inaugural paper stressed the importance of the United States remaining a global leader in shaping the future of money by encouraging innovation in tokenized currency to improve payment efficiency, security, and accessibility, especially for underserved populations, while, at the same time, actively influencing international CBDC standards to promote interoperability, protect democratic values, and counter authoritarian financial systems like China’s e-CNY. The paper issued a call to action for proactive engagement in global discussions to ensure the continued relevance of the dollar in a digital economy.


Collaboration with leading thought leaders and practitioners in the digital asset space, as well as experiments that test and explore concepts with financial industry partners, are an essential part of the Project’s activities. In addition to hosting or participating in discussions with academic think tanks such as MIT Connection Science and Engineering,18 UC Berkeley’s Center for Responsible, Decentralized Intelligence,19 and the Georgetown University Law Center’s Institute of International Economic Law,20 the Project has conducted two pilots that explored wholesale and retail issues, respectively.


In November 2022, the Project was part of the first-ever, private sector-initiated exploration of a wholesale US CBDC for post-trade securities settlement.21 The Project collaborated with the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation (DTCC) to understand how a tokenized US dollar could function in what is known as “delivery versus payment” (or, simply, immediate settlement) of securities transactions, which is the standard for capital markets to reduce risks for parties to a transaction. The pilot successfully demonstrated that such immediate settlement between tokenized securities and CBDCs was possible across two distinct networks, providing enhanced transparency and simplified reporting compared to existing systems.


In August 2023, the Digital Dollar Project collaborated with Western Union and BDO Unibank to explore the potential of a retail US CBDC to enhance cross-border remittance payments, focusing on the US-Philippines corridor. The published study22 reported that retail CBDCs could reduce counterparty and credit risk, lower costs, reduce fraud and failed transactions, and improve customer visibility.


In December 2023, just outside of Washington, DC, the Digital Dollar Project co-hosted with the Atlantic Council a first-of-its-kind gathering of international policymakers, technologists, financial services providers, innovators, and consumer and privacy advocates from across the globe to explore the digitally networked future of money. The three-day-long event brought together deputy governors and ministries of finance from 13 central banks, including 12 US federal agencies, and was live-streamed to over 100,000 viewers. After three days at the conference, two conclusions were very clear: digital currency networks (both sovereign and nonsovereign and centralized and decentralized) are coming very fast, and the time for global standards—dare I say, enlightened democratic standards—is now.







## Digital Dollar Privacy



One of the most important issues that needs to be addressed in both the existing system and the future of cryptoassets is privacy. With that in mind, the Project published in October 2021 its “Privacy Principles for a Digital Dollar.”23 It pointed out that democratic values and privacy protections rooted in the US Constitution should distinguish dollar-based digital currency from other digital currencies, reinforcing the dollar’s global prominence. To establish a balance between privacy, security, financial inclusion, and transparency in a digital currency system, the paper identifies four core principles that should guide the development of US-dollar-based digital currency:




- Privacy: Dollar-based digital currency should protect users from unwarranted corporate tracking and government surveillance, with access to usage data strictly controlled by due process and Fourth Amendment protections.



- Security: Dollar-based digital currency should enhance rather than compromise security against theft, fraud, and illegal seizures, providing robust protections for digital transactions.



- Accessibility: Dollar-based digital currency should improve financial access for Americans and global dollar users by creating a more efficient, lower-cost system that reduces barriers for the un- and underbanked.



- Transparency: The operational framework of dollar-based digital currency should allow independent verification of its security, functionality, and resistance to exploitation.


The Project’s privacy principles underscore the need to reassess outdated financial privacy policies and anti-money laundering frameworks to align with modern digital technology. They encourage robust consideration of the need to ensure that all forms of digital currency based on the US dollar, including stablecoins, are secure against impermissible monitoring, while addressing new challenges in the digital era.


In July 2023, the Project released a working paper that explored the secure adoption of a digital dollar through the lens of operational and compliance risks to the US banking industry. The paper emphasizes a collaborative approach between public and private sectors to ensure that US-dollar-based digital currency is secure, efficient, and aligned with existing financial systems. The working paper made several recommendations for banks and financial institutions to adopt advanced security measures, including multifactor authentication and cryptographic checks, and strengthen transaction monitoring. It encouraged policymakers to update recordkeeping and reporting rules, provide guidance on offline transactions, and enhance third-party risk management frameworks. It called for public/private partnerships to refine technical standards, support financial literacy, and ensure stakeholder engagement.


In November 2023, the Project published “Interoperability Standards for Digital Assets,” outlining four principles for Digital asset development. They should work seamlessly across borders and institutions, avoiding fragmented “walled garden” payment systems. They should protect user privacy while meeting international regulatory standards. They should be governed by frameworks that ensure stability and reflect societal values. And they should run on environmentally sustainable infrastructure. The paper also called for close collaboration between governments and the private sector to achieve these goals.”24







## Future Proofing the Dollar



Most recently, the Project published a comprehensive review of the state of US-dollar-based digital currency. “Modernizing the US Dollar for the Future of Digital Networks”25 details how money is increasingly being recorded and transferred in the twenty-first century through a variety of digital network instruments, including bank-operated tokenized deposits, commercially operated stablecoins, and sovereign-operated CBDCs. The paper compared these instruments and considered the many advantages that would accrue to the US dollar and the business case for modernization with emerging digital technologies of tokenization, distributed ledger technology, and smart contracts. It identified the range of benefits from reductions in cost and risk to greater improvements in transaction speed and collateral efficiency. It cataloged the numerous private sector developments and innovations, providing early demonstration of these benefits. The paper noted that perhaps the most important benefit to come from modernizing the US dollar is in reinforcing its status as the world’s reserve currency.


“Modernizing the US Dollar for the Future of Digital Networks” reflects the Digital Dollar Project’s five years in examining how best to modernize the US dollar for the digitally networked twenty-first century.26 Through cross-industry interviews, research, and collaboration synthesis, the paper presents the elements required to develop a roadmap specific to US-dollar-denominated digital currencies and networks, akin to the G20 Roadmap for Enhancing Cross-border Payments.27 It explains why modernization of the dollar is important for numerous reasons, including maintenance of US leadership in innovation, interoperability, risk management, and standards development.


Cognizant of the January 23, 2025, Trump Executive Order prohibiting US executive branch agencies from undertaking any action to establish, issue, or promote CBDCs, the Digital Dollar Project continues to avoid championing any particular digital dollar instrument, but to fairly analyze the major contenders.


And yet, there is no stopping technological evolution, and the global future will be “all of the above,” with current efforts bearing proof. Undoubtedly, US-dollar-based stablecoins and tokenized deposits are evolving quickly, based on enabling technologies that offer tangible benefits to financial market utilities, financial institutions, corporations, and consumers. Foreign CBDCs are also coming, despite any current US federal government prohibition. Some may even be under consideration by countries, such as Bermuda, that peg their currencies to the US dollar, effectively creating US-dollar-based CBDCs by foreign sovereigns.


The Trump administration and Congress are correct to empower the US private sector to take the lead in tokenizing the US dollar in the form of stablecoins. Only the private sector can bring the kind of urgency and technical innovation that will not only make digital dollars work, but work with useful “apps” and the necessary “coolness factor” that will attract the world’s patronage. Yet, it is inevitable that there will be discernible differences in the many likely different US stablecoin offerings, from differences in trading liquidity, liquidation periods, issuer profiles, unique apps, and federal and state licensure. As a result, they will likely trade at various discounts and premiums to one another, despite the fact that they are all backed by similar holdings of US Treasury securities. Trading markets will emerge similar to today’s foreign exchange markets to work out pricing differences in US-dollar-based stablecoins. In time, a wholesale US CBDC may need to be created to establish the benchmark value of a sovereign digital dollar to which the various private sector stablecoins are priced at par or at some discount.







## The Work Continues



As my colleagues and I wrapped up our meeting with the assembled national security officials in northern Virginia—a tense meeting at first but ultimately good natured—we felt their respect for the important work of the Digital Dollar Project: exploring how to “future-proof” the dollar for a world of competing digital networks, how to maintain the dollar’s primary reserve currency status, and, perhaps most importantly, how to preserve the universal values of economic freedom for which the dollar has historically stood. When it comes to digital systems of value, the primary US policy focus should be squarely on the dollar—the foundation of the US economy and American prosperity—and how best to transition it to a new digital future while, perhaps most importantly, reclaiming the classical liberal values for which the dollar has historically stood.


But now it is time to turn away from the US dollar and take a 200-quid journey into the English countryside.

# Chapter 7 Darkness at Noon




A visit to a private lodge near Windsor Castle to review the Biden administration’s policy of hindering digital network innovation, the possible ideological reasons behind it and its detrimental but, fortunately, temporary impact on US innovation







## In the English Countryside



On a crisp mid-autumn morning in 2023, I finished a full English breakfast in the dining room of my usual London haunt, calm and genteel Durrants Hotel.1 With the morning sunlight enlivening the room’s dark rosewood paneling and the aroma rising from an invigorating pot of breakfast tea, I jotted notes in the margins of the typed remarks I would give at a private gathering that evening about an hour’s drive into the countryside west of London.


Clive, Durrants’ genial, yet commanding, head concierge, came in and told me that my driver was waiting outside. I finished my cup, grabbed my notes and stepped outside into the waiting Mercedes sedan. It headed west on George Street, north on Baker Street and west again on Marylebone Road through the neighborhoods of Paddington and Bayswater. The landscape gradually became more suburban as we merged onto the M4 Motorway. Then over the better part of an hour the scenery turned into countryside as we crossed the Thames River, narrower and glinting in the autumn light. We drove through a series of “round-abouts” and took a turn that led past a modest wooden sign.


The sign proclaimed our entrance into “Windsor Great Park,” a royal parkland of more than 5,000 acres on the border of the English counties of Berkshire and Surrey. The park bordered Windsor Castle, the country residence of King Charles III and the British Royal Family. For centuries, the park was the castle’s royal hunting ground. Today, it continues under the ownership of a government trust that keeps it open to the public free of charge from dawn to dusk. We drove along a series of ever smaller roads that meandered along a gently rolling surface of lawns, fields, and small woods. Here and there were huge and ancient oak, beech, and plane trees.


We left the road onto a pebbled lane past a row of enormous trees leading to a grand house and pulled to a stop. The house was Cumberland Lodge. Built in the seventeenth century by a captain in Oliver Cromwell’s army, Cumberland Lodge was taken over by King Charles II following the restoration of the monarchy in 1660, and for much of its subsequent history, it was the residence of a series of English nobility from Sarah Churchill, Duchess of Marlborough to William Augustus, Duke of Cumberland, the third son of King George II and from whom the building takes its name.


I was there once again to attend the European Financial Forum, an annual private gathering of current and recent financial market regulators, many European and a few American. The Forum is a unique event, bringing together several dozen, most senior financial policymakers for a series of closed-door presentations and informal discussions accompanied by hearty meals and libations. Over the course of just 24 hours, current financial regulators have the chance to speak about financial and monetary policy and regulation with their recent agency predecessors and other trusted professionals in the quiet of a secluded location under strict Chatham House rules of anonymity.2


I checked in and received a key to a grand bedroom, caught up on calls and email, grabbed lunch in the stately dining room, and greeted friends and former colleagues at the opening reception. I was especially pleased to catch up with the remarkable David Wright, a 34-year veteran of the European Commission in Brussels in charge of financial services policy, Secretary General of the International Organization of Securities Commissions (IOSCO), and current chairman of EUROFI, the European financial services think tank. A keen and fluent authority on global financial regulation, Wright had the respect of his public sector peers. He would be presiding officer of the Forum’s program.


I was there, as I had been in the past, to speak about developments in US regulatory policy. There was more than one current US regulator in attendance and dozens of European ones. I was the first speaker of the afternoon session. The title of my remarks was, “The Digital and Crypto Financial Future: Defining the Right Regulatory Approach.”







## Reviewing the Record



Not long after we assembled in the crowded conference room, David Wright gave me the cue to begin. I started by noting that the very same week marked the 15th anniversary of the publication of the Bitcoin white paper by Satoshi Nakamoto.3 I described the rapid rate of digital network innovation in the decade and a half since and said that financial market regulators worldwide were increasingly recognizing and taking steps to engage with the blockchain revolution. I voiced my often-stated opinion that crypto, tokenization, and blockchain technology would change everything we knew about finance, banking, and the very concept of money itself. I said that, therefore, the world dearly and rightly needed models of well-crafted regulation that protect crypto investors from market misbehavior and fraud while furthering development and innovation.


I briefly reviewed the record of the first Trump administration, in which I had served, where the cryptocurrency industry enjoyed relatively benign treatment despite some critical voices in and out of the administration. Nevertheless, instead of imposing strict rules, the Trump financial regulatory team, led by then-Treasury Secretary Steve Mnuchin took the largely hands-off approach described in my book, CryptoDad. This approach allowed the industry to flourish in a somewhat adaptable regulatory environment and, in some areas, a generally conducive one, most notably under Acting Comptroller of the Currency Brian Brooks and at the CFTC under my administration and that of my successor, Chairman Heath Tarbert.


I went on to explain that the Biden team initially took a similarly indifferent and, in some cases, forward-looking4 approach to crypto, but unfortunately, it gradually became less supportive and more hostile. In March 2022, Biden signed an Executive Order on “Responsible Development of Digital Assets,”5 proposing a comprehensive government crypto strategy. The purpose of the order was to mobilize an expanding administration-wide approach that focused on consumer protection, market fairness, and preventing illegal activities. It called on agencies like the SEC, CFTC, and FinCEN (the Financial Crimes Enforcement Network)6 to intensify efforts to combat scams, market manipulation, and noncompliant exchanges.


Unfortunately, the 2021 Biden PWG Report (described in Chapter 3) and the 2022 digital asset Executive Order were missed opportunities. Instead of supporting modernization, the Biden administration appeared overly concerned with protecting legacy financial systems, institutions, and frameworks. It seemed intended to shelter Washington, DC’s financial agencies from financial sector innovation and market evolution. The PWG report, in particular, appeared designed to defend bank incumbents from competition by restricting nonbanks from issuing stablecoins. It made no call to reinterpret 90-year-old analog regulatory frameworks and update twentieth-century rulebooks to accommodate twenty-first-century digital technology. Rather, it gave license to Biden’s regulators to continue to use civil enforcement powers to insist that innovators first satisfy rigid and decades-old regulatory strictures before solving commercial and societal shortcomings of the venerable financial system.


Looking out at the 40 or so senior regulators and financial market leaders gathered at Cumberland Lodge, I explained that under the cover of those White House directives, Biden administration regulators set out to stymie digital financial network innovation through four major tactics: (1) refusing to adapt analog-era rules for new innovation; (2) wielding aggressive regulatory enforcement litigation; (3) denying commercial banking services to disfavored businesses, their sponsors, and individual principals; and (4) avoiding engagement with Congress on new legislation. All four steps broke long-standing Washington, DC, norms. No one in the room seemed surprised or shocked by what I had just said.








## Refusing to Adapt



I went on to explain that the first tactic of avoiding rulemaking for digital assets was deployed separately by Biden appointees at the SEC,7 the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation (the FDIC, federal regulator of insured deposit-taking banks and institutions), the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (the OCC, regulator of federally chartered banks), and the Federal Reserve’s office of supervision. Despite responsible market advocates’ uniform calls for appropriate regulation, Biden regulators resolutely declined to promulgate new rules for the nascent digital asset innovation. This refusal was despite the fact that agency rulemaking is generally intended to give flexibility to venerable regulatory frameworks to accommodate new technology needs that do not rise to a level needing Congressional legislation.


At the Gensler SEC, rulemaking that could have provided a realistic path to registration for digital asset sponsors was deliberately avoided. This refusal to engage was particularly infuriating to Congress, which angrily cited “Gensler’s SEC refuse[al] to create clear, functional regulation for consumers and investors engaging in the digital asset ecosystem.”8 The SEC chairman defended this refusal by saying that existing rules were adequate under the policy of “same activity, same rules,” meaning that regulatory treatment should focus on actual market behaviors—not the technology used. According to Chairman Gensler, “There’s no reason to treat the crypto market differently from the rest of the capital markets just because it uses a different technology.”9 Elsewhere he added, “…activities should be subject to consistent regulation, though, regardless of the entity, the technology, or the business model. If a driver is pulled over for speeding, it doesn’t really matter if she’s driving an electric vehicle or a gas powered one.”10


Gensler’s stated explanation is discredited by the SEC’s own regulatory framework. SEC securities regulations are highly differentiated depending on whether a securities issuance is debt or equity, whether the issuer is a municipality or a corporation, and whether the investors are sophisticated or not, rich or poor. Moreover, the core logic is weak. Transportation is indeed an activity, and yet, there are very different rules and regulations depending on whether the transportation is automotive, railroad, maritime, or aeronautical. Imagine trying to pull over the pilot of an A-380 Airbus passenger jet for speeding.


Refusal to enact apt crypto regulation at Gensler’s SEC was especially incongruous considering the agency’s vigor in promulgating rules to further social activism and regulatory overreach in such areas as environmental, social, and corporate governance.11 One area of crypto regulation where the SEC was only too happy to conduct rulemaking was in the area of crypto custody. Issued in March 2022, SEC Staff Accounting Bulletin 12112 (referred to as “SAB 121”) required crypto custodians to record digital assets held for customers as liabilities on their balance sheets, making it economically infeasible to offer crypto custody services.13 In May 2024, Congress recorded a bipartisan vote with strong Democrat support to defy the SEC and rescind SAB 121.14 Unfortunately, President Biden vetoed the resolution, arguing that dropping the SEC’s guidelines would undermine its authority over accounting practices.15 The veto clearly demonstrated the Biden administration’s lack of support for regulation that would accommodate the promise of crypto innovation.







## Enforcement and Prosecution



I told the Cumberland Lodge gathering that the second set of crypto smothering tactics deployed by Team Biden was aggressive enforcement known as “regulation by enforcement.” Rather than promulgate clear rules applicable to crypto, the SEC increased its docket of legal actions against cryptocurrency-related companies and their individual founders for noncompliance with the agency’s dated and inapt regulations. From April 2021 to January 2025, the SEC initiated 125 cryptocurrency-related enforcement actions, resolving 98 of the actions with penalties totaling approximately $6.05 billion.16 This level of enforcement activity was extraordinary compared to prior administrations.17


Despite the unprecedented level of enforcement actions, the Gensler SEC failed to protect investors in some of the biggest failures in crypto, including FTX, Terra/Luna, Three Arrows Capital, Celsius, and Voyager.18 As for FTX, the SEC’s failure to create a means for FTX to come into its regulatory framework left FTX customers without protection from the firm’s fraud. Fortunately, the CFTC’s crypto-neutral regulatory framework meant that FTX customers trading in CFTC-regulated markets suffered no financial loss whatsoever.


In more than a few cases, the regulatory enforcement actions seemed unnecessarily antagonistic and lawless.19 “Gallows humor” in the crypto industry had it that getting sued by the SEC was a sign that a business had a sufficiently viable business model to pose a threat to the incumbent financial system. That system, it seemed, enjoyed the “protection” of Gensler’s SEC. Getting sued by the SEC meant that you had promise and might be disruptive. That simply had to be stopped.


The SEC issued an unprecedented amount of so-called “Wells Notices,”20 which are used to communicate to a company or entity that they are under investigation for potential violations of securities laws.21 I am professionally aware that in some cases, Wells Notices were issued with neither a preliminary SEC investigation nor any subsequent enforcement action, a clear breach of agency norms. The impact was to hamper the industry’s development and ability to raise capital. I advised one crypto firm that had received a Wells Notice to petition the SEC after more than a year of painful silence to lift the notice and let them get on with their business.


On August 14, 2024, the SEC announced that it had compelled 26 large financial service firms to pay an unprecedented amount of more than $390 million to settle charges for record-keeping failures.22 Yet, the agency’s own Office of Inspector General (OIG) reported that messages from Gensler’s government-issued iPhone, spanning October 2022 through September 2023, were missing and could not be retrieved.23 That period of missing communications covers the period of the bank record-keeping failures, the collapse of FTX, the Grayscale lawsuit over a spot Bitcoin ETF, and a slew of other enforcement moves that defined Gensler’s tenure.24 Apparently, the same record-keeping rules that applied to the financial services industry did not apply to the SEC chairman.


Worse, the Gensler-led SEC was caught being duplicitous in its judicial maneuvers in cases brought against crypto firms. In one particularly egregious case, a federal court severely sanctioned the agency in 2024 for “undermining the integrity” of judicial proceedings, engaging in misconduct, and intentionally misleading the court about evidence used to obtain a temporary restraining order and freezing of assets.25 The US District Court for the District of Utah ordered the SEC to pay $1.8 million in attorney and receivership fees and dismissed the SEC’s case. I can find no record that the SEC was ever so sanctioned by a federal court in its entire history. This recent rebuke was, and is, a huge black eye for the SEC, hurting its credibility now and for a long time to come with the federal judiciary, before which it must bring its hundreds of legitimate enforcement actions.







## Debanking



I knew that I had the room’s full attention. I was provocative in explaining how the Biden team had broken US judicial norms in ways that the previous Trump team did not. I felt that my next case would be especially concerning.


I explained that Biden’s bank regulators had acted to deny commercial banking services to crypto companies, their sponsors, and individual principals, making it impossible for banks to serve the emerging industry. The ostensible purpose was to manage “high-risk” banking activities and protect the “safety and soundness” of the traditional banking system. Yet, in their zeal to thwart crypto, the Biden team likely contributed to the demise of several banks, risking collapse of the banking system.26


I explained that the matter of “debanking” first arose during the Obama administration with “Operation Choke Point”—a controversial Justice Department initiative that discouraged banks from working with certain businesses that the Obama team considered “high-risk,” such as payday lenders and firearm retailers.27 The policy led to the closure, often with little notice or explanation, of hundreds of bank accounts determined to be too “risky.” Observers asserted that Operation Choke Point violated the Takings Clause of the Constitution’s Fifth Amendment, which prohibits the federal (and local) governments from taking private property except for public use if it provides “just compensation.”28 The US Justice Department officially ended the program in 2017 under the first Trump administration.


Back in power in 2021, the Biden team dusted off Operation Choke Point29 and directed it against crypto companies and founders30 and the banks that serviced them.31 Officials at the Fed, FDIC, and OCC pressured large and regional banks under their supervision to shutter banking services for hundreds of digital finance technology companies and their founders.32 Multi-year banking relationships were suddenly ended with little notice or explanation, causing operational chaos and thwarting payrolls and vendor payments for weeks to months.33 I am informed by members of the accounting profession that auditors were also encouraged by the SEC and the Public Company Accounting Oversight Board (PCAOB), whose board members are appointed by the SEC, to refrain from providing financial auditing services to the crypto industry.


US Senator Bill Hagerty called Operation Choke Point 2.0 “the coordinated effort by the Biden administration’s financial regulators to suffocate our domestic crypto economy by debanking the industry and severing entrepreneurs from the capital necessary to invest here in America.”34 According to columnist Charlie Gasparino, “Debanking is such an odd word for one of the most insidious parts of cancel culture, and its sponsors like it that way. It sanitizes, via clumsy lingo, what is essentially something of dangerous Orwellian magnitude: negating an American citizen’s ability to save and conduct business through a big bank.”35 For expressing an opinion or starting a business out of step with the progressive culture norms that have infected so much of society, you too can see your economic livelihood go up in smoke and “debanked.”36


But it was worse than that. The Biden team went after the banks themselves that served the emerging crypto industry, namely Silvergate, Silicon Valley Bank, and Signature.37 Other than their first initial “S” and the similarity of their customer bases, the banks were in different states of financial solvency. The popular narrative that all three were mismanaged is false. Silvergate and Signature were solvent. They just serviced an industry that was out of favor with their regulators. Despite short sellers and members of Congress encouraging a bank run based on rumors that Silvergate had exposure to a failure of FTX, Silvergate remained solvent and able to do business until the Federal Reserve told it to reduce its crypto exposure to only a small part of its business.38 Yet, serving the crypto industry was Silvergate’s primary business, and so, barred from that business, it chose to voluntarily liquidate. Silvergate did so in an orderly fashion and without public assistance or bailouts.39


In the case of Signature Bank, another bank serving the crypto industry, none other than the late Barney Frank, non-executive board member and former Congressional co-author of the 2010 Dodd-Frank Wall Street Reform and Consumer Protection Act, declared that the firm was fully solvent when bank regulators suddenly shut it down late on a Sunday evening, causing widespread panic.40 In fact, in the weeks before its closure, Signature bank was actually looking for ways to curtail its exposure to crypto in order to reduce the displeasure of regulators.


The failure of the third “S,” Silicon Valley Bank or SVB, had little to do with its crypto banking clients and much to do with its unpreparedness and that of its Federal Reserve supervisors for unrealized losses on its balance sheet stemming largely from investments in US Treasury securities, which on paper were underwater due to rapid and unanticipated interest rate increases.41 About 94% of SBV customers’ deposits exceeded the $250,000 FDIC insurance limit and therefore took flight when SBV’s balance sheet losses became known.42


In all three cases, the banks were subject to reckless treatment by bank regulators driven by disdain for the banks’ service to the crypto industry.43 While Operation Choke Point 2.0 was directed at the budding crypto industry, it presumably could have been directed at any other lawful industry that the Obama and Biden administrations found distasteful. It proves the ability of a determined US presidential administration to operate outside of institutional norms to censor targeted financial activity. It smacks of the British military’s attempts to seize American colonial arms and the tower disarming the square repeatedly throughout human history. It unwittingly justifies one of the core premises of Bitcoin and decentralized crypto: censorship resistance.







## Nonengagement



With the undivided attention of the Cumberland Lodge gathering, I turned to my last point: how Biden market regulators, particularly at the SEC, refused to support congressional efforts to pass meaningful digital asset legislation.


I explained that in July 2023, a bill was introduced to better define the roles of the CFTC in regulating crypto. To ease its passage, companion bills were put forward jointly in the House committees that oversaw the SEC and CFTC: the Financial Services and the Agriculture Committees, respectively. They were sponsored by the Committee Chairmen Patrick McHenry (R-NC) and G.T. Thompson (R-PA), as well as their subcommittee chairs.


The legislation labored through the lower chamber with little to no constructive involvement from the Biden White House or the SEC.44 Nevertheless, 10 months later in May 2024, the House of Representatives passed the legislation, entitled the “Financial Innovation and Technology for the 21st Century Act,” known as FIT21. Though imperfect, the bill established key guidelines for crypto, including token issuance, trading, and custody. The final bill was approved with bipartisan support, with 71 Democrats joining 208 Republicans in passage.


Still, the White House pushed back, citing a lack of consumer and investor protections. President Biden issued a statement criticizing the bill.45 SEC Chairman Gensler also published his opposition to the legislation (a fairly unprecedented move), arguing that it would undermine the SEC’s efforts to classify crypto assets as investment contracts by removing them from SEC oversight.46 Unfortunately, the Senate did not pass FIT21 companion legislation, and so, the legislation did not make it to the desk of President Biden. In the end, the refusal to cooperate and active opposition by White House and SEC doomed Congress’ thoughtful attempts to put crypto on a sound legal and regulatory footing.







## Lost Opportunity



I brought my Cumberland Lodge presentation to a close. I said that, as a consequence of the Biden administration’s policy of persecuting digital asset innovation, talent was fleeing the United States. I noted that the venture capital firm, Electric Capital, did an analysis every year of engineering talent in crypto. Their research showed that in the first three years of the Biden presidency, the global share of crypto developers in the United States declined each year so that 80% of all crypto development was offshore by 2024,47 a shameful accomplishment.


I noted that the United States was now struggling to maintain its leadership position in digital network technology. Other countries and regions had taken more proactive measures to secure their stake in the sector. I said that that included agencies under the leadership of people in the room: in the UK, His Majesty’s Treasury’s just then announced conclusory statement on its crypto asset regulatory regime, and Japan, Singapore and Switzerland with their early and continuing leadership. It also included the European Union and its forward-leaning Markets in Crypto-Assets (MiCA) regulation, establishing a comprehensive licensing and disclosure framework for crypto-asset issuers and service providers operating across the European Union.48 I related that, as an American business advisor and lawyer, it pained me to advise clients to avoid launching crypto businesses in the United States during the Biden years.


I explained to the group that I had deliberately recounted the norm-breaking work of the Biden-era SEC in the hope that it will never be repeated. It is the same reason why I am recounting it here. Such suppression of innovation must not be normalized now or ever. However arguably enlightened or righteous the motivation, it is improper for an unelected regulatory body such as the SEC to act to suppress an otherwise legal economic activity. Determining the legality of any human action is the prerogative of an elected legislature, the US Congress. If not banned by Congress, then the only legitimate response of a US regulatory body to new innovation within its statutory purview is to bring the activity within its regulatory framework. I faced this same issue during my CFTC chairmanship with the emergence of crypto futures. Voices in industry, media and government encouraged me to use agency authority to block certification of crypto futures to trade in CFTC-regulated markets. To not block it, they argued, would be to “legitimize” crypto. My response was that it was not for me as an unelected agency head to legitimize or delegitimize an otherwise lawful innovation. Short of Congress banning crypto, my only course and, in fact, my duty was to adapt the CFTC’s regulatory framework to accommodate crypto and properly regulate it. I could not avoid that duty for lack of agency resources or personnel or even whether it posed a threat to the status quo of the financial system. My job was to regulate it in such a way that it did not pose a threat to financial stability. I would have to let the industry status quo fend for itself. If I wasn’t willing to step up to the duty of unbiased regulation, than I should have stepped down. Rather, I stepped up and did my job.


To my mind, the Biden years of financial regulation were ones in which the federal policy “tower” set out by all means to suppress the decentralized crypto “square.” Someday the square would fight back. I was surprised it happened so soon. Right after inauguration Day 2025, in fact.
