# Chapter 8 Winners and Losers




The Author’s eyewitness account of backroom efforts by both US presidential candidates to attract the growing cadre of crypto voters and why one effort was way too little and the other was just right




“Would you like another cup of coffee?”


“No, thank you. I’m fine.”


With a smile, I waved off the pleasant flight attendant. It was a clear mid-July morning, and I was seated in the modest front cabin of United Flight #3718 bound for Wisconsin.


The trip was a bit of a last-minute thing, as I was asked a few days earlier to attend the 2024 Republican National Convention (RNC) to give a TED-style talk on digital asset policy in the first and, potentially, second Trump administrations. The program was being put on by the Blockchain Sessions, an independent, nonpartisan organization1 that would also give presentations at the Democratic National Convention in mid-August, at which I was also asked to speak.


I planned to give a version of my standard PowerPoint presentation emphasizing crypto and blockchain as a new architecture of finance, but also discuss digital assets, including stablecoins and CBDCs, and try to demystify some of the confusion. I hoped also to attend some RNC events and meet with senior campaign officials.





## Too Little, Too Late



As the flight soared over Lake Erie en route to the Republican public spectacle in Milwaukee, I reflected on the prior week’s Democratic private crypto conclave in Washington, DC.


That meeting took place at 10:00 am on Wednesday, July 10, 2024, in a large conference room in the basement of the historic Willard Hotel a block away from the White House. The meeting hosts were Mark Cuban, businessman and television personality, and Democratic Congressman Ro Khanna, who represents a California district close to Silicon Valley. The White House Representative was Anita Dunn, a Biden confidante and head of communications.2 Also present was US Senator Kirsten Gillibrand (D-NY), a respected crypto advocate. Industry participants included more than two dozen representatives of some of the most important digital asset companies and their leaders and investors.


Khanna began with a short welcome and plea for dialogue between the Biden administration and the industry. Cuban prefaced his remarks by saying that he was a stalwart supporter of the administration. He then launched into a 10-minute diatribe on the Biden administration’s failures regarding crypto. At one point he went so far as to say, “You guys totally suck on crypto. You’ve blown it. You’ve allowed Trump to own the issue.”


Dunn did not respond to his criticism. Instead, she spoke from prepared notes, pedantically saying that crypto innovation was “important for American competitiveness and financial inclusion.” She referenced the Administration’s Statement of Administration Position3 accompanying its veto of the repeal of the SEC Staff Accounting Bulletin 121 (SAB-121) and read the portion that said that the Administration wants to support development of a durable legal framework for crypto.


At this, Cuban interjected that if the administration truly wanted to support development of a durable legal framework for crypto, it shouldn’t have vetoed the repeal of SAB-121 in the first place. He explained that SAB-121 (described earlier) required entities registered with the SEC to record on their financial statements a liability and a corresponding asset for digital assets, a controversial accounting interpretation that hindered banks from offering custody services for Bitcoin and other crypto.


Senator Gillibrand spoke next. She was very forceful and articulate, speaking directly to Dunn, laying out the current state of US policy development. She said that crypto had grown into a global industry that was not going away but was at risk of moving offshore. If it did move offshore, she said, it would leave the US regulatory umbrella and create extra risks for consumers. She took direct aim at Gary Gensler, saying that he was particularly destructive to US crypto development.


She then explained the legal distinction between digital commodities regulated by the CFTC and digital securities regulated by the SEC that, to my expert ears, was a tour-de-force legal and policy exposition, quite remarkable for a politician.


In her remarks, Senator Gillibrand specifically acknowledged the successful launch of Bitcoin futures in 2018 during the Trump administration. Turning to me, she said, “Chris, how long ago was it that you kicked off Bitcoin futures?”


Surprised at first by the question, I replied, “Five and a half years ago” (in fact, it was then six-and-a-half years before). Recovering, I added, “That market today is deep, liquid, transparent, and well regulated by the CFTC.” I added, pointedly, “It’s proof that regulators can engage successfully with crypto, if they are willing to.”


Gillibrand then detailed the substance and progress of current Senate crypto legislation. She then directly asked for the administration to support both Stablecoin legislation and her own crypto bill, Lummis-Gillibrand.4 Dunn did not respond.


Congressman Khanna then opened the discussion to the seated industry representatives. In this give and take, Anita Dunn got a real earful. One participant said that President Biden should fire Gary Gensler, admit that the White House’s veto of Congress’ repeal of SAB 121 was wrong, and separate from Elizabeth Warren, who was “toxic” to crypto innovation. Other participants echoed that the extreme positions of both Gensler and Senator Warren were detrimental to the Democrats’ ability to appeal to crypto supporters and young voters in the coming presidential election.


Another participant noted that, while it was good that the White House sent its communications director, Anita Dunn, to meet with the industry, the next day it would be sending Caroline Crenshaw, a known crypto antagonist, to a Senate confirmation hearing for a second term as a SEC commissioner. Dunn did not seem to recognize Crenshaw’s name and had to whisper a question to a staffer for Crenshaw’s title and position.


Other participants said that they were lifelong Democrats who had never been so disappointed in its leadership. One said that he had been warning the White House for a year that its negative approach to crypto would turn into an election-year liability, and now it had. Another person said that, while appreciating Dunn’s attendance at the meeting, it was disappointing that the White House sent its communications lead and not a substantive policy director. Dunn did indeed seem a poor choice for a White House representative, with obviously little crypto domain knowledge or policy influence.


After 45 minutes, Anita Dunn apologized that she had to depart. She said that she had taken copious notes that would be shared with the administration, and that the White House was listening to the concerns of the industry and wanted to get its policy right. She thanked the group for its candid remarks and left the room. Not long after, the meeting broke up, and the participants departed, quite uniformly dispirited.


Over the next few weeks, the Willard Hotel meeting was mentioned in some media reports5 and described as an important step toward better Biden administration crypto policy.6 I discounted these as planted by the White House and some loyal Democrat participants. My sense was that the participants were quite disappointed in the level of engagement from the White House and somewhat shocked at the degree to which it was politically out of touch on crypto innovation.


One participant, a lifelong Democrat, said to me as we were leaving the Willard: “Too little, too late.” I think that is a good overall summary of the Biden administration’s support for crypto and American digital asset innovation.







## Generation Gap



“Would you like anything else before we begin our initial descent into Milwaukee?”


“Nothing, thank you.”


I continued thinking about the Willard Hotel meeting. I wondered how things had gotten to this point for Team Biden. I recalled that the subject of crypto did not generally divide the two US political parties during my service in Washington from 2014 to 2019. In fact, crypto was not political back in 2017 when we greenlighted Bitcoin futures at the CFTC. Yes, we ran up against some opposition from the legacy financial industry (including full-page appeals in The Wall Street Journal)7, European regulatory complaints, and some beltway press derision.8 But we didn’t face full-throated political opposition. My fellow CFTC Commissioners—Republicans and Democrats—were characteristically unified in support of regulatory innovation. They included CFTC Commissioner Russ Behnam, who would go on to lead the CFTC during the Biden Administration with poise and distinction and without surrendering the agency’s leadership in crypto innovation.


The political divide a decade ago and, somewhat still today, is a generational one: older people, including older Washington politicians who frantically call their grandkids to help download a new mobile phone app, generally struggle to appreciate crypto and are often disdainful of it. Younger people, on the other hand, the generation who grew up in a socially networked world and who love video games and avoid banks, either get crypto or are open to getting it.


In a recent podcast recording, one of my interviewers, a smart high school student, said, “So, I have this theory about the prevalence of what you would call meme coins and meme stocks as well, which is that they’re actually a ‘resistance’ for young people …. What I mean by this is that the S&P is currently valued at 25 times the average price-to-earnings ratio, which is historically overvalued. Right? When my old man was young, it was 11 times. Housing is completely unaffordable, and you know, traditional wealth paths are closing. So, do you agree with me that young people are effectively creating their own asset class as an act of resistance?”9


I thought that the young interviewer was onto something. I responded that the generational divide over crypto reminded me of author Douglas Adams’ “Rules for Technology”:




- “Anything that is in the world when you’re born is normal and ordinary and is just a natural part of the way the world works.”



- “Anything that’s invented between when you’re 15 and 35 is new and exciting and revolutionary, and you can probably get a career in it.”



- “Anything invented after you’re 35 is against the natural order of things.”10


Washington’s countless septuagenarian and octogenarian politicians had encountered and mastered traditional finance between the ages of 15 and 35. Crypto arrived well after they turned 35 and was thus “against the natural order of things.” For many, therefore, crypto needed to be ignored, constantly ridiculed, and, ultimately, suppressed.


I wondered where and when this generational divide over crypto had evolved into a political divide? How did the Democrat party leadership get to be so institutionally anti-crypto, and when did Republicans come to embrace it?


My theory on the crypto political divide is based on the past half century of US financial history. Beginning in the 1980s with the “Reagan Revolution” policies of lowering taxes, government spending, and regulation, and drawing upon the writings of Friedrich Hayek11 and the television appearances of Milton Friedman,12 the dominant economic philosophy came to be that the free market, albeit imperfect, is the best mechanism to allocate capital within a society. That philosophy effectively held sway throughout the last two decades of the twentieth century into the early years of the twenty-first and generally remains the guiding light for mainstream Republicanism.


Arising out of the 2008 financial crisis, however, and accelerated by the Obama administration through the Dodd-Frank Act, an alternative philosophy emerged. This one argued that free markets, often intermediated by large, elite Wall Street firms, had done a poor job of allocating capital, especially for historically marginalized communities. To address this market failure, what was needed was a strong degree of political control to curb the worst excesses of the free market and corporate America.13 That political control would be provided by the Washington beltway political class and its instrumentalities in academia and the mainstream media. This philosophy was encapsulated in the Dodd-Frank Act, which I often refer to as a “victory of Washington over Wall Street.” The law and the general philosophy underpinning it was the guiding light for mainstream Democrats through the Obama and Biden years.


The emergence of crypto challenges both traditional Republicans and Democrats because it repudiates elements of each party’s economic philosophy. Crypto holds that capital allocation need not depend on political elites ensconced in the towers of Washington or financial elites commanding the towers of Wall Street, but can instead be governed by transparent, programmable software operating in the open square of digital networks. It frees society from reliance on capricious bank intermediaries and venture capitalists, as well as controlling politicians who direct capital to favored supporters—the traditional toll takers and gatekeepers of both towers. In many ways, crypto represents the rise of Silicon Valley—and perhaps Main Street—as genuine rivals to Wall Street and Washington, offering the square as an alternative architecture for determining who controls capital and on whose terms. The result may be a four-way contest for that agency—and a historic challenge to the towers themselves.


For the Washingtonians, loss of their hard-won control over the levers of finance is a bitter loss, especially for Elizabeth Warren and her financial regulatory disciples, who held sway over the Biden administration’s financial market personnel and policies. During those years, some wondered if she and her acolytes would become more crypto-friendly if the industry worked to better educate them in the new innovation. Certainly, the industry tried to do so. The Winklevoss Brothers launched a multi-million dollar ad campaign in 2019 entitled, “The Revolution Needs Rules.”14 I respected this initiative and others like it, including by the Digital Chamber, one of the leading American advocacy organizations for American innovation in digital ledger technology, Bitcoin, digital currency, and digital assets, where I served on the advisory board.15


Those efforts were highly influential with open-minded policymakers on both sides of Washington’s political aisle. But not so for Elizabeth Warren and her self-proclaimed “anti-crypto army”16 who, I believe, fully appreciated digital asset innovation and its enormous potential. That was exactly why she attacks it so fiercely. She knows it could rob her and her supporters of the political control they have wielded over the current financial system.


To my eyes, Democrats’ partisan antipathy to crypto really began with the failure and dénouement of Sam Bankman-Fried (SBF). As the valuation and popularity of his crypto platform soared in 2021 and 2022, SBF lobbied for regulation of the crypto industry. In the footsteps of their prominent Democrat parents, SBF and his brother became active in DC politics and the second largest contributors17 to the Democratic Party, contributing $40 million to campaigns and political action committees in 2022.18 SBF’s soaring influence with Democratic leadership may have been symbolized by the kiss blown to him and captured on C-SPAN by Maxine Waters, Democratic powerbroker and chair of the House Financial Services Committee.19 I believe the embarrassment of SBF’s subsequent downfall led Democrats to adopt a game plan of strategic attack, better known in sports as “the best defense is a good offense.” From the SBF debacle through the 2024 elections, leading Democrats like Gary Gensler, Elizabeth Warren, and the vocal crypto antagonist, California Democratic Congressman Brad Sherman20 would rail that crypto was little more than the tool of money launderers and drug dealers. I believe the embarrassment factor, including Gensler’s own undisclosed interactions with SBF,21 became a driving force behind the SEC’s disdain for crypto, a complete reversal of the traditional American embrace of innovation.


Looking at things in the best possible light, it was an overcorrection by the Democrats. Biden-era crypto antipathy often seemed to conflate protecting financial stability with preserving the financial status quo, preferencing the tower over the square. Undoubtedly, their skepticism and dismissal of crypto was not without cause, following high-profile collapses like FTX and TerraUSD that deserved strong criticism. Yet, at worst, the Biden administration’s unallayed and constant crypto hostility went beyond constructive criticism and appeared to be driven by reactionary fear of loss of political control over capital allocation and traditional finance. One informed opinion is that the Biden administration’s antipathy to fintech innovation was driven by electoral politics: that taking a confrontational posture toward the technology industry would help Democrats reconnect with working-class voters who had drifted away from the party.22 Instead, it failed to gain many working-class voters while it burned up existing support from allies in the tech sector, including Silicon Valley, and lost those on the younger end of crypto’s generational divide.23


Whatever the motivation, regulatory hostility to crypto and digital asset innovation would inevitably become unsustainable as US national policy. America’s spirit of innovation merits smart regulation, not political animus. It was the failure to see the consequences of that course and reverse the strategy in time for the 2024 election that, I believe, contributed to Democrats’ electoral losses.







## Just in Time



“Thank you for flying with us today. I hope you have a great visit to Milwaukee.”


“Thank you. I am sure I will.”


I debarked the plane and made my way through Milwaukee’s unassuming General Mitchell International Airport.24 I grabbed an Uber, which dropped me off in front of a large brick, former warehouse in an older part of Milwaukee where I checked into the industrial-chic Iron Horse Hotel, a homage to the city’s home to the Harley-Davidson motorcycle works. After settling into my room, I returned downstairs and took a stool in the biker-themed bar and ordered an appropriate drink: Jack Daniel’s and soda.


I thought about the Democrats’ crypto dilemma and contrasted it with the budding crypto positivity of the Trump campaign. A few months before, when I spoke at the Digital Chamber’s 2024 DC Blockchain Summit in mid-May, I may have been the first to publicly accord Trump as America’s “first crypto president.” There, I had said,



Politics is politics. And politics abhors a vacuum. What one party gradually comes to disdain and demagogue, the other party will champion.


So, it is with crypto. What one side calls upon an army to suppress, the other builds a campaign to advance.


In addition to announcing he’s now “good with it”—(crypto, that is)—former President Donald Trump may justifiably claim to be: “America’s first crypto president.”


That is because of the launch of regulated Bitcoin futures under the CFTC in the first year of the Trump presidency. The enduring success of that regulated futures marketplace has helped ensure that the world’s first digital commodity—Bitcoin—is priced in US dollars. Dollar pricing of the world’s natural commodities is a key underpinning of the dollar’s reserve currency status. Dollar pricing of Bitcoin and other digital commodities will buttress the dollar’s reserve currency status in the digital future to come. This is indeed an important and enduring achievement.25




The truth was that candidate Trump really hadn’t seized the mantle of crypto president thus far in the campaign. I hoped my remarks would encourage him to do so. Undoubtedly, the opportunity was building as more crypto innovators, especially crypto miners, made their way to the Trump campaign headquarters at Mar-a-Lago in Palm Beach and, eventually, to Trump himself.







## Crypto Talk



I paid for my Jack Daniel’s and walked out of the Iron Horse Hotel, getting into an Uber. It took me to Milwaukee’s creative hub: the historic Third Ward. It is home to galleries, performing arts venues, and chic restaurants occupying nineteenth-century warehouses and storefronts. I climbed the stairs of 217 North Broadway, a former coffee roasting mill and a beautifully preserved relic of the great American industrial age. That vintage building was now a premier event space, set to record “The Blockchain Sessions,” my afternoon TED-style talk to a group of delegates to the 2024 RNC. 26 I again referred to Trump as “America’s first crypto president.”27 The next day, RNC delegates nominated Donald Trump as their 2024 presidential candidate.



Talking About Crypto in the summer of 2024



Two weeks later, in late July 2024, Donald Trump stepped up and seized the mantle of crypto president. In remarks given at the Bitcoin 2024 conference in Nashville,28 Trump claimed to be the first American president to address a Bitcoin event.


Trump stated: “I stand before you today filled with respect and admiration for what the Bitcoin community has achieved.”29


Trump referenced the Biden administration’s hostility to crypto and spoke effectively about the frustration of the audience by saying:



The Biden-Harris administration has waged a war on crypto and Bitcoin. For those who work in this industry, they target your banks and choke off your financial services. They block ordinary Americans from transferring money to your exchanges. They slander you as criminals and scammers. They punish you for violating regulations they can’t explain, making up the rules as they go along.


The day I take the oath of office, the persecution stops and the weaponization ends. The moment I’m sworn in, Joe Biden and Kamala Harris’s anti-crypto crusade will be OVER. And as long as I’m in the Oval Office, Elizabeth Warren and her goons will keep their hands off your Bitcoin.




Trump then promised to fire SEC Chairman Gary Gensler on the first day of his new term, seemingly surprised by the biggest applause of the speech.


He went on,



I will always defend the right to SELF-CUSTODY. America will once again be a nation that protects property rights, privacy, free association and free speech.


As part of our effort to provide regulatory clarity, we will create a framework to enable the safe and responsible expansion of “stablecoins”— allowing us to extend the dominance of the US dollar to new frontiers around the world.




Trump further vowed to be “pro-Bitcoin” and “pro-innovation” if he was reelected, saying, “Our nation has never thrived by trying to censor new ideas and shut down the dreams of our people.” He promised that, if elected, the United States would retain all of the Bitcoin it currently holds to be the “core” of a strategic national Bitcoin stockpile. He said he would keep the United States ahead of China and other countries in cryptocurrency and Bitcoin innovation as the “crypto capital of the world,” a line that became the watchword for the Trump campaign’s crypto policy and its successful capture of the crypto generation.


In mid-August 2024, Trump named Howard Lutnick, chairman of Cantor Fitzgerald, a successful New York financial services firm, as a co-chair of his transition team with responsibility for personnel appointments. I had known Howard for close to 20 years as a fierce competitor to my former firm, GFI Group,30 a close colleague in our former industry trade association,31 and a regulated registrant during my time as CFTC Chairman.32 Some people say that there are seven sides to Howard Lutnick: one is a humanitarian, the other six are a sextet of the shrewdest and most artful performers to ever play on Wall Street.


As the 2024 campaign summer turned into the campaign autumn, I met with Lutnick in his spacious Park Avenue offices. I undertook to assist him in sourcing candidates for CFTC commissioners and chairman. I made clear that I did not wish to go back into government service. He dismissed my declension with a smile and a short, “We’ll see about that.”


A few weeks later, on November 5, 2024, Donald Trump won a decisive election victory: 312 electoral votes (226 for Harris) and 2.2 million more popular votes.33 His party also won control of the Senate and the House of Representatives. The Republican candidate’s pro-crypto policy prescriptions were now those of the executive branch of government.







## Back to the Countryside



On the day following the 2024 US elections, I caught an overnight flight out of Newark Liberty International Airport, waking up early the next morning at London’s Heathrow airport. Carrying nothing more than my briefcase and a garment bag, I found my waiting driver, who whisked me away from the airport into the early morning English countryside.


Once again, I was headed to Cumberland Lodge for the 2025 European Financial Forum, and once again, I was the hotly anticipated opening speaker to talk about the earthquake that had just shook Washington.


As before, David Wright prompted me to begin. I again began with a teasing tone: “He’s back!!! Donald Trump that is. He will be the 47th president of the United States.”


I explained that not only did Trump win by a sizable margin, but his party won control of both legislative chambers. One would have to go back to the American elections of Ronald Reagan, Lyndon Johnson, and Franklin Roosevelt to find results this complete. I suggested that Trump’s victory may be the biggest political comeback in American history, even world history, in some respects. Trump managed this victory despite two assassination attempts, two impeachments, two different general election opponents in the same cycle, nine years of major institutions like the FBI and Justice Department weaponized against him, and 91 highly politicized legal charges resulting in a questionable felony conviction. And, on top of this, according to one media-monitoring analysis, he received consistently negative coverage from the three major US broadcast news networks, estimated as 85% negative, while that of Harris was only 22% negative.34 Digging down a little further, I noted that Trump outperformed with almost every major demographic group: Whites, Hispanics, Blacks, Catholics, Protestants, Jews, young people, union members, and independents. The only segment where Harris did particularly well was with those earning more than twice the national average income.


I pointed out that the significance of Trump’s victory is that he has a clear mandate to govern. This is especially so since his campaign was very detailed and substantive about its priorities. His win was a rebuke to Obama-era identity politics and to old print and broadcast media, in favor of new media like X and podcasts that helped circumvent the traditional narrative gatekeepers that were stalwart in their opposition to Trump. That meant that it was also a huge victory for crypto and digital finance innovation, which also sought to bypass traditional financial intermediaries.


Continuing, I expected Trump to have a very active first year in office seeking to turbocharge the US economy. I expected that we would see unorthodox political appointments (many business people and few academics and Washington insiders), restrictions on illegal immigration and deportations of criminal aliens, ramping up of energy production, deregulation (especially of recent regulations), a focus on law and order, and a major effort to extend the tax reform of his previous administration.


Turning to the international context of Trump’s election, I said that I expected that Trump would move forward with imposing tariffs in a transactional manner, rise to the defense of US multinational firms, such as Apple and Tesla, against EU protectionist measures, and defend US social media (such as X) from EU government censorship and control. I expected the Trump team to engage meaningfully in financial and economic standard-setting bodies like the Basel-based Financial Stability Board, but on the basis of deference to comparable regulatory frameworks, not adherence to one-size-fits-all global standards. I expected a return to skepticism about global conventions and protocols that limit US action, but not about participation in global institutions themselves.


I concluded by saying,



America remains a big, complicated, dynamic and aspirational society. It has once again surprised the world with a political evolution and, in some ways, a revolution without bloodshed.


I believe it was Winston Churchill who said: “Democracy is the worst form of government, except (of course) all those other forms of government that have been tried from time to time.”


Despite all the blather that Donald Trump was a threat to democracy, what we saw earlier this week was American democracy in action—disruptive, thrilling, messy and chaotic—but democracy none the less.


For that, I am grateful. Thank you.







## And Again



In this same room a year before, I had wondered who I had reached and who I had offended with my provocative remarks about the Biden team’s crypto repression. This time, I knew from the mixed applause that there was some dismay with my message and, no doubt, with the messenger himself. The room was filled with regulators who were anxious about the impact of a new Trump administration.


Still, David Wright, ever diplomatic, called me back later that evening to discuss what to expect for crypto regulation in a second Trump administration.


I began by saying that although crypto may be seen as a niche interest, the election results were a vindication for the entire crypto industry, an industry that contributed a whopping $160m to the 2024 campaign.35 I said, “The crypto industry went all in on Team Trump. He cashed those checks. They helped him win. Now the bill is due. And the Trump team intends to deliver.”


I said that Trump had a detailed plan for crypto innovation, laid out in the July speech in Nashville. And he would have the support of Congress with more than 250 pro-crypto representatives elected to both houses. Next to Trump himself, crypto was a clear winner in the 2024 election.


I continued by saying that I expected Trump to move forward with some sort of early executive order instructing US regulators to honor his pledge not to interfere with citizens’ right to self-custody crypto and utilize self-hosted wallets for crypto transactions. And I expected an early executive order ending the Biden administration’s harassment of banks that lend to US-led digital asset innovation. As for regulatory agency leadership, I expected the Trump administration to move quickly to staff financial regulatory leadership positions with professionals who are overtly aligned with President Trump’s commitment to digital asset innovation and leadership. I thought that there would be fewer academics and more business people. I expected most of these appointments to be intelligent and well-qualified people, but who may be a little new at the political game. I told the group that I suspected they would come to like working with most of the Trump team, so “go easy on them at first.”


I said that I anticipated quick repeal of SAB 121 that was universally hated by the banks. Once it was gone, I expected traditional banks and broker-dealers to custody digital assets, regardless of whether they are securities. I also expected stablecoin legislation to advance quickly with White House support.


I concluded by saying that with these changes, the United States likely will indeed become the crypto capital of the world. I expected the story of crypto in 2025 under Trump to be less about the “crypto bros” and more about how quickly traditional financial institutions act to move into crypto, now that government bullying had ended. For some years, financial infrastructure providers like some of America’s big exchanges and service providers in accountancy and law had been sitting on the sidelines after warnings from the Biden administration not to engage with crypto. With Trump’s election, they would not wait for Chairman Gensler to leave the building before ramping up.


With that, my 2025 Cumberland Lodge presentations came to an end. I looked forward to a drink at the bar. There, that evening, I was delighted with the degree of camaraderie and select good cheer I encountered. Apparently, there were more than a few European financial regulatory leaders ready to see the end of Team Biden. Maybe I would be invited back next year after all.


Flying back to New York, I started to anticipate how the new Trump team needed to proceed to deliver on its crypto promises. I thought of it in three stages: First, ending the bad policies of the previous administration toward financial innovation. That phase could be achieved relatively easily by changing regulatory agency senior personnel. The debanking of crypto would stop by firing those at the OCC, FDIC, the Fed, and the SEC who were responsible for it. Regulation by enforcement would be stopped, and the repeal of SAB 121 would be enabled by new leadership. The second stage would be one of delivering on some specific and somewhat random promises from the campaign. That included creating a crypto stockpile, achieved by action of a new Treasury Secretary; ending capital gains taxes on crypto payment transactions (whereby someone buying a cup of coffee with crypto would have to calculate the capital gains associated with a particular token that was spent), requiring an act of Congress and formation of a crypto council, requiring White House action.







## Under Consideration



It was one thing to get rid of bad crypto policy and deliver on specific campaign promises; it was another thing—the hardest thing—to enact good crypto policy. For good policy to be durable, it had to be enacted through Congressional action and then implemented through public notice and comment, and rulemaking by the regulatory agencies. Congressional legislation for stablecoins and crypto token regulation was needed, and then paths to registration and trading at the SEC. Perhaps most needed were first-rate leaders at the various regulatory agencies.


In the 10 days following the 2024 election, several news outlets reported that I was under consideration by the Trump transition team to be nominated as SEC chairman.36 Friends and supporters urged me to throw my hat into the ring for consideration. Yet, I had already told Trump campaign co-chair Lutnick that I didn’t want to run another federal agency, and I had many reasons for not wanting to helm the SEC. First among them, perhaps, was that I would not have my former CFTC chief of staff, Mike Gill, to help me resuscitate the SEC. I knew that the agency was in the same shambolic state in 2024 as the CFTC found itself in 2013 after years of administration by Gary Gensler, when staff morale was bitterly low, defections were high, and talented human capital was hard to attract.37 By 2024, the SEC’s reputation was similarly tarnished by years of politicized enforcement actions and court-imposed sanctions for mendacious trial behavior.


Mike Gill served as CFTC chief of staff during my administration from 2017 to 2019. He was murdered one evening in January 2024 when crime in the District of Columbia was spiraling out of control. He was picking his wife up from work, as he did every night, and texted her to say that he was waiting in the car outside her office building, just seven blocks from the White House. As he sat in his idling Jeep Cherokee, an unhinged gunman opened the unlocked front passenger door and shot him in the head.38 Mike was pronounced brain dead and put on life support while family and friends said their final goodbyes and the hospital worked to find matches for his donor organs. He left behind his wife of 26 years and three fine and accomplished adult children.


Mike Gill lived a modest life of profound consequence—not through the pursuit of recognition, but through the steady, generous application of his many gifts in service to others. As a superb operational manager and outstanding lawyer, he brought order, integrity, and excellence to the institutions he served, including the CFTC, understanding better than most that democracy functions only as well as the dedicated professionals who sustain it from within. But the full measure of Mike cannot be taken in titles or accomplishments alone. He was a man who gathered stray souls—who noticed the overlooked, befriended the lonely, and made people feel seen. His wit and warmth could animate a room; his counsel could steady a troubled mind. Rooted in a faithful Christian life and devoted to his family with his whole heart, he modeled a kind of love that radiated outward—to friends, colleagues, and strangers alike—making every place he entered a little more humane, a little more whole.


I was and remain deeply shaken by the murder of Michael Gill. I put out a statement saying that “Mike’s life reflected everything that is good and true.” Donald Trump issued a statement commenting on the shooting and saying, “God Bless Michael and his family.”39 Expressions of sadness were received from the Governor of the Bank of England40 and senior regulators and colleagues across the globe.41 Mike’s murder took from Washington one of its finest public servants and robbed the world of a man who made others better simply by knowing him. What he built cannot be measured in policy or precedent—it lives instead in the people he shaped, the institutions he strengthened, and the lives he quietly, faithfully transformed, including mine.


In December 2025, I knew I didn’t have it in me to run another federal agency without my invaluable “Sancho Panza”42 by my side. Moreover, I knew there were better candidates than me to chair the SEC. The chief one was my good friend and Vanderbilt law school contemporary, Paul Atkins, who had prompted me into government service 10 years before. Atkins knew the SEC inside and out, knowledge that would be invaluable in bringing it back to life. He was also enormously respected by the agency staff, even if they did not uniformly agree with his deep-seated faith in free market capitalism.


After Vanderbilt, Atkins practiced law with the fine New York firm of Davis Polk & Wardwell. He made his way to the SEC in the 1990s, where he worked as chief of staff to then-Chair Richard Breeden and later as counsel to Breeden’s successor, Arthur Levitt. Atkins became a commissioner at the agency in 2002, serving until 2008. Afterward, he founded Patomak Global Partners, where Atkins advised financial service clients on complex public policy issues and risk management challenges.43


Atkins, furthermore, was a strong proponent of digital asset innovation. He and I had served together as advisors to Washington’s Digital Chamber. Paul was determined to end the SEC’s gratuitous lawsuits against crypto innovation and, rather, undertake the hard work of producing clear regulatory rules of the road for crypto. Like me, Atkins was not at all a reflexive opponent of market regulation but a proponent of regulatory proportionality: regulation should be fit for purpose and well-tailored for the public policy set out by the legislature, not the whim of the regulator. Respecting constitutional limits was essential to good government and measured regulatory action.


I spoke to Atkins following Trump’s victory and let him know that he had my support. To quash any rumors that I was interested in chairing the SEC, I tweeted:



A little over a week later, I did an afternoon business television broadcast with Charlie Gasparino, in which I promoted Atkins for SEC chair.44 I cited the need for “institutional knowledge” of the SEC and “a deep commitment to innovation.” A copy of the clip was forwarded to Mar-a-Lago. A week later, Donald Trump named Paul Atkins as his designee for SEC chairman.


Early in that same FoxBusiness interview above a chyron that read: “Giancarlo is lead contender for new ‘crypto czar’ role that’s being floated by Trump Transition Team,” I was asked, “[You will] go from CryptoDad to Crypto Czar, how does that happen?” I replied that “we’d see” and referred to the “active consideration” in Mar-a-Lago of how to get Trump’s crypto agenda accomplished and the right people to make it happen. I explained that the need for a so-called “Crypto Czar” went back to Trump’s July 2024 Nashville speech in which he laid out a series of steps45 necessary to make America the “crypto capital of the world,” including a crypto council, the ability to self-custody crypto, and a crypto reserve. I went on to say, “These things are hard to do” and “a lot of thought” needs to go into how to achieve them. I explained that accomplishing the promised actions “ranged across the board” and across the federal government. I said, “you need tax writers” in Congress to make sure that Trump’s promised moratorium on crypto transaction taxes was achieved; you’ll need the White House counsel’s office to write the executive orders “to end what is called Operation Chokepoint 2.0, the debanking of crypto”; you are going to need somebody to run the crypto council that candidate Trump promised during the campaign. Most importantly, you needed a White House liaison with both houses of Congress and the regulatory agencies to keep crypto legislation and agency rulemaking in line and on target. For these things, there needed to be “a White House point person, a ‘crypto Czar,’ if you will.”


In fact, I had been speaking privately to people with influence in the Trump transition effort about the need for a crypto czar. Early on December 5, I got a call from David Sacks, Silicon Valley entrepreneur, investor, and member of the famous “PayPal Mafia” (alongside Elon Musk and Peter Thiel). He told me that he would soon be announced as White House Crypto and AI Czar. Sacks kindly acknowledged that my name had been under active consideration, but that he had intervened, added the AI component, and obtained the position for himself. He kindly asked me if I would consider serving as his deputy for crypto. I said that I hadn’t had a boss in many years and I was probably too senior to be a deputy, but would be pleased to be a resource to him. Two weeks later we met for breakfast at the Four Seasons Hotel in Georgetown. I was immediately reassured by Sacks’ intelligence, political vision, and determination. I was now certain of three things: that Sacks would be an effective crypto czar, that I could not serve as a deputy, and that the White House was truly serious about making the United States “the crypto capital of the world.” All good.


During breakfast, Sacks shared an idea he had about hosting a grand “Crypto Ball” during the inauguration weekend. “It’ll be huge!” he said.


Alas, I wouldn’t be able to make it. I had a long scheduled appointment on Inauguration Day that I could not miss.


Let me now take you, dear reader, on a little narrative detour far away from Washington, DC, to an industrial park on the Swiss/Italian Border. There, let us consider the dangers of gullibility that too often accompany a triumphant return.

# Chapter 9 Bella Figura1




The Author’s Inauguration Day detour to a Swiss duty-free zone near Lake Como to consider the nature of youth, art, and naivete







## Youth



In CryptoDad, I recounted my family story, including how in the 1950s my American-born father, son of an immigrant brick mason, took advantage of his Italian language fluency and the post-war strength of the US dollar to enroll in one of Italy’s finest medical schools at the University of Padua.2


In December 1954, while returning home for Christmas, my father’s flight from Rome descended into New York’s Idlewild Airport, rammed into a pier, burst into flames, and hurtled into the waters of Jamaica Bay.3 Twenty-six passengers died. Miraculously, my father, then 23, was able to climb out of a broken section of the tail wing of the submerging Douglas DC-6 aircraft, swim through the icy waters of Jamaica Bay, and climb up a pier, one of only six survivors.4 Uninjured but in shock, my father was rushed to a Queens hospital, where he was reunited with his parents and his then-girlfriend, my mother.


My mother and father were married the following year. Together in 1956, they took up residence in the Italian college town of Padua, where my father finished his doctorate in medicine. During school breaks, they toured around post-war Europe in a 1956 Type 14 Karmann Ghia roadster.5 Blond, pretty, and very American in manner, my mother Ella Jane stood out both on and off campus. She attracted the attention of a dapper older bachelor who occupied the penthouse of the modernist Biri building in which they lived. Known as Il Commendatore, the “commander” cut a grand figure in the neighborhood with a red cravat loosely tied around a white wingtip collar, gray spats over his shoes, and a silver-tipped walking stick. With courtly gentilezza,6 the Commendatore would greet my young mother as she walked her Cocker Spaniel puppy, Nerone. In time, the Commendatore took to calling her, “mia bella Ella!”







## Art



One afternoon the Commendatore explained that he was a private collector of Impressionist art and invited my mother to see the paintings in his penthouse. My mother demurred, saying that she couldn’t possibly come without her husband. The Commendatore indicated he understood and, in a grand gesture, said that he would throw a dinner party in my parents’ honor to meet the local grandees. He did so a week later. In between courses of prosciutto and fresh figs, polenta and shrimp, and bigoli, the thick local pasta, served with duck and prepared by the Commendatore’s resident chef, my parents chatted with a baronessa and several cavalieres, dottores, and professores.7 While enjoying bites of food and sips of the dry local Soave white wine, my mother eyed the artwork by French painters such as Pissarro, Monet, Renoir, Morisot, and Italian artists de Chirico, Modigliani, and painters of the Macchiaioli movement. Canvases covered the walls from ceiling to floor, and many others were simply stacked against tables and sofas. In a small bathroom, my mother examined works by Italian painters Morandi and de Pisis. She told the Commendatore how beautiful they were. He said, “Oh, do you like the de Pisis? He died recently. I knew Maestro Filippo de Pisis when he lived in Venice. He was very extravagant. Do you know he had a talking parrot and two personal gondoliers on 24-hour duty? He would have loved to meet you!”


One morning after, my parents found a canvas, wrapped in newspaper, leaning against their apartment door with a note thanking them for being the Commendatore’s dinner guests. The oil painting was signed “de Pisis.” Over the next two years, the Commendatore gave them more paintings from his collection, one or two signed Morandi and eight beautiful landscapes, still lives, and portraits signed “de Pisis.” One was a portrait in pencil sketched on the back of a handbill for a 1930s exhibition in Florence of De Pisis works. Another was a small oil painting of London’s Westminster Bridge signed “de Pisis” that they received after another entertaining dinner party. This one was inscribed on the back, “Con notte affettuosità—alla simpatica signora Ella—questa opera del grande maestro Filippo de Pisis.” (“For a lovely night—to the charming Mrs. Ella—this work is by the great master, Phillip de Pisis.”)



Westminster Bridge, in the manner of Filippo de Pisis



My father graduated from medical school in the spring of 1958, and my parents returned to the United States. Tucked in their luggage were a dozen or so artworks received from Il Commendatore. The paintings were a source of great pride for my family and were prominently displayed on the walls of my parents’ homes for decades. My parents stayed in touch with Il Commendatore, exchanging Christmas cards and visiting him in Italy from time to time. He died in 1976, and I never met him, but I recall the smile in my mother’s eyes whenever she spoke about him, even in her final years.


My parents died over the past decade. In settling their estates, my brothers and I learned that none of the works received from Il Commendatore were listed in the official catalogue raisonne’ of the artists, including all eight works by de Pisis. Not a big deal, we were told by the New York appraisers. Because Il Commendatore’s collection was a private one, he may have received the works directly from the artists and they were never shown publicly. We needed to have the works reviewed by a European jury of experts and declared authentic. How much would that cost? Several tens of thousands. That much? No need. We’ll just enjoy the paintings as they are.


In October 2019, a few months after completion of my chairmanship of the CFTC, I was in Italy as a guest of Università Cattolica del Sacro Cuore, the Catholic University of Milan, to speak at an international conference on the subject of financial derivatives and their usage under Catholic teaching. I was on a panel with Cardinal Peter Turkson, widely regarded as one of Africa’s most influential Catholic figures and head of the Vatican’s Dicastery for Promoting Integral Human Development, that was influential in a recent Vatican Bollettino on the topic of financial derivatives (discussed later in Chapter 9). The day after the conference, my wife and I were enjoying a stroll in the Piazza del Duomo, when we spotted a giant sign hanging several stories against the side of Milan’s Museo del Novecento (“museum of the twentieth century”). The sign announced a major retrospective exhibition of Filippo de Pisis. We joined the line, bought tickets, and toured the extensive exhibit. By the end of two hours of careful study, I was certain that my parents’ paintings deserved placement right alongside the dozens of beautiful landscapes, still lives, and portraits by del grande Maestro.


First, we had to get our paintings declared authentic, a process that turned out to be an extended and expensive task. Fortunately, a fine New York area art conservator, Crosnier Restorations, was able to confirm that the de Pisis canvases, oils, and framing were appropriate for the time of the artist’s career. We retained an Italian art specialist lawyer who regularly advised the famous Venice art exhibition, the Venice Biennale. He was experienced in working with the Associazione per Filippo de Pisis, the only organization that could authenticate de Pisis works. Of course, they would only do so after a physical examination of the works and review of extensive historical data, including ownership by my family. (Shame the provenance of the works was not recorded on a blockchain.) And, no, the jury would not come to the United States to do so. Shipping the works to Italy, however, presented a problem. Under Italian law, the Italian government could seize works of national patrimony if they had originally left Italy without a proper export permit. If my father had obtained such a permit in 1958, I certainly could not locate a copy of it. Moreover, the Associazione per Filippo de Pisis was required to alert the Italian government if and when they declared the works to be authentic. So, we were caught in a Catch-22. If we didn’t get the paintings seen by the Italian experts, then they couldn’t be authenticated. If they were seen and authenticated by the experts, then they could be seized by the Italian government. We could lose either way.


But wait, in typical Italian fashion, there was a workaround. The paintings could be shipped to a special facility in a duty-free zone on the Swiss side of the Italian border where the Milan-based art experts could come and examine the works. And, because the facility was in a duty-free zone, the works would technically never enter Europe for purposes of import duties and certainly not enter Italy where they could be seized. After the examination, they could be whisked right back to America.


Now, all of this would take a long time to arrange. The shipping process was complicated and painstaking. The art experts of the Associazione per Filippo de Pisis only gathered infrequently and by appointment. It would also cost a bundle: the lawyers, the art experts, the use of the special facility, and the museum-quality fine art packaging and shipping. Fortunately, my brother Michael was graciously willing to share the costs, believing that it was worth the bother and the expense. Since the Museo del Novecento 2019 retrospective, prices of de Pisis works had jumped in price. Once authenticated, our de Pisis collection could be worth millions of dollars. And of far greater value, we agreed, was validating our patrimony for our children and confirming the grand affection for our mother of the dashing Il Commendatore.


In late summer 2024, we learned the date of the examination: Monday, January 20, 2025. The Associazione per Filippo de Pisis would travel from Milan by train north to the first stop across the Swiss border, where we would meet in the rail stop of Punto Franco di Chiasso. That day would be an ordinary Monday in Switzerland, but a Federal holiday, Martin Luther King Day, in the United States. It would also turn out to be Inauguration Day for the second Trump administration, an event that I didn’t even contemplate when I agreed to the scheduling.


Part of the reason that I readily accepted the examination date was because I was already going to be in Switzerland. I have become a recurrent participant in an annual crypto conference that takes place the second week in January each year. CFC St. Moritz is one of the world’s premier digital assets and blockchain conferences for the most senior echelon of crypto investors and decision-makers. It takes place at the five-star Suvretta House Hotel nestled in the Engadin region of the Swiss Alps. The January 20 date would allow my wife and me to attend the conference in St. Moritz and then make our way to the Swiss lake city of Lugano near the Italian border the day before the art experts arrived.


On that gray and damp day, my wife and I stepped under the portico of Lugano’s once-grand Hotel Spendide Royal into a hotel car for the 40-minute drive along and across the southern leg of Lake Lugano and further south to the town of Chiasso near Lake Como. There, alongside an enormous rail terminus was the Punto Franco duty-free zone. We pulled up to a crossing beam beside a guard house and presented our passports to the uniformed guard and explained our purpose. After closely looking us over, he raised the barrier and let us through. After several further bottlenecks, we found the art examination building, where our driver left us.


As we were early, we found seats in the lobby and waited. Before long, an associate of our law firm, a smart, well-dressed consigliere arrived. She explained that she had spoken recently to the Associazione’s director and confirmed that they had received the Crosnier assessment and were looking forward to the examination. Minutes later, the experts arrived. What I noticed first was the smiles on their faces. They seemed to anticipate finding some long undiscovered additions to the de Pisis’ catalog. Good. So were we.


One of the most recognized opening credit sequences in television history is the opening of the spy genre spoof, Get Smart. The main character, Maxwell Smart, goes through a series of vault-like doors and drops through the floor of a phone booth to enter his secret spy agency headquarters. In a similar fashion, my wife and I, our lawyer, and the de Pisis experts made our way through a series of steely warehouse doors, narrow loading docks, a creaky service elevator, and finally through a large, heavy vault door. Along the way, I chatted in my spotty Italian with the experts, a nephew and grand-niece of de Pisis, plus an acclaimed art professor from an Italian University. We emerged into a sunlit room under a huge glass atrium ceiling. Arrayed on a set of easels and drafting tables were our eight oil paintings.


Very politely, but disappointingly, my wife and I and our lawyer were asked to leave the room. Back we made our way through the vault door, the service elevator, the loading dock, and the warehouse doors to the waiting room. There we waited and waited. I could almost hear the famous “Think” theme of another American television staple, Jeopardy, as we sat and waited for the judges’ decision. An hour went by then another hour, then 15 minutes more. Finally, the three judges reentered the waiting room.


The smiles were gone, replaced with looks of serious concern. I stood back as the experts circled our lawyer. I couldn’t make out most of what they discussed, but I comprehend enough Italian to know that the word “falso” meant “false” and “frode” sounded a lot like fraud. I also was aware that sometimes art juries were sued by art collectors if they failed to authenticate questionable works. I wanted to know what the experts observed, and I didn’t want them to get defensive and clam up worrying that I might get litigious. I said to my lawyer, “Please translate what I say.” Then, I said,



Signore e signori, please understand that my family and I have sent you these oil paintings that have been in my family for 70 years for one reason and one reason only—to know the truth of their origin. We will have no issue with you if you find they are not authentic. Their sentimental value to us will be unchanged whether the Maestro painted them or not. But we have come a long way at great expense to know the truth and for that we can only turn to you. Please be candid with us.




The jurors nodded their heads and displayed some degree of relief. The older one, the nephew of de Pisis, explained that they had strong doubts as to the works’ authenticity. There were indications in the way clouds were painted, in the earth tones of mountain scenes, in the palettes of flowers and, most importantly, in the scroll of the de Pisis signature. They were certain that the painter of all eight works was the same artist, but suspected it was not Maestro de Pisis. However, the experts were not prepared to make a definitive determination of inauthenticity at that time. They would go back to their office, do some research, and write to me within the next six weeks with their final decision. With that and, I detected, true disappointment, they left the building. My wife and I and our lawyer left soon afterward.


My wife and I departed Switzerland for Italy the next day by train, where I did some business in Willkie’s office in Milan and enjoyed risotto al gorgonzola that evening with some dear friends at the fabulous Ratanà Restaurant near Porta Venezia. The next morning, we caught the train to Padua and settled into a small hotel near the university. That afternoon, we attended Mass at revered St. Anthony’s basilica, walked through the old university streets, and visited the ancient medical school. In contrast, the med students looked especially young and contemporary. I imagined my mother and father aged 24–26 hurrying along these same streets in the post-war years of the 1950s. My young parents imagined themselves worldly and sophisticated to be there, but at best they were adventuresome, enthusiastic, and very naïve. How could they have believed otherwise than that the gifts from this kindly and seemingly successful art collector were anything but original works of art by great Italian artists?


A month or so later, my Italian lawyer called me on the phone. The experts had done their research and had reached a definitive conclusion. The works were all forgeries. They had been painted by a well-known and prolific 1950s Milanese forger, not an uncommon practice in post-war Europe.


Not only would our paintings not be listed in the definitive de Pisis catalogue raisonne’, now they would be included in a list of counterfeit works so that any future buyers or gallery owners would be forewarned. Worse, the experts suspected that the forger’s agent to sell the fraudulent paintings—the forger’s “fence”—was none other than Il Commendatore!







## Naivete



I reflected on this experience and our 70-year family story. I know my mother would have been pained to hear the news about her debonair Commendatore. I could understand how my parents as young adults could get caught up in the excitement of a seemingly accomplished and worldly art collector giving them some tasty scraps from his huge collection.


I know I also got caught up in the excitement of the possibility that the paintings’ rediscovery would dramatically increase their value. It is so easy to think that something that cost so little could increase in value so much. We are all susceptible to it from time to time. Yet, when you are older, you have the experience of having fallen for it to make you more cautious. Something “too good to be true” almost never is. When you are young, you haven’t yet experienced it.


I had to miss Washington’s “Crypto Ball” and the inauguration ceremony itself to be reminded of the dangers of covetousness and gullibility and the virtues of discernment and caution. Hence my focus on the architecture of crypto, not on the “number go up” side of crypto. I know the speculative side is equally present and very, very attractive to millions of people. It is what generates headlines and interest and, certainly, investment. Yet, I know that “number go up” must someday inevitably reverse into “number go down.” When it does, there will be tears.


That same weekend we were in Switzerland, in fact the same night as guests at the Crypto Ball rubbed elbows with Washington’s political elites, President Trump launched a crypto “meme” coin called $TRUMP.8 As with other meme coins, there’s no underlying product or service available. Over the next 48 hours, the coin’s market cap climbed to $14 billion, with 80% of the coins held by the Trump organization and affiliates. On Sunday came the launch of another meme coin named after the first lady that also spiked in price, surpassing $2 billion in value. Since that night, both the Trump and Melania coins have dropped significantly from their highs. The market cap of $TRUMP is now below $1 billion.9


Earlier, I described meme coins as lottery tickets sold at a carnival. At best, they are just collectables, something to tell your friends about. Value is entirely in the eye of the beholder, like a painting.


Whatever one’s politics, however, it is extraordinary for a sitting head of state to launch a meme token in which affiliated entities hold the overwhelming majority of supply. It is not unfair to ask whether it should ever happen at all. Entrepreneurs do not easily stop being entrepreneurs just because they temporarily serve in government. They continue to look for ways to sell and market things and make money. Yet, perhaps they should hold themselves to a higher standard once they hold the public trust. Merchandising public service diminishes it. The architecture of digital assets is not well served by conflating genuine financial innovation with monetizing the celebrity of elected office. Public servants should aspire to be more like Abraham Lincoln than Il Commendatore.


You get the good with the bad in politics. The rest of us need to stay clear-sighted. My trip to Switzerland reminded me of that.






## Notes




- 1 An Italian aesthetic of projecting an image of success, composure, and dignity pursuant to an ideal in which form is as important as substance. See: Luigi Barzini, The Italians (1965).

- 4 Oregon Statesman, “26 Dead as Italian Plane Hits Pier at New York Airport,” December 19, 1954, p. 1, at: https://oregonnews.uoregon.edu/lccn/sn85042470/1954-12-19/ed-1/seq-1/
