# Chapter 10 Triumphant Return




The Author’s eyewitness view of the White House’s 2025 Crypto Summit, its vindication of the work of leaders of the digital assets industry and his proposal to use an eighteenth-century naval tactic to combat twenty-first-century crypto crime





On Friday evening, February 28, 2025, I was relaxing on the couch at home after a long and demanding week. Two days before, I had published an op-ed1 cowritten with Chris Perkins, former US Marine Corps Captain and today head of Franklin Templeton’s crypto unit. The article proposed drawing upon a rarely used Constitutional provision to combat state-sponsored cyber hackers. Our article was drawing a lot of attention.


I was drained from hustling to fill key gaps in Willkie Farr & Gallagher’s increasingly recognized crypto law practice that was now in hot demand since the incoming Trump administration was rapidly lifting Biden-era shackles on innovation. I was losing to the SEC Mike Selig, a protégé and one of Willkie’s most crypto-native partners. Selig had been one of my first law clerks at the CFTC from September 2014 to August 2015. His research on Bitcoin contributed to my support for the agency’s 2015 determination that, as the world’s first digital commodity, Bitcoin was subject to the CFTC’s exclusive US jurisdiction. Selig went on to build a well-regarded digital asset legal practice and was one of the first hires I made in building Willkie Digital Works. Selig’s crypto legal chops led him to be recruited by the new Trump-appointed SEC leadership as chief counsel to the Crypto Task Force and advisor to incoming Chairman Paul Atkins.


Within weeks of Selig’s departure, another highly qualified law partner, Neal Kumar, left to become general counsel at Polymarket, the prediction contract marketplace founded by Shayne Coplan that I talked about earlier, and Justin Browder, a fine lawyer and good friend, was recruited away by a law firm that sought to replicate the success of Willkie Digital Works. Fortunately, we were able to refill our legal capacity with highly qualified lawyers, including savvy Tamika Bent, a former advisor to CFTC Commissioner Kristin Johnson, and the extraordinarily talented Chelsea Pizzola, another of my former CFTC law clerks, who went on to be deputy chief of staff and counsel to my successor, CFTC Chairman Heath Tarbert.


Easing into the weekend with an ice-filled glass of Braulio amaro in one hand, the mobile phone in my other hand lit up with a tweet from President Trump. It announced that the White House would be hosting a Crypto Policy Summit the following Friday, March 7.


I typed out and hit send on a text to White House Crypto Czar David Sacks: “David—Kudos on initiating the upcoming Crypto Summit. I would be most grateful for an invitation to attend. Much to discuss. Thanks.” I didn’t expect an immediate response. Yet, a few hours later, Sacks gave my text a thumbs up.


I was flooded over the next few days with inquiries from colleagues, clients, and journalists asking if I would be attending and seeking my help in getting an invite. It quickly turned into the hottest ticket in and out of the cryptosphere. By midweek, reporters, led by the irrepressible Ellie Terrett, were putting out names of who had received coveted invitations. The guests appeared to be limited to CEOs who had contributed several million or more dollars to Trump’s presidential campaign. I wondered if I should still expect an invitation. On Thursday, March 6, the invitation arrived.


At 9:30 a.m. on Friday, March 7, a bright and warm late winter’s day, I arrived at Willkie’s DC office in downtown Washington. After meeting with a colleague and making some client phone calls, I walked east on K Street to the St. Regis Hotel where I greeted former Acting Comptroller of the Currency Brian Brooks. Like me, Brooks had been a strong proponent of digital asset innovation during the first Trump administration and would be attending the day’s crypto summit hosted by the second Trump administration. Not long after, I was joined by Chad Cascarilla, founder and CEO of Paxos Trust Company, where I served as an independent director.



As Cascarilla and I ate a light lunch, he filled me in on the commercial restrictions being placed on it by its primary regulator, the New York Department of Financial Services (NYDFS). One could have hoped that New York Governor Hochul would have gotten the hint over the past few years as one after another crypto investment firms left New York City for Miami, Nashville, or Austin. But Hochul missed that sign and now seemed to vie with Oregon and Massachusetts to be a last holdout of Biden-era crypto antipathy.


Cascarilla and I hopped into a waiting black Chevy Suburban that drove us several blocks to the White House. In addition to the usual uniformed guards, there was a friendly female protocol officer to welcome us with a cheerful hello. Cascarilla and I presented our credentials at the White House gates and were waved through. Unlike so many of my previous official visits as CFTC chairman to the White House’s famous West Wing, we entered the White House through the ceremonial East Wing, leading into the residence itself. Clearly, this was no ordinary event.


Once inside, uniformed US Marine officers directed us up the wide stairwell to the ground floor, the primary entry point for visitors to various public functions. It encompasses the grand East Room, renowned for hosting state dinners and receptions, as well as the elegant Green Room and Blue Room, used for official events and meetings. Mingling in the White House’s grand entrance hall, we were served hors d’oeuvres, wine, and refreshments as we greeted the other invited guests.


After a short while, we were ushered into the magnificent State Dining Room. Featuring elaborate chandeliers and gilded moldings, the State Dining Room had been the setting for historic dinners and receptions. Overlooking the room was a large and uniquely beautiful oil painting of President Lincoln. Beneath it was a wide horseshoe-shaped table. Each seating contained blotter, notepads, agenda materials, and speaker squawk boxes. Name cards indicated the designated seating arrangements, with the Presidential seal indicating President Trump would sit in the center under the contemplative gaze of Abraham Lincoln.


Another protocol officer directed me to my seat, which, to my surprise and honor, was alongside the president in the center of the horseshoe, beside several of his cabinet officers: Treasury Secretary Bessent, Commerce Secretary Lutnick, SBA Administrator Kelly Loeffler, House Whip and (Congressional Crypto Caucus leader) Tom Emmer, Crypto Czar David Sacks, and his crypto lieutenant, Bo Hines. We all shook hands and said hello.


Other than the government officials at the head of the table and a few others arrayed behind, all other 30 or so seated guests seemed to be celebrated crypto moguls. Among the notables were Michael Saylor of MicroStrategy, Brian Armstrong of Coinbase, Tyler and Cameron Winklevoss of Gemini, Vlad Tenev of Robinhood, Arjun Sethi of Kraken, Sergey Nazarov of Chainlink, Matt Huang of Paradigm, Kris Marszalek of Crypto.com, Fred Thiel of Marathon Digital, Mike Belshe of BitGo, Jonathan Steinberg of Wisdom Tree, Zach Witkoff of World Liberty Financial, and seated to my right, Brad Garlinghouse of Ripple.


Notably attending the meeting and seated behind the guests were a number of Administration officials, including the acting Chairman of the CFTC, Caroline Pham, and SEC Commissioner and “CryptoMom,” Hester Peirce. Symbolically, these officials had no microphones and were there to listen. Disappointingly, neither Federal bank regulators nor representatives of the Federal Reserve were present to hopefully learn.


Also in attendance was former CFTC Commissioner Brian Quintenz, whose February 2025 nomination by President Trump to be CFTC Chair I had publicly supported. Months later, Quintenz’ nomination was officially withdrawn by the White House.2 I continue to believe that Brian Quintenz would have made a fine Chairman of the CFTC.


Looking around the room, I chuckled silently. I imagined that most of the guests (and certainly some members of the Cabinet) were themselves Democrats or former ones and would not have been out of place at a 2020 Napa Valley political fundraiser for Nancy Pelosi. Yet, here they were thanking and complimenting the Trump administration for not only listening but acting on their concerns.


As we were taking our seats, several White House ushers wheeled into the room a large object covered by a drape. They placed the mysterious thing in the center of the room and departed.


David Sacks gave opening remarks and listed the remarkably long list of official actions taken or being taken by the new administration regarding crypto. He thanked the guests for attending and asked them to speak one by one about areas of concern and, especially, raise new issues that the administration had not yet addressed but should be focused on. Michael Saylor went first and then, one by one, each guest spoke. Each person was allowed to speak without being on the clock or cut off. Some of the issues raised were ones that the administration was already well focused on, such as debanking. The subject that seemed to be most articulated was the need for Congress to codify into law the new acceptance of crypto innovation so that it would not change again if another hostile administration returned to power.


When it was my time to speak, I took advantage of Sacks’ instruction to propose an initiative that no one had discussed. I said,



I was honored to serve as CFTC Chairman during President Trump’s first administration when we greenlighted Bitcoin futures—the first US regulated market of any kind for crypto. Seven years later, that marketplace is deep, liquid, transparent, well policed for fraud and manipulation and priced in US dollars. I was supported at the time by CFTC chairman nominee Brian Quintenz and since then by acting Chair Pham, both of whom are here today.


I want to thank President Trump, along with David Sacks, Bo Hines, Secretary Bessent, Secretary Lutnick, Administrator Loeffler, Representative Emmer and others for ending the American government’s war on crypto.


But while the former administration’s war on crypto has ended, the war on the US crypto industry continues to be waged by foreign, state-sponsored cyber hacking organizations.


North Korea’s cyber-crime organization, the Lazarus Group has recently staged one of the largest hacks in crypto history. Last year alone, hackers stole over $2 billion, half of which can be directly traced to Lazarus, which diverts stolen digital assets to funding North Korea’s nuclear weapons program. These adversaries stand in the way of the Trump administration’s plans to make America the “crypto capital of the world.”


I recently penned an op-ed with Chris Perkins, a former Marine Corps intelligence officer. We propose that Congress use its powers under Article I, Section 8 of the Constitution to authorize President Trump to issue “Letters of Marque and Reprisal,” effectively enlisting private companies to hack foreign adversaries on the behalf of the US government. It would provide a low-cost, flexible, and effective option to counter state-sponsored cyber hacking. It is time to unleash the awesome creativity and computational power of the American firms represented here today to fight back and seize stolen crypto property taken by foreign adversaries.


Privateering has a colorful history in the United States, including the legendary exploits of privateers like John Paul Jones during the American Revolution and Thomas Boyle of the War of 1812. I understand that President Trump recently hung President Madison’s portrait in the Oval Office. The president might quite like the idea of being the first president since James Madison to unleash the power of the US private sector on America’s digital adversaries.




With that, Commerce Secretary Lutnick gave me a warm smile; Congressional Whip Tom Emmer requested a copy of the op-ed, and Treasury Secretary Bessent asked me to brief the US Treasury staff on the specifics of our proposal.


Moments later, President Trump entered the room. Everyone rose with a standing ovation. He shook many hands as he walked to his seat and thanked the guests for their warm welcome. He then invited the White House press corps inside and allowed them to listen and photograph the proceedings. In his remarks, he introduced his cabinet members and acknowledged the prominence of the guests. He said that his team would not use the meeting to announce new initiatives, but to review their current program and consider additional actions.



Trump then said that he had a surprise. He instructed that the large object in the center of the room be unveiled. Uncovered was the gleaming gold, FIFA Club World Cup trophy, created by Tiffany & Co.3 The president explained that the trophy had been presented to him in the Oval Office a short while earlier by the president of Fédération Internationale de Football Association (FIFA). The White House ceremony marked the upcoming 2025 Club Football World Cup that would take place in the United States that summer.


Trump explained that, as they were wheeling out the trophy at the end of the Oval Office ceremony, he told the staff to take it upstairs to the State Dining Room to show the guests at the Crypto Summit. He noted that the trophy was “one of a kind,” with a special feature—it opens with a golden key. He said, “Go ahead and show everyone.” An attendant used the gold key to unlock the trophy, whereupon it expanded to include three concentric rings around a central disk laser-engraved with vivid depictions of the game’s history and inscriptions in 13 languages and Braille.4


Trump said proudly, “There is only one of these trophies and only one key to open it. I thought you would like to see that. I understand that crypto also uses unique keys, so this trophy is a symbol of that.”


The president then asked the guests to share some of their thoughts. Spotting Cameron and Tyler Winklevoss, Trump said, “There’s the handsome crypto twins! What do you guys think of what we’re doing?” One of Winklevoss brothers replied, “Mr. President, we are very grateful to you and your administration for everything you are doing for crypto innovation in America. It is a total ‘180’ from the previous administration, which undermined American leadership with outright hostility to digital asset innovation.”  The other brother then said, “If you had asked me a few months before the election, I would have told you that depending on the vote, we were either going to succeed or were going to jail. We were either going to the White House or going to the ‘big house.’ We didn’t know which one.” Everyone in the room laughed.


Trump roared, “Me too! Me too! You know, they tried to put me in jail! They tried everything to put me in jail. But the American people didn’t want that. Had the election gone the other way, I guess some of us might be in jail. We’d be in jail together. But, fortunately, we won and, because of that, we are all here now. We’re all here in the White House!”


With that remark, any final reticence of the guests seemed to be dispelled. The crypto industry and the leader of the free world were one. Loathing and persecution had brought them together. Their interests were aligned. Their goals were the same: survival and success. A few other guests spoke, largely thanking President Trump for his championing of the burgeoning digital asset industry. A sense of relief and excitement flowed through the room. The final winter’s ice melted away.


Perhaps a bit more than 30 minutes after his arrival, an aide whispered something into Trump’s ear, and he stood up. He said that he was sorry that he could not stay longer, but he had to attend to other matters. He said that he hoped that members of his team could stay longer. He again thanked everyone for attending, worked his way along the table shaking hands and smiling, and then slipped out to a rousing round of applause.


Not long afterward, the meeting adjourned. The group departed the White House in twos and threes, many headed to a reception being hosted elsewhere by the crypto trading platform, Coinbase. There, they shared their observations and enthusiasm with eager members of the press, members of Congress, and trade association officials. The buzz was real.


Looking back on the very first White House Crypto Summit, it was clear that its purpose was to make a statement. Situated in the White House residence, under the gaze of Abraham Lincoln, with all the pomp and circumstance of a state visit and welcomed by the president himself, who stayed for 30 minutes, and key members of his cabinet who stayed far longer, this summit sent a message to the participants and their supporters and a message to regulators and other branches of the US government.


Like that of the “Great Emancipator,” Abraham Lincoln, under the inspiring portrait of whom Donald Trump sat, the message was simple and clear: “I have heard your pleas. Your persecution is over, and your cause has been vindicated. Go forward and flourish.”





## Policy at Start-up Speed



If there is one common denominator of leaders of the second Trump administration, it is that they are self-made individuals, many of whom are entrepreneurs. Their propensity to self-start is boosted by government service where they march to an increasingly fast beat. For those in Trump’s second term, that fast march began to beat at noon on Inauguration Day, January 20, 2025. The march did indeed proceed as I had anticipated. First, some particularly bad public policies of the previous administration, like debanking, regulation by enforcement, and preventing crypto custody through dubious accounting rules, were ended. This was done through leadership changes at the Federal Reserve Board and financial market regulators. Not wishing to be “fired on day one,” Gary Gensler resigned from the SEC a few minutes before Trump was sworn in.


Taking their place were nominees and acting regulatory heads accustomed to taking action and adept at moving the levers of administrative power, especially in favor of crypto innovation. They included Acting SEC Chairman Mark Uyeda, who immediately reshuffled the SEC’s crypto enforcement effort, rescinded SAB 121, and formed an SEC Crypto Task Force, with Mike Selig as its chief counsel. Earlier, Trump had named my friend, long-standing financial technology and crypto innovation advocate, Paul Atkins, as SEC chairman, subject to Senate confirmation. Trump also named direct and determined Commissioner Caroline Pham as acting chair of the CFTC. She moved immediately to reduce the agency’s cost structure and redirect its enforcement efforts. She addressed open issues under the 2010 Dodd-Frank Act and initiated the CFTC’s Crypto Sprint to advance oversight of digital asset markets.


The same pattern took place at the banking agencies, with the experienced Travis Hill becoming Acting Chairman of the FDIC and immediately announcing “a more open-minded approach to innovation,” and the capable Rodney Hood was appointed Acting Comptroller of the Currency, pending the confirmation of Jonathan Gould as Comptroller. Gould has a strong crypto background, having served as the top lawyer for blockchain business BitFury, where he once stated: “Blockchain technology represents a generational opportunity to not only build stronger financial services systems, but fundamentally reshape a number of industries globally.”5 And, in perhaps the most important financial leadership positions of all, President Trump named Howard Lutnick as commerce secretary and Scott Bessent as treasury secretary, both of whom are long-standing supporters of blockchain’s transformative potential, particularly in modernizing financial systems.


At some point I was expecting the Trump drumbeat to slacken. It didn’t. Three days after his inauguration, Trump signed a Digital Assets Executive Order6 establishing a President’s Working Group on Digital Asset Markets chaired by David Sacks that included the Secretary of the Treasury, the Chairman of the Securities and Exchange Commission, and the heads of other relevant departments and agencies. Among other things, the order revoked various Biden crypto hampering actions, called for crypto expertise beyond the federal government, initiated a study of the creation of a national crypto reserve, and sought recommendations for “regulatory and legislative proposals that advance the policies established in this order.” Less than two weeks later, Sacks held a press conference coinciding with the release of the draft of the Senate’s GENIUS Act, alongside companion House legislation for federal stablecoin licensing and regulation, which I described earlier.


Meanwhile, the SEC’s new Crypto Task Force got to work under the stewardship of “CryptoMom” SEC Commissioner Hester Peirce, a great champion of financial technology innovation and investor empowerment. On February 4, 2025, Peirce outlined key priorities for the task force, chief of which is to clarify the application of federal securities laws to digital assets.7 Undertaking this task was a remarkable turnaround for the SEC, which had previously responded to an industry petition seeking clarity on when and how securities laws apply to cryptocurrency with a curt response that clarity was not needed, the agency had higher priority agenda items, and it preferred to gather more information before engaging in rulemaking.8


Ably assisted by Mike Selig, the task force set out to establish “clear regulatory lines” by drawing upon input from a wide range of individuals and entities. On February 21, 2025, Commissioner Peirce issued a statement inviting public input on a series of issues related to crypto assets and blockchain technology.9 The task force also focused on removing the previous administration’s makeshift use of enforcement actions in crypto, dropping dubious and widely criticized cases against Coinbase, Ripple, Kraken, Consensys, Cumberland/DRW, Crypto.com, and others.


While the pace and redirection of the work of the SEC and its task force has been remarkable and, perhaps, unprecedented in the agency’s history, it has not deviated far from the agency’s core mission. Under the Biden administration, the SEC seemed to take the view that any asset represented in digital form was per se a security. The new SEC rejected that expansive definition of “security” and said that any individual financial instrument must be specifically analyzed as to its appropriate treatment. New Chairman Atkins said bluntly, “I believe that most crypto tokens trading today are not themselves securities.”10


On April 11, 2025, I went down to Washington to attend a Crypto Task Force roundtable, “Between a Block and a Hard Place: Tailoring Regulation for Crypto Trading.”11 Assembled were a panel of experienced SEC hands, well-versed in the minutiae of SEC law and regulation. Yet, what struck me was that they seemed to view crypto as just some new alternative asset class. They generally struggled with how crypto tokens would trade beside traditional securities. They seemed unaware that emerging technology companies like Bluprynt, founded by the brilliant Georgetown Law Professor Chris Brummer, were building tokenized regulatory compliance layers that financial institutions, stablecoin issuers, and real-world digital asset platforms would adopt to operate lawfully at scale. The assembled securities lawyers did not really seem to anticipate that within a decade most, if not all, new issuances of securities would likely be a native form as on-chain tokens that would trade at a significant premium to those remaining securities that were not tokenized and on-chain. From talking to Mike Selig afterward, I knew that the task force anticipated that reality, but it seemed that the securities bar of the law profession still had some way to go.


A month later, now-confirmed SEC Chairman Atkins said, “[j]ust as the shift to digital audio revolutionized the music industry, the migration to on-chain securities has the potential to remodel aspects of the securities market by enabling entirely new methods of issuing, trading, owning, and using securities …. Blockchain technology holds the promise to allow for a broad swath of novel use cases for securities, fostering new kinds of market activities that many of the Commission’s legacy rules and regulations do not contemplate today.”


On July 22, the White House released its Digital Asset Market Report.12 It set out five key goals. The first was positioning the United States as the leader in digital asset markets by establishing a fit-for-purpose market structure framework to protect consumers and support economic growth and innovation. This included expanding the CFTC’s authority over spot markets for digital commodities like Bitcoin and Ethereum. Secondly, the report called for the end of government actions that debanked the crypto industry, while allowing banks to modernize with the support of digital asset innovation. Third, the report called for strengthening the international role of the US dollar by licensure and regulation of USD-based stablecoins and combatting illicit finance by providing clarity regarding the Bank Secrecy Act obligations and reporting. Fourth, the report called for the abandonment of “regulation by enforcement” with a refocus of enforcement efforts on terrorists, drug cartels, and other bad actors; Finally, it called for fairness and predictability in digital asset taxation.


A week later, SEC Chairman Atkins spoke about the need for American leadership in what he termed the “Digital Finance Revolution.”13 To achieve it, Atkins announced “Project Crypto”—a commission-wide initiative to modernize securities regulation to fulfill President Trump’s vision of the United States as the “crypto capital of the world.”14 Positioning it as a defining opportunity for American leadership in the future of finance, Atkins proposed a regulatory agenda to integrate distributed ledger “blockchain” technology systems into US markets, support decentralized finance, and launch a new “innovation exemption” to accelerate the commercial deployment of novel technologies. Atkins signaled an abrupt about-face from his SEC predecessor toward a flexible regulatory posture that would shape the future of the US digital asset market for the twenty-first century.


And the beat goes on. In August 2025, the White House announced the signing of an Executive Order allowing 401(k) investors to access alternative assets, including private equity, real estate, and digital assets.15 The order directs the Secretary of Labor to reexamine and clarify guidance on fiduciary duties for such investments in ERISA-governed 401(k) and other defined-contribution plans, and to work with relevant federal agencies on regulatory adjustments.16 President Trump also signed an executive order to stop “unfair banking” of the crypto industry.17


For this administration, the innovation furthering mission continues, just like it does in technological innovation generally and in the American private sector particularly. Whatever mistakes are and will be made, and there will be some, it is admirable to see a government that moves at the pace of business, indeed, at the pace of an innovative, start-up business.

# Chapter 11 Back in the Well




The author’s appearance before a Congressional body discussing the coming digital network future of finance




I had only been able to briefly touch upon the “letters of marque” proposal at the White House. There’s a lot more to the idea. In fact, the idea really originated with Mike Gill, to whom this book is dedicated. It was Mike Gill who first suggested to me that Congress dust off the Constitution’s provisions for “letters of marque and reprisal.” It was a pet idea that he brought up more than once during some downtime we shared in one or other airport lounge or financial capital barroom between DC and Singapore. I confess I had never paid attention to the obscure Constitutional provision, but I was taken by Mike’s recounting the early American history of its celebrated application. Mike’s idea was that the provision be used to go after Mexican drug cartels.


I had forgotten all about privateering until Chris Perkins brought it up to me in January 2025. Perkins’ proposal was that letters of marque be employed to go after state-sponsored cyber hackers. I immediately warmed to the idea for its simple logic, but also because it honored Mike’s memory. Perkins and I got to work on an op-ed calling on Congress to empower modern-day digital privateers to hunt down and punish state-sponsored cyber hackers such as North Korea’s Lazarus Group.1





## Letters of Marque



In our writing, Chris Perkins and I noted that 2025 was off to a terrible start in terms of cyber penetration. Lazarus Group, a North Korean–sponsored hacking organization, stole $1.4 billion from Bybit, a major crypto exchange.2 This was one of the largest hacks in the crypto industry’s history. In the prior year alone, hackers pillaged their way across the sector, stealing over $2 billion.3 More than half can be directly traced to Lazarus Group, which diverts stolen digital assets to various illicit activities, including North Korea’s nuclear weapons program.4


We proclaimed that the status quo was unacceptable. Policies and government capabilities had fallen short. American businesses remained exposed, and Lazarus’ exploits and funding of North Korea’s atomic weapons program had obvious national security implications. Perkins and I proposed a twenty-first-century privateer program that would issue letters of marque and reprisal to American companies or individuals to hack wallets and retrieve funds controlled by sanctioned governments, entities, or individuals. Recipients would be immune from US prosecution for their activities directly related to executing this mission. We explained that “letters of marque and reprisal,” often referred to as “letters of marque” (marque rhymes with mark), are formal commissions or licenses issued by governmental authorities sanctioning the use of force by private citizens against enemy nations. Letters of marque afford privateers both legal authorization to engage in hostile activities and recognition of privateers’ entitlement to some portion of the proceeds of the sale of the captured vessel and its cargo, collectively referred to as the “prize.” Such letters had been routinely issued in the fifteenth through nineteenth centuries to maritime merchantmen sailing armed vessels (referred to as “privateers”) who were authorized to attack and capture other merchant vessels sailing under the flag of an enemy nation.


The Constitution expressly grants Congress the ability to issue letters of marque,5 authority often delegated in practice to the executive branch. They were used by the United States extensively during the American Revolution and the War of 1812. Congress has expressly authorized the issuance of letters of marque at least twice since then in the Second Barbary War6 and the American Civil War, but no specific letters were issued.7


Perkins and I pointed to the digital future of finance. With the rise of artificial intelligence (AI), the sophistication of hackers is increasing exponentially. AI “agents” can more efficiently identify vulnerabilities in code. Low-cost, AI-generated deepfake video and audio capabilities deliver perfect impersonation, allowing hackers to more easily swindle unwitting victims. However, advanced AI tools and capabilities can work in both directions. Neo-privateers, indemnified and powered by letters of marque and reprisal, could use the most sophisticated technologies to attack the attackers and hack the hackers. By leveraging the private sector to fight back in the crypto space, government agencies could focus on higher-priority security issues. Undoubtedly, legitimate concerns would need to be addressed in any contemporary authorization, including terms and conditions of participation, Congressional oversight and concerns about state-sponsored vigilantism, and possible conflict escalation. Nevertheless, we closed by proclaiming that “the time has come for the United States to embrace its history and launch a neo-privateer program. Letters of marque … provide an elegant solution to protect American innovation and its national security.”8


Soon after the op-ed appeared, I would be discussing letters of marque with a Committee of Congress. Not long after that, I would be discussing it with the US Secretary of the Treasury.







## Back Where I Started



One early spring morning two weeks after the White House Crypto Summit and a rushed breakfast at Washington’s Capitol Hill Club,9 I stepped out into the bright mid-Atlantic morning sunshine. I walked a hundred yards up New Jersey Avenue in view of the Capitol and entered the Longworth House Office Building and climbed the stairs to the House Agriculture Hearing Room. It was very familiar.


Eleven years before, with my family and parents by my side, I had been introduced in this room by a US Senator and several senior Congressmen and sworn in as a CFTC commissioner by Supreme Court Justice Clarence Thomas. Since then, I had given testimony in this room a half dozen or more times as CFTC commissioner, chairman, and, in recent years, as a private citizen.


The House Agriculture Committee was holding a hearing entitled, “The CFTC at 50: Examining the Past and Future of Commodity Markets.” Testifying with me were a group of futures market professionals, including my dear friend, colleague, and professional mentor, Dr. Richard Sandor.10


I gave my opening remarks11:



Thank you Chairman [G.T.] Thompson, Ranking Member [Angie] Craig and committee members for holding this hearing on the 50th anniversary of the CFTC.


It is right to acknowledge the CFTC’s remarkable record of success and the enormous economic value it provides for the US economy and American consumers.


When I am asked to explain the purpose of the CFTC, I use a comparison to the better-known Securities and Exchange Commission. I explain that the SEC overseas markets for capital formation. That is, markets where those with business ideas find those with capital to fund their growth and success.


That is not what the CFTC does. Rather, the CFTC oversees markets for risk transfer.




I went on to explain that Congress had authorized the CFTC to regulate markets where those with business risk—risk to farmers of falling prices for crop production, risk to American manufacturers of rising energy prices, and risk to home builders of fluctuating interest rates—can offset some or all of that risk with parties better able to bear it. CFTC markets for risk transfer are very different from SEC markets for capital formation and require specialized regulatory skills and understanding. I said that, fortunately, the CFTC and its great professional staff have those skills in spades.


I explained that during almost five years on the Commission, I travelled the country and visited agriculture producers in over two dozen states from Montana, Texas, Arkansas, Louisiana, and Iowa to Minnesota, Missouri, New York, Georgia, Mississippi, and Oklahoma. I walked in wheat fields and harvested soybeans, tramped through rice farms and beneath pecan groves, milked dairy cows and toured feedlots, visited grain elevators, and viewed cotton gins. I met with American energy and food producers, going 900 feet underground in a Kentucky coal mine, 90 feet in the air in an Arkansas crop duster, and climbing 99 feet up a North Dakota oil rig.


I added: “And, many of my fellow commissioners continue to do the same. What other federal agency does that?”


To emphasize the importance of robust and well-regulated derivative markets, I shared one of my most interesting experiences as CFTC chairman. I had to do with the publication in the spring of 2018 by the Vatican of an official bulletin, known as a bollettino. This official bulletin of the Holy See,12 titled “‘Oeconomicae et pecuniariae quaestiones.’ Considerations for an ethical discernment regarding some aspects of the present economic-financial system,” laid out certain ethical principles to govern economic and financial systems. While many of the points made in the document were quite interesting, the bulletin fundamentally mischaracterized the nature of derivatives as largely speculative products tantamount to gambling.


I said that as the chairman of the CFTC, the developed world’s premier commodity derivatives market regulator, and a practicing Roman Catholic, I felt compelled to respond. The CFTC’s Chief Economist Bruce Tuckman and I issued a response to the Holy See to set the record straight and explain that derivatives were not “ticking time bomb[s] ready sooner or later to explode.”13


In our letter to the Vatican, itself covered in a front-page Wall Street Journal story,14 we explained that derivatives have been used for thousands of years to manage commercial and market risk.15 Yet, today in many of the world’s poorest societies, the lack of functioning risk transfer markets meant that the boom and bust cycle of subsistence was a source of poverty, crime, and hunger. We explained that each planting season, farmers across the globe face a myriad of uncertainties from unfavorable weather patterns, equipment costs, farmhand availability, market prices, and others.


Where available, derivatives serve as an essential tool to mitigate and constrain these risks in a number of ways. First, they provide reliable and fair pricing benchmarks that promote market efficiencies overall. Second, derivatives reduce price volatility in a resource-constrained world by removing the economic incentive to hoard physical supplies. Farmers can quantify and transfer the risks they want to avoid at a reasonable price to persons willing and able to hold that risk. Such risk protection reduces earnings volatility and, thus, price volatility, benefiting all parties, including consumers who likely never think about derivatives markets in the first place. Finally, by entering into futures contracts to sell farm production at a predetermined price, the farmer can secure revenue regardless of market fluctuations that may occur down the line. This provides the farmer with financial predictability and stability, enabling better planning and investment in the business.


Tuckman and I told the writers of the Bollettino that it was the absence, not the presence, of functioning derivatives markets that harmed some of the world’s poorest and most vulnerable populations. Our presentation better educated the Vatican and tempered its underappreciation of the role of derivatives in alleviating global hunger and malnourishment. I was subsequently invited to a series of meetings in Rome to meet senior Vatican officials and discuss finance and derivatives, perhaps one of very few market regulators to ever be so welcomed.


I then went on in my testimony to the Agriculture Committee to explain that beyond agriculture, derivatives enhance other aspects of modern life. They are used by both big and small enterprises, such as commercial manufacturers, power utilities, retirement funds, banks, and investment firms. I said that more than 90% of Fortune 500 companies use derivatives to control costs and other risks in their worldwide business operations. Energy companies, for instance, use futures contracts to hedge against gas and electric price volatility, ensuring stable energy costs for consumers. Similarly, financial institutions may make use of interest rate swaps to manage the costs associated with mortgage lending to make homeownership more affordable. Through the use of innovative new products like event contracts, consumers and businesses may utilize derivatives markets to hedge risks of national and global events.


I explained that one area where these markets are critical is in managing the risk associated with the US dollar, the world’s primary reserve currency. When the CFTC was reformulated out of the Department of Agriculture 50 years ago, it was quite specifically to safeguard a breakthrough in financial innovation: financial futures. These new instruments supported the US dollar’s primacy as the world’s reserve currency by offering participants a means to hedge against foreign exchange and interest rate risk associated with the dollar, enabling businesses and governments worldwide to protect themselves and stabilize their own finances. This market function underscores just how critical the CFTC’s mission is to the success of American markets for the transfer of US-dollar-based risk.


I declared that American derivatives markets are also some of the world’s best regulated. The CFTC is globally recognized as the world’s preeminent derivatives regulator with some of the most knowledgeable, skilled, and committed professional staff of any market regulator in the world. The CFTC’s unparalleled global reputation for expertise and effectiveness allows both domestic and international participants to have confidence in American trading markets. This confidence fosters market growth, as participants trust that the regulatory environment in which they operate is one based on openness, innovation, the rule of law, and integrity.


I continued by pointing out that, first off, many of the world’s market regulators send their derivatives specialists to be trained by the CFTC. Secondly, CFTC segregation requirements for customer funds protect market participants from misappropriation. The CFTC has been a global leader in clearinghouse supervision for decades, both before the 2008 financial crisis and ever since. Even in the face of extreme volatility, CFTC-regulated derivatives clearing firms successfully handle and manage risk, enabling valuable price risk transfer to support and stabilize the broader financial market. Under the CFTC’s watch, not a single CFTC-regulated clearinghouse has ever defaulted or even come close to using its mutualized default resources to cover market losses, not even during the great financial crisis.


I went on to explain that the United States is the only major economy to have a regulatory agency specifically dedicated to derivatives market regulation. It was worth asking whether having such a dedicated commodity derivatives regulator is the reason why US commodity futures markets are bigger and more globally important than many global competitors. Or is the fact that American futures markets are more critical than many overseas competitors the reason why they require a highly skilled and dedicated regulator? Both are probably true and, whatever the reason, it is clear that the CFTC provides an American advantage in global economic competition.


I highlighted the CFTC’s proactive role in fostering and promoting innovation in US derivatives markets, balancing the CFTC’s mandate with the need to support technological advancements and new product development. The CFTC promotes market and product innovation by allowing derivatives exchanges to introduce new products through a self-certification process, a framework whereby exchanges may list new contracts and financial products without formal CFTC approval, provided they certify that the new products comply with the Commodity Exchange Act and the CFTC’s regulations. This approach has enabled the rapid introduction of novel and innovative financial instruments, such as derivatives based on cryptocurrencies and prediction event contracts.


I said I was proud of the CFTC’s work in 2015 to designate Bitcoin as a digital commodity and in 2017 to greenlight the launch of Bitcoin futures, the world’s first fully regulated derivatives marketplace for crypto of any kind. It is a perfect example of how the CFTC facilitates market-driven innovation while maintaining effective oversight of regulatory compliance and market integrity.


I explained that another way in which the CFTC encourages innovation is through the agency’s Office of  Technology Innovation. Established in 2017 as LabCFTC, the Office of Technology Innovation serves as the CFTC’s innovation hub by providing a venue for CFTC operating divisions, market participants, startups, and technology firms to engage collaboratively on cutting-edge developments in blockchain, AI, decentralized finance, and other transformative technologies with the potential to advance American derivatives markets. This collaboration ensures that the CFTC’s regulatory approach can develop alongside private-sector market innovations. I noted that the Committee proposed to establish LabCFTC in an amendment to the Commodity Exchange Act, which I readily supported.


I closed by noting that the other panelists had described many characteristics that distinguish the CFTC—its principles-based regulatory approach, its focus on end users, and its embrace of innovation. I added one final, but highly remarkable CFTC quality: the relative lack of partisanship among the commissioners. Organizations are said to reflect the “tone from the top.” Certainly, the CFTC’s reduced partisanship mirrors the general cordiality and frequent bipartisanship of the House Agriculture Committee and its Senate counterpart. That characteristic, in turn, reflects the courtesies and values of America’s heartland. Maintaining this attitude is critical for the CFTC to continue to foster open, competitive, and financially sound markets.


I then stated:



With a budget and staff that is a pittance against those of its Federal regulatory peers, the CFTC is pound-for-pound the best value in Washington – especially for American farmers, producers and everyday consumers.


Mr. Chairman, fifty years after its creation, I am delighted to join this committee in saying: “Happy Birthday, CFTC!”




Opening statements complete, the question-and-answer session began. Congressman Austin Scott of Georgia asked me if the CFTC’s legal framework was well suited to regulate digital currencies. I answered,



Well, let’s start with the fact that the CFTC has been looking at digital assets going back to at least 2014 when I first started with the Commission. And under my predecessor, Chairman Tim Massad,16 we declared in 2015 Bitcoin to be the world’s first digital commodity under CFTC jurisdiction. And over the last few years, while our sister agency, the SEC, has really been quite frankly resistant to engaging with digital assets, the CFTC has upped its game considerably. It has over a decade of studying the most important digital assets, which are the digital commodities like Bitcoin and Ethereum. So the CFTC’s inherent knowledge base is better than any other agency in Washington …




The chair recognized Congressman Dusty Johnson of South Dakota, who asked,



Chairman Giancarlo, you knew a lot about swaps before you became a commissioner and then chairman … So in your written testimony, one of the headings is the next 50 years. And then I got excited when you started to talk digital assets because I thought, oh, we are going to get into something real here. But you didn’t address the market structure bill that passed out of committee on a strongly bipartisan basis, and I don’t want to put you on the spot. It wasn’t like you were an author of it or anything. But any observations for us as we get ready to relaunch that effort here in committee?




I was indeed very familiar with the draft legislation, having previously provided specific comments to the committee staff, which he obliquely acknowledged. In fact, I especially liked that the bill addressed the important public interest in closing a gap in CFTC oversight.


As I have often explained, spot trading markets that facilitate immediate physical delivery of tradable goods are different than markets for futures, forwards, and options deliverable in the future. In spot markets, the CFTC has only limited authority over trading of digital asset commodities. As a result, the CFTC cannot require platform registration, operator supervision, or standard investor protection measures in the spot markets that the CFTC requires in US derivatives markets to police against fraud, manipulation, and abuse. That left a big gap in supervision since it was calculated that between two-thirds and three-quarters of crypto currencies are traded in those unregulated spot markets.17 This bill that Congressman Johnson wanted me to discuss would bring the CFTC’s practical and seasoned oversight to spot trading in these most popular cryptocurrencies. I did indeed support the provisions in the bill that extend the CFTC’s oversight to cover spot digital commodity markets.


I replied, Yes, the United States needs a regulator for spot markets for crypto, I truly believe.


Congressman Johnson was not satisfied. He clearly wanted more.


“Yes …,” he prompted.


I deepened my voice and said,



When I look around the landscape, there is really only one agency that is ready to take up the baton of regulating digital commodity spot markets, and that is the CFTC. It has been engaged continuously under both Republican chairs and Democratic leadership for the past dozen years in this marketplace. Its record in terms of Bitcoin futures, Ethereum futures, and now, just recently launched Solana futures, is superb. The information is transparent. It is available. The markets operate in an orderly fashion. I mean, I don’t want to throw shade at a sister regulator, but its failure to engage—




Congressman Johnson was loving it. He jumped in, “Oh, please do, please do. We are fine with that here.”


I continued:



The SEC’s failure to engage is quite notable against an agency like the CFTC that has engaged and done so quite successfully and proven that regulators can engage with this new innovation and do so successfully.


“And I will say one other thing. Crypto is a lot more than just about, “is the number going up?” This is a new architecture of finance that is going to change everything we know about how you record who owns what and who is transferring what to whom.


The United States must be a leader in this, and [the CFTC] is the agency that has already served as a leader for the last dozen years.


The Chair later recognized Congressman LaMalfa18 of California.


Mr. Giancarlo, proposed reforms for us to consider in the crypto spot market where fraud can certainly run rampant, we are positioned to provide some commonsense regulation in that arena. Could you speak to other reforms you have to see from our efforts in Congress and the Administration that you haven’t got to touch on so far in this discussion today?


I answered,


Well, I do want to, if I may, speak to the issue of fraud in crypto markets. There is no question that fraud in spot markets is an issue, and I think it is a reason why we need proper regulation. But, we at the CFTC, along with 50 state governments, have been trying to root out fraud in some of the world’s oldest markets like gold markets for dozens if not hundreds of years, and the fraud still takes place. Unfortunately, the job of regulators is one of cops and robbers, and we do our best as cops when the robbers figure out something new and they get a step ahead, and our job is to stay a step behind, not two steps behind.




Congressman LaMalfa closed, “Thank you. Very good, complete answer. Thank you for that. Indeed, as long as people are people, we are always going to have to keep an eye on them, and only one step behind is–and that is reality, just not two. I like that. Thank you.”


A while later the chair recognized Congressman Nunn of Iowa, who said,



Chairman Giancarlo, you have been called “CryptoDad” I believe … Under the last administration, we saw [that other agency] the SEC that tried to cut out the CFTC completely. They labeled almost everything in the digital space, a security. And I asked a predecessor here, SEC Chairman Gensler, a simple question. Is a digital asset–called Ether at the time, still there now–a commodity, or is it a security? Now, he couldn’t answer that, but he was happy to regulate it to death.


So my question now is that we have moved on from the last Congress. The opportunity [is] to provide comprehensive rules of the road so we can onshore digital assets here for the future and not see them flee off to the Bahamas, or worse, fall in the hands of places like Tehran, China, and others. As we work to get that legislation across the finish line, what can the CFTC do to help provide clarity on assets like Ether or others that operate more like a commodity? I would appreciate your thoughts.


I was pleased to reply:


“Well, the truth of the matter is that the CFTC has been crystal clear on this for almost a decade now. In 2015 the CFTC, in a bipartisan manner, declared Bitcoin to be the world’s first digital commodity under CFTC jurisdiction. In 2017 we green lighted the world’s first regulated market for any type of derivative on crypto. That was Bitcoin futures. Eight years later, that marketplace is deep, it is liquid, it is transparent, extremely well regulated. So those efforts to box out the CFTC totally failed. The CFTC is recognized not only here in the United States but worldwide as the world’s primary crypto regulator with a very successful track record. In your own state, Iowa was the first leader in terms of looking at events contracts out of the University Iowa, and that is one of the big innovations on the horizon where the United States has another opportunity to lead the world in setting the regulatory structure for these new instruments.”


Congressman Nunn said:


… I think you are absolutely right here. We need to be able to provide the framework for this and making sure that there are legitimate rules of the road, as it were, to not only be able to onshore but then, as you just highlighted here, that there is a key partnership between the CFTC, the SEC, and then also being able to go after those illicit actors.


You talked a little about letters of marque. In our few seconds, talk to me here about what we can do for the illicit side of this.


I was especially pleased to reply:


We have the capacity to knock these people right on their heels. When I was a boy, there was a bully at school. My father said it is not enough to put your hands over the face. You need to punch him in the nose. We have been putting our hands over our face saying, please don’t attack us, please don’t attack us. Those days have to be over. We have to fight back.




Congressman Nunn exclaimed, “As a combat veteran, I am ready to punch back. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.”


With that, the hearing came to an end.


I stepped out onto the hearing room’s balcony with its fine view of the US Capitol building and took photos with my dear friends and panelists, Dr. Richard Sandor, industry veteran D’Anna Dow, and fellow former CFTC chairman, Jim Newsome. It was a clear blue-sky day, just as it was 11 years before when I first spoke in that room and began my journey in Washington politics.
