# Chapter 12 An Internet of Liberty




Because Crypto is not going away, but only gaining steam, we must emplace sound regulatory structures that serve democratic values, especially individual liberty, personal privacy, and human dignity




In 2013, Jim Harper, constitutional law scholar and Senior Fellow at the Cato Institute, opened an account with the digital currency exchange Coinbase, relying on its standard “click-through” agreement to guarantee his privacy and the security of his financial information. He used his Coinbase account to receive payments in Bitcoin in compensation for his consulting services to the Bitcoin Foundation. 1 He then promptly converted almost all his Bitcoin income to dollars. Harper rigorously reported and paid all taxes on his Bitcoin income and capital gains.2


By the end of 2016, Harper had stopped using Coinbase, transferring his remaining cryptocurrency to an independent digital wallet consistent with the custom of “self-custody.” Harper and thousands of others have learned through the collapse of crypto exchanges and platforms that you do not truly own your crypto unless you control the cryptographic keys associated with any given unit of digital money. “Not your keys, not your coins,” the saying goes.3


In November 2016, the Internal Revenue Service (IRS) served a sweeping “John Doe” summons on Coinbase, seeking an enormous trove of financial records for millions of Coinbase users, including detailed transaction logs, account profiles, due diligence records, and even private correspondence between Coinbase and its customers from 2013 to 2015. Coinbase initially resisted, prompting the IRS to seek judicial enforcement. In subsequent proceedings, the court narrowed the IRS’s demand. Nevertheless, the IRS was able to obtain from Coinbase troves of information on Jim Harper and thousands of other unnamed American citizens.4


At no point was Harper notified that the IRS had seized and reviewed his private records. He heard about the IRS’s action but did not realize his data was affected. Harper only learned that the IRS had gathered up his private financial records in August 2019, when he received a menacing letter from the agency. The letter’s message was unmistakable: “We have information that you have or had one or more accounts containing virtual currency but may not have properly reported your transactions involving virtual currency.”5


In fact, the IRS had no basis for such a claim—Harper had paid every cent of tax that he calculated he owed through his consulting business. Yet the letter made clear that the IRS had obtained Harper’s confidential financial data. For Harper, this unlawful seizure was not merely an abstract privacy violation—it threatened his family’s security. When self-custodying cryptocurrency, financial privacy becomes a matter of physical security. If criminals rightly or wrongly suspect that an individual holds significant cryptocurrency, they may resort to home invasion, kidnapping, or worse to steal it.6 And that danger persists. The IRS continues to hold Harper’s records to this day, despite the fact that he has paid his taxes in full. Harper’s privacy, security, and constitutional rights remain in jeopardy, though there is no chance whatsoever that the IRS’s possession of his data will result in any tax collections.7


With the New Civil Liberties Alliance as his pro bono counsel, Harper filed a lawsuit in July 2020 contending that the IRS had unlawfully accessed his private financial records in violation of the Fourth and Fifth Amendments of the Constitution and in contravention of the Internal Revenue Code.8 The federal district court dismissed the suit in March 2021, concluding that it did not have subject matter jurisdiction over suits like Harper’s that challenge IRS’s information-gathering activities. Harper appealed, and the appellate court reversed the lower court ruling, finding that it did have jurisdiction.9


Once again, the district court dismissed Harper’s suit, holding that he had no Fourth Amendment interest in his own records. It further held that IRS’s compliance with statutory procedures automatically rendered any seizure of Harper’s records reasonable. The lower court rejected Harper’s Fifth Amendment due process claim, ruling that he lacked both a property interest in his own financial records and a protectable liberty interest in their privacy.10


This time the First Circuit affirmed the decision.11 It held that the “third-party” doctrine foreclosed Harper’s Fourth Amendment claim, reasoning that Harper did not have a privacy interest in the records he entrusted to Coinbase, even though his contract with Coinbase expressly limited their disclosure.12 In so doing, the appellate court affirmed the IRS’ seizure of Harper’s private records without a warrant, without notice, and without providing him with an opportunity to object. As a result, the IRS retains a permanent means to monitor Harper’s historical and future financial activity,13 despite there being no allegation that Harper has violated any tax law.


On February 21, 2025, Harper filed a certiorari petition14 urging the Supreme Court to revisit the third-party doctrine and restore Fourth Amendment protections to digital records that Americans store with service providers. Harper argued that such unchecked surveillance by the IRS is governmental abuse that the framers of the Constitution intended to prevent through the Fourth Amendment’s right to individual privacy15—the same right to privacy and against unlawful searches and seizures that Paul Revere’s Massachusetts militiamen fought for on the road to Lexington and Concord. Harper’s petition argued that the case offered a strong opportunity to reform the third-party doctrine for the emerging age of digital networks of value.


In support of Harper’s petition, my colleague, Kristy Littman, and several Willkie lawyers provided pro bono representation to a law professor who filed an amicus (friend of the court) brief with the Supreme Court.16 Coinbase also filed an amicus brief supporting Harper’s case,17 as did the Cato Institute and quite a few others.18


On June 30, 2025, the Supreme Court declined to hear the case without offering an explanation. As a result, at least for now, the IRS can continue to gather and hold Jim Harper’s and all other Americans’ digital records stored with service providers.


The Harper case, it turns out, is only the most visible surface of a vast financial surveillance apparatus.





## State of Financial Surveillance



In America, courts have generally protected privacy for the past half century under a legal doctrine called the “reasonable expectation of privacy” test.19 That is, when US government agents want to seize or search a person, their house, their papers, or their effects, courts will ask whether doing so violates the owner’s reasonable expectations of privacy.20 Disappointingly, this “reasonable expectation” approach to constitutional interpretation is not terribly rigorous or protective, especially in the realm of financial services. There, the Supreme Court has found that government agents can seize and examine a suspect’s checks and bank statements without obtaining a warrant because each individual check had passed through several hands in the financial services system.21 This is the so-called “third-party doctrine” at issue in the Harper lawsuit. It holds that a person cannot claim constitutional protection for information held by a third party. Supreme Court justices ranging from Sonia Sotomayor22 to Neil Gorsuch23 have questioned both this “third-party doctrine” and the “reasonable expectation of privacy” test.


And yet state surveillance persists in the United States, especially in the current financial system where banks and other providers of financial services are obligated to keep extensive records of who their customers are and what the institutions do with customers’ money. Financial institutions must make those records readily available to government agencies for law enforcement, tax compliance, and national security purposes.


The slippery slope of financial surveillance began in the United States with the 1970 passage24 of the Bank Secrecy Act (BSA). It requires financial institutions to keep records of cash purchases of negotiable instruments, report cash transactions exceeding $10,000 (a lot of money in 1970 but the equivalent of just over $83,000 today25), and report suspicious activity. When Congress enacted the BSA in 1970, organized crime was the target, and cash was the criminal’s tool. Ever since, banks have been required to keep certain records on their customers and turn those records over to law enforcement upon request. BSA power is exercised with no checks or balances, unlike a traditional criminal search warrant, which must be narrowly tailored and issued by a judge or magistrate upon a showing of probable cause that a crime was committed and that specific evidence of that crime exists in the place to be searched. Under the BSA, however, a prosecutor can issue a subpoena without going before a judge and can seize a citizen’s entire bank records for the past 10 years at no cost to the government. The burden falls entirely on the bank.


Surveillance of everyone’s financial transactions without suspicion of crime is anathema to privacy and democratic values such as the presumption of innocence. But the Supreme Court regrettably ratified the practice in a pair of 1970s cases—California Bankers Ass’n v. Shultz26 and US v. Miller.27 The Miller case was the beginning of the previously mentioned third-party doctrine. After the terrorist attacks of September 11, 2001, the BSA was turbocharged, with many more entities required to participate in tracking and reporting Americans’ financial transactions. The recipient of this information is the Treasury Department’s Financial Crimes Enforcement Network, known as FinCEN.28 The Financial Action Task Force,29 a Paris-based organization, presses governments worldwide to maintain parallel financial surveillance regimes.


The current financial surveillance regime is known by various combinations of initials—AML for “anti–money laundering,” KYC for “know your customer,” and CDD for “customer due diligence.” Sometimes it’s called CFT for “combating the financing of terrorism,” a moniker that presumes way too much about its effectiveness. It is financial surveillance, short and simple. As a practical matter, every bank must know the identity and some basic personal details about its customers to render basic services. But under the BSA, banks must surveil and monitor individual bank transactions and much more. BSA-mandated record-keeping deputizes financial services providers into being part of a huge monitoring operation that covers everyone, not just criminal suspects. That monitoring operation collects massive amounts of citizens’ everyday financial information in case someone does something wrong, and not just when there is probable cause that someone has actually done something wrong. This kind of general financial surveillance stands on shaky Constitutional ground and is dubious public policy, with costs that appear to exceed its benefits by quite a bit.30 This is especially so as digitization has made this surveillance more detailed and revealing. It is a legal framework in tension with American values. And it holds back the potential benefits of tokenization and distributed ledger technology.







## Chicken Wire or Velvet Rope



The advent of digital networks of value is an opportunity to replace the BSA surveillance regime with something more privacy-protective in line with the US Constitution and democratic American values.


In February 2023, Jim Harper—now at the American Enterprise Institute—and I published the White Paper, “The Values of Money: Will Tyranny or Freedom be in Your Digital Wallet?” We argued that the world would soon see the advent of digitally networked money in the form of stablecoins and central bank digital currency. We said that the United States had a chance to lead by advancing well-established democratic principles, but only if it was willing to engage in fresh thinking about current US financial surveillance, including a re-examination of the Bank Secrecy Act.31 We cited prominent pleas to begin that process now.32


Harper and I pointed out that although the BSA has been in place for more than 50 years, there has never been a clear and articulate comparison of its costs and benefits. We noted that most BSA benefits are presumed or supported by highly speculative assumptions, creating a false sense of security while diminishing the constitutional rights of American citizens. We noted that, as often as not, any questioning of BSA benefits is chased off by bankers and politicians intoning about the heinous crimes committed by drug kingpins and terrorists.


Unlike presumed BSA benefits, the cost of global financial surveillance is easier to measure and probably heavier. According to the Cato Institute, US financial institutions spend annually an estimated $59 billion complying with the Bank Secrecy Act regime. This regime forced these institutions to file 28.7 million reports on their customers in 2025. And yet, these reports only tipped off 275 investigations by the Internal Revenue Service’s Criminal Investigations division.33 Fifty-nine billion dollars on compliance, 28.7 million reports produced, and yet only 275 investigations generated—a fairly weak return on investment.34 Worldwide, the aggregate cost of BSA compliance is easily in the hundreds of billions of dollars annually. Another cost is the damage done by “de-risking”—the refusal of financial services providers to serve certain customers, markets, and even countries. The de-risking of countries may drain their economies, tax bases, and institutions, putting them in the “failed state” category and perversely imperiling global security.35


Nor does the BSA actually appear to be effective at achieving its aims.36 Instead of stopping major crime, the system floods law enforcement with low-value information, overwhelming agents and obscuring real threats, effectively drowning law enforcement in noise. But while it doesn’t stop crime, the BSA creates a trove of permanent info on everyone. Worse still, the incentives are misaligned and asymmetrical. To avoid liability, financial institutions are required to report anything remotely suspicious. If they fail to file a SAR, they risk serious penalties—even indictment.37 But they face no consequences for overreporting. The vast overcollection of data is the unsurprising result.


Harper and I went on to note that as a security measure, BSA financial surveillance is somewhat akin to chicken wire on a farm. It will help exclude small-time threats. Think of drug distributors as barn cats eyeballing the poultry. The wire may hold off foxes and raccoons for a while, but they will find their way in. Yet, chicken wire really fails against what matters: large or persistent threats such as bears or burglars coming in the main house.


An example of the ineffectiveness of such BSA chicken wire came in May 2025, when the crypto trading platform Coinbase, experienced a significant data breach in which attackers stole personal information from tens of thousands of customers by bribing overseas customer support personnel with old fashioned cash payments.38 The hackers were not after the crypto, but the sensitive user data, including names, IDs, and bank information retained by Coinbase pursuant to BSA requirements. The problem was not crypto; the problem was the Bank Secrecy Act and its requirements for the overcollection of highly valuable, sensitive personal data. In that case, the BSA chicken wire did little to prevent crime, but it did provide a false sense of security behind which enormous troves of personal information were stored and ultimately stolen.


Given the direct dollar costs, identity requirements, and indirect costs of de-risking, Harper and I concluded that a better analogy for the current financial surveillance regime is a velvet rope. It turns the global financial services system into an exclusive nightclub, reserved for the world’s wealthy who are endowed with effective identity credentials. Financial surveillance excludes under-credentialed people around the globe, people who would benefit most from being able to transact, save, and invest through reliable financial institutions. In a world of eight billion people, roughly a billion and a half do not have access to financial services,39 often because they do not have identity documents good enough to satisfy financial surveillance requirements.







## Privacy in the Internet of Value



There is probably no topic more in need of thoughtful consideration than the privacy and censorship implications of the emerging Internet of Value. It is essential that we make sure that the emergence of digital currency and other tokens of value is consistent with economic freedom, privacy rights, and human liberty. That will be difficult to do without acknowledging and addressing the existing government surveillance operation that I have described, a surveillance process that enables governments to trace and monitor individual financial transactions without probable cause of wrongdoing and court-issued permission to eavesdrop and surveil. The financial surveillance “velvet rope line” is increasingly subject to abuse, even by governments of free societies. It is taking democratic societies closer to the norms of closed societies than is socially acceptable to admit. As the world transitions to digital money—both sovereign and nonsovereign—these policies should be openly and boldly reconsidered.


Many thoughtful people on both sides of the political aisle are appropriately concerned about misuse of such “honeypots” of financial data. They are especially concerned as emerging digital network technologies further expand the footprint of financial data. Add to this the massive pools of personal information already collected by social media platforms—location history, search activity, communications metadata—and we find a world where financial surveillance can be linked to virtually every aspect of your identity, movement, and behavior.


Katie Haun—who as a federal prosecutor led some of the US government’s earliest and most significant crypto crime cases before becoming one of the sector’s most prominent venture investors—has seen the surveillance apparatus from both sides:



Technological innovations over the past several decades have brought incredible convenience to economic life. Now our privacy standards must catch up. With Congress considering landmark legislation on digital assets, it’s an important moment to consider what kind of financial system we want—not just in terms of efficiency and access, but in terms of freedom.


Financial surveillance shouldn’t be the price of participation in modern life. The systems we build now will shape what freedom looks like for the next century. It’s time to treat financial privacy like what it is: a cornerstone of democracy, and a right worth fighting for.40




Inappropriate access to such data can wreak havoc on financial privacy and economic liberty. Florida Governor Ron DeSantis,41 Ohio Congressman Warren Davidson (R-OH-8),42 and others are quite right to be concerned about loss of privacy with central bank digital currencies. Yet, the same concerns apply to our existing analog financial system as well as new digital stablecoin systems onto which BSA requirements are being grafted. It is entirely foreseeable that private-sector stablecoin operators could be put under government pressure to conduct surveillance, report on activity, and disable financial transactions with disfavored groups and activities in a similar fashion to the Biden administration’s now increasingly evident censorship of speech on social media,43 and its aggressive debanking of crypto firms and their creators.







## Less Than Genius



While we have sadly come to expect financial surveillance of traditional finance, the GENIUS Act now imposes late twentieth-century financial surveillance over twenty-first-century digital money and, ultimately, the networks it travels on. The GENIUS Act makes stablecoins fully subject to the full panoply of financial surveillance under the Bank Secrecy Act.44 It requires stablecoin issuers to have “technical capabilities, policies, and procedures to block, freeze, and reject specific or impermissible transactions.”45 Admittedly, it was done so that bank deposits and stablecoins are placed under the same degree of financial surveillance, so that one does not become a preferred route for illicit finance. Still, it is regrettable that Congress did not use the opportunity to reconsider and replace the whole current BSA apparatus with something less constitutionally suspect and more in line with American values and individual liberty.


The word “privacy” hardly appears in the GENIUS Act.46 It is disappointing that many commentators who criticize central bank digital currency for its potential to violate individual privacy seem to have little to no public objection to subjecting US-dollar-based stablecoins to BSA financial surveillance. Unwarranted surveillance is not made acceptable because private actors are doing the surveilling.


In fact, US policymakers’ intention to subject stablecoins to the full panoply of financial surveillance is given away by coupling its passage in the House with something called the “Anti-CBDC Surveillance State Act” prohibiting the Federal Reserve from directly or indirectly issuing a central bank digital currency for the sake of protecting individual privacy from government control.47 It was as if the politicians were trying to shout, “Look at how we are valiantly protecting your privacy by banning CBDCs. But don’t look over there! Don’t look at how we’re imposing comprehensive financial surveillance on your use of stablecoins.”







## “Value” of Democratic Values



Human privacy in the early twenty-first century is in a sorry state throughout the globe. Law enforcement and state security apparatuses are using facial recognition technology to scan our likenesses, digital license plate readers and building turnstiles to scan our movements, social media surveillance to track our writings, and phone taps to listen to our conversations.48 They will soon track our transactions in digital currency.


A new, digital evolution of political power is emerging in the form of an “Information State” that “governs by controlling the codes and protocols of the digital public arena, which it uses to engineer the public’s compliance.”49 Across Western society, noncompliant citizens are being arrested for “thought crimes.”50 The Canadian government shut the bank accounts of truckers who objected to government COVID-19 policies.51 The current Carney government has proposed Bill C-22 (the Lawful Access Act) to enhance government surveillance capability by compelling metadata retention at scale by telecom providers, mandating encryption backdoors, and imposing gag orders on service providers. It creates the conditions under which free expression withers — not by banning speech, but by ensuring no one can speak privately.52


The US State Department’s August 2025 report on global human rights noted that human rights had worsened in Britain, France, and Germany due to regulations on so-called “hate” speech.53 A February 2026 report of the House Judiciary Committee reported that the European Commission weaponizes European law regulating online speech to impose global online censorship requirements on political speech, humor, and satire and regularly interferes in national European elections to suppress political criticism of EU-favored candidates.54


And it could get worse. “Imagine an internet where your identity is automatically attached to everything you do—every website you visit, every click you make.”55 That was the statist vision behind New IP, a proposal Chinese engineers introduced at a United Nations telecom forum in 2019 that would have replaced the current open internet with a system designed for government surveillance and censorship.56 New IP was part of China’s strategy to embed its authoritarian values into the global technology sector,57 the tower quashing the square.


We must find a way to reverse this global drift toward digital surveillance—a trend unworthy of a free people. The GENIUS Act was a missed opportunity for America. Congress could have encoded privacy into the very foundation of the dollar’s digital future, but did not. It should have started from a simple premise: the US dollar is the world’s greatest trademark—more recognized, more trusted, and more consequential than McDonald’s or Nike—and it belongs to the American people. Any entity permitted to use that trademark, including stablecoin issuers, should have been held to rigorous terms and conditions. The most important of those conditions should have been an unambiguous obligation to protect users’ financial privacy.


Throughout this book, I have discussed the opportunities and challenges of tokenizing things of value and placing them on digital networks. Those opportunities and challenges are even greater when it comes to our privacy. Once tokenized, digital assets become transferable and controllable with minimal friction and delay—creating vast quantities of data that are subject to collection in massive troves, ripe for surveillance by nosy government and data-mining by commercial banks and tech firms. They can change the entire concept of asset ownership. Who controls the flow of value—that is, the rails upon which value moves and is transferred—gains effective control of value itself.


Professor Danielle Zanzalari of Seton Hall University makes the case that control and design of digital wallets will be the new battleground over financial privacy and autonomy.58 By eliminating intermediaries and linking users directly to blockchain-based platforms, wallet designers and providers will wield outsized influence over who participates in the digital economy. The professor’s central concern is the market power that will be amassed if digital wallets become the new entry points to the economy and a single system dominates access to its digital rails. She points to China’s national wallet that can be accessed by state security agencies and Europe’s new government-backed wallets that are part of its new digital identity framework. She argues that Washington should counter these moves by requiring open, transparent standards for wallet privacy and interoperability while allowing private companies to compete on innovation and design.59


Fit-for-purpose digital currency regulatory frameworks and their instrumentalities must protect citizens’ privacy rights if the future of money is to enjoy their “trust and consent.” In democratic societies, lawful transactions in digital money must be immune from political surveillance, censorship, and overweening pressure to disable otherwise legal transactions. Without such immunity, economic activity will be frighteningly beholden to the shifting political winds in Washington, London, Brussels, and Beijing.60







## Got It Right Before



Thirty years ago, the first wave of the internet—an Internet of Information—rolled across the globe. Leaders in the United States and allied democracies made sure that the internet we use today reflected the values of open and free societies rather than closed and repressive ones.


In a remarkable effort, the public sector and the private sector worked together to set global standards and create key governing institutions: the Internet Society and the Internet Corporation for Assigned Names and Numbers, among others. The results have been economically and socially transformational—and in many cases liberating61—not only for the United States, but also for the entire free world.


Today, we are in the middle of a new era of change that is similarly unstoppable and inevitable: an Internet of Value. We must once again work together to ensure that the dollar and other digital currency networks are on the cutting edge of speed, reliability, accessibility, and inclusion. Designed properly, the systems and instrumentalities on which digital transactions operate should offer independent assurance about technical function and security.62 But we must go even further and make sure that digital currencies reflect virtues of free enterprise, individual autonomy, and free economic expression. If we get the digital technology carrots right, we won’t need sticks to preserve democratic digital instruments. And if we get the values right, the value of the dollar will be sustained for decades to come. It is up to the United States and citizens of the world’s democracies “to define tomorrow’s digital rules before China writes them for us.”63


That is why the advent of digital currency offers the opportunity to reassess contemporary financial surveillance activities in their entirety.64 It is an opening to reverse lamentable trends—financial surveillance, debanking, and de-risking—and replace them with a new architecture that can better ensure privacy while effectively and efficiently satisfying important law enforcement and national security objectives.65


There is no shortage of sensible proposals. In an insightful review of financial privacy in the digital age,66 SEC Commissioner Hester Peirce addresses law enforcement agencies’ fear of the disintermediation potential of distributed ledger technology. She writes, “An increase in disintermediation may seem particularly daunting to law-enforcement agencies because many have come to rely heavily on the third-party doctrine, which gives them easy access to information people provide to third parties.”67 She continues, “Rather than something to be feared, we should embrace these tools’ ability to help humanity live freer lives without unwarranted financial surveillance.”68 “Denying people financial privacy—whether through sweeping surveillance programs or restrictions on privacy-protecting technologies—undermines the fabric and freedoms of our families, communities and nation.”69


Nicholas Anthony of the Cato Institute argues that decades of surveillance-oriented legislation, from the Bank Secrecy Act forward, have systematically eroded Americans’ right to financial privacy, and that Congress should replace this failed framework with one that treats financial privacy as a fundamental right, restoring meaningful legal protections for individuals’ financial data in the digital age.70 For his part, Jim Harper proposes a new approach to crime prevention in the digital network future of money by creating a system through which law enforcement agencies could query digital transactors for investigative purposes in a communications channel parallel to private transaction channels.71 Such a system would maintain or strengthen law enforcement capabilities while obviating the existing, broad, and costly financial surveillance regime.


These approaches will only succeed if the United States and like-minded nations are willing to rethink the scope of today’s financial surveillance regime and invest in new technologies and regulatory tools that serve legitimate law enforcement needs without eroding civil liberties.72 The tools to do so are already emerging. Privacy-preserving blockchains like the Canton Network and privacy-enhancing technologies (“PETs”)—including zero-knowledge proofs, homomorphic encryption, and multi-party computation73—allow parties to confirm that a transaction is lawful without ever revealing the underlying private information.74 When combined with AI-driven advances in pattern recognition and big data analysis, these tools can enable law enforcement to identify wrongdoing when it actually occurs, rather than surveilling entire populations on the chance that it someday might. Digital networks and digital dollars built on these principles—with privacy encoded into their very architecture—can become trusted instruments of financial autonomy and inclusion for people everywhere who hold democratic values and basic human rights dear.75


Perhaps someone in Washington is listening. In September 2025, Treasury Under Secretary for Terrorism and Financial Intelligence John K. Hurley recommended reforms to the BSA, including shifting compliance from process-driven reviews to more objective, results-focused measures.76 While not focused on protecting Constitutional rights of privacy, Hurley did call for the use of enhanced digital technologies such as AI, blockchain analytics, and digital identity tools to improve reporting. Perhaps this serves as a start in recognizing that the current AML/KYC “emperor has no clothes” when it comes to its true cost-effectiveness in preventing financial crime. The first step in fixing a problem is admitting that there is one.


In March of 2026, the US Treasury published a report to Congress outlining how financial institutions can use innovative technologies like blockchain and AI to combat illicit finance related to digital assets.77 The report acknowledged a tension between privacy and illicit-finance controls, noting that privacy-enhancing tools (like mixers and privacy coins) have legitimate uses but can also facilitate crime. It recommended identity solutions that balance privacy interests to support BSA compliance while protecting users. The report also acknowledged that linking real-world identities to on-chain histories creates privacy risks and called for safeguards and further work to balance effective enforcement with minimizing unnecessary exposure of individuals’ financial data.


Austin Campbell, adjunct professor at Columbia Business School and a leading stablecoin policy advisor, put it well:



This report strikes a very good balance to me. Privacy is a balancing issue. On one hand, it is 100% correct that just waving your hands at it and saying “for the children” produces terrible outcomes. It creates honeypots, government abuse, and more. On the other hand, it is 100% incontrovertible that bad actors are using the system and need to be interdicted at times.78




Let’s hear again from Jim Harper. His crusade against the IRS for financial privacy is not finished. He is working on state legislation with national prospects that will make clearer that people can own and have rights in their digital papers and effects.79 And it is not his only crusade. A frequent traveler, he does not carry a REAL ID or passport, both of which he views as anathema to Americans’ freedom to travel. The additional security procedures add a half-minute to his travels, he says. In the past, declining to present an ID has gotten him through security faster!80


Harper has never been through the body scanner that the rest of us pass through as part of the security routine. He waits a little longer, encouraging others to join him in the pat-down that he jokingly calls the “freedom massage.” Harper believes such machines cost taxpayers too much, provide no more security than magnetometers, and dissuade some people from flying. Sadly, few Americans are willing to go through so much trouble just to preserve our privacy.


In mid-2015, Coinbase began contacting Harper to ask questions that he knew were required for BSA compliance—the source of his Bitcoins, the sources of money used for purchases, what his outgoing transfers were for, and so on. He didn’t offer up his private information, which is unnecessary for financial services providers to collect. Coinbase locked his account to protect itself from regulators. Harper wrote, “This information is none of your business. Please unlock my account, or I’ll take my business elsewhere.” That was the end of the correspondence. In early 2023, Coinbase closed Harper’s account.


The increasing threat to individual privacy from government and technology surveillance and control can only be reversed in the United States by a new generation of federal agency heads committed to the Constitutional balance of political authority and decentralized liberty, the tower and the square.


It is time to meet them.

# Chapter 13 Changing of the Guard




The debut of SEC Chairman Paul Atkins and CFTC Chairman Mike Selig and the advent of CLARITY




It was a damp and nippy November morning in 2025 when I exited the grand lobby of Washington’s Union Station and strode across Columbus Plaza. It was a pleasant change to walk through the station and out onto the plaza and not be accosted by the sight, sound, and menace of the agitators and political performance artists who had been permitted and likely paid to occupy the public space the past four years. In fact, the entire interior of Union Station was polished clean and rid of aggressive actors. What a contrast from the shabbiness of Newark’s Penn Station, where I had boarded the 5:40 a.m. Amtrak Acela two and a half hours before. New Jersey’s once grand passenger train terminal that I fondly remember from my childhood now served as a dingy recreation area for Newark’s destitute population.


Adjusting my wool cap against the morning chill, I made my way up Capitol Hill along First Street. I walked past the Monocle Restaurant, a long-serving Washington watering hole for Senators and their staffs and the lobbyists who called on them. It was where I entertained my late parents with a celebratory lunch following my Senate confirmation hearing almost 10 years before.


Continuing onward, I entered the white marble Russell Senate Office Building, the oldest and most majestic of the suite of Congressional office buildings adjacent to the nation’s capital. The morning would be spent in a series of informal meetings with several members of the Senate Agriculture Committee. I was there as a former CFTC chairman to voice my support for the Senate confirmation of Mike Selig as the next agency head.


The meetings had been arranged by Charlie Thornton, my former CFTC Director of Legislative Affairs, who now ran a busy government relations firm out of Nashville. First up was Senator Roger Marshall, who had replaced Pat Roberts, former chair of the Senate Agriculture Committee during my tenure. Senator Roberts had become a great ally when I conceived and launched the CFTC/Kansas State Agriculture Futures conference in 2018, which Charlie Thornton described as “critical to rebuilding the Senate’s trust in the CFTC.” Marshall was already a “yes” vote for Selig and only really latched onto our conversation when I mentioned that Selig intended to revitalize the CFTC Ag conference. Afterward, I met with Senators Tommy Tuberville of Alabama, known as “Coach” from his days as the head of Auburn’s football program, Cindy Hyde-Smith of Mississippi, and Senator Bill Hagerty of Tennessee, former Ambassador to Japan and my friend and Vanderbilt Law School classmate. All seemed pleased to hear a strong personal endorsement of Selig’s competence and character.


After lunch with Nathan Anonick, the Senate Agriculture Committee’s reflective Senior Counsel, I returned to the Capitol for the formal confirmation hearing of Mike Selig as CFTC chairman. Because of the large, expected turnout, the hearing was held in one of the Capitol’s large all-purpose hearing rooms rather than in the Agriculture Committee’s distinguished but intimate meeting room.


The confirmation hearing was gaveled to order by Chairman John Boozeman of Arkansas. By prior invitation, I was asked to formally introduce the nominee. I noted the honor of being again before the Committee and joined by several former CFTC chairs and commissioners. I explained that I had gotten to know Mike Selig over a decade ago as a law clerk on my staff at the CFTC. I said I was struck then and remain impressed by his good nature, native intelligence, and interest in commodity futures markets. I explained that after George Washington University law school, Mike went on to build a law practice advising clients about the Commodity Exchange Act. I invited Mike to join me at Willkie, where he excelled, impressed his colleagues, and was elected a law firm partner, no small feat. In February 2025, he retired from the firm to serve as chief counsel to the SEC’s Crypto Task Force.


I said that the Mike Selig that I knew and that the Committee would hear from was a man of character, a straight shooter, and an honest broker. He treated people with respect and courtesy. He believed in robust and healthy markets overseen with energy and integrity and well enforced against those who sought to manipulate them. He believed in serving America’s farmers and producers and, I expected, that he would follow past chairs and travel across the country meeting with constituents to make sure that CFTC-regulated markets are serving their needs. I closed by saying that I was confident that the Committee members would enjoy working with Mike Selig just as I and so many others have.


Selig then gave his opening statement. He framed his prospective chairmanship around “common-sense, principles-based” regulation that preserves market integrity while keeping pace with innovation. He declared that “regulation by enforcement” and unwritten “staff lore” caused uncertainty for market participants and said he favored clear, accessible rules developed through formal processes. He emphasized safeguarding customer assets and policing for fraud and manipulation. He pledged ethics-forward leadership with strict adherence to conflicts protocols, and he committed to acting as a “strong cop on the beat.” On digital assets, Selig supported prompt, thoughtful implementation of any congressional legislation and rule-making to encourage domestic rather than overseas innovation.


Thereafter, Selig answered questions from the Committee members. He handled himself skillfully, with discipline and insight. He said that overregulation had driven activity offshore and impeded hedgers’ ability to access essential risk-management tools. He vowed to streamline agency processes so that market participants need not navigate opaque, unwritten rules. On event contracts, Selig signaled deference to courts on statutory interpretation while reiterating the Commission’s duty to monitor markets, especially those readily susceptible to manipulation. Selig described his approach to leadership as upholding the law, prioritizing investor protection, and deploying flexible, principles-based tools that adapt to market innovations.



After two hours or so, the meeting was gaveled to an end. I shook Selig’s hand and congratulated him on the success of his first hearing. I told him that I was certain there would be many more hearings where he would appear as CFTC chairman. Later, I joined him and dozens of other friends and former colleagues for celebratory drinks at, where else, the Monocle. I was proud of the once-young law clerk who had started work in my office not quite a decade before. I knew that the CFTC would be in great hands.


A week later, the Senate Agriculture Committee confirmed Selig’s nomination. On December 22, 2025, Michael S. Selig was sworn in today to serve as the 16th chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission.





## Getting to Work



Since taking the reins, CFTC Chairman Selig has picked up right where he left off, working with Paul Atkins at the SEC, focused on harmonizing regulatory frameworks between their agencies, particularly concerning cryptocurrency and digital assets. Their joint efforts are directed at clarifying product classifications and enhancing regulatory clarity to foster innovation in digital financial network technologies and tokenization.


To support his innovation-forward agenda, Selig moved quickly to build a first-rate team. His choice for chief of staff said much about his intentions. Amir Zaidi had served as both a senior advisor and director of the Division of Market Oversight during my chairmanship—the period that produced the first federally-regulated bitcoin futures contract. There was no one in Washington who knew the agency’s markets and mission more thoroughly.


For senior advisory roles, Selig drew from the best of both government and private practice. Michael Passalacqua brought deep expertise in crypto assets and blockchain technology sharpened during his time on my team at Willkie Farr & Gallagher. Meghan Tente, who had served as chief of staff to acting chair Caroline Pham and was a trusted colleague from my own tenure, contributed seasoned institutional knowledge. Cal Mitchell arrived from the Treasury’s Office of Legislative Affairs, having previously served Senator Bill Hagerty—a connection that would prove useful as the Clarity Act negotiations heated up. On the communications and agricultural fronts, Brooke Nethercott—a former aide to House Financial Services Committee Chairman French Hill—took charge of the Office of Public Affairs, while Emma Johnston, drawn from Senator Tommy Tuberville’s office, was named senior agriculture advisor, keeping faith with the CFTC’s core constituency of American farmers and producers. Taken together, the team’s collective depth of regulatory, congressional, and private-sector experience left little doubt about Chairman Selig’s seriousness of purpose as he steered the CFTC into one of the most consequential periods in the agency’s history.


At the SEC, Paul Atkins also got off to a fast start. During his first year of chairmanship, Atkins has pursued a pro–capital-formation, deregulatory agenda emphasizing cost–benefit analysis in rulemaking, while reducing disclosure burdens and streamlining reporting. The SEC has put forward market-structure reforms to boost competition and securities market liquidity. It has increased securities industry outreach and coordination with overseas regulators, adopted a more welcoming stance toward emerging digital assets through greater engagement with the crypto sector, and aligned enforcement priorities to be more selective in prosecuting perpetrators of fraud and manipulation, while favoring settlements and policy guidance for rule infractions that do not cause investor harm.


Atkins’ leadership has received the close support of Commissioners Hester Peirce and Mark Uyeda. In particular, Hester Peirce continues to persistently advocate for better-tailored regulation, clearer guidance, and crypto-positive safe harbors, using speeches, dissents, and rulemaking comments to push for streamlined disclosures and predictable treatment for digital-asset firms. Meanwhile, Mark Uyeda has backed cost-benefit analysis, reduced compliance burdens, and market-structure changes to boost competition and liquidity, contributing votes, statements, and technical input that helps shape rule proposals, while directing the Commission’﻿s coordination with international standard-setting bodies like the International Organization of Securities Commissions.


I watched all of this with quiet satisfaction. Together, Selig and Atkins had the talent around them to match the ambition of the moment—and within months, they would deliver one of the more consequential acts of crypto regulatory clarity in American history.







## Clearing the Air



In mid-March 2026 at the important annual DC Blockchain Summit,1 CFTC Chairman Selig gave a visionary address entitled the “Trust Revolution.”2 Speaking live to the Summit audience, he described crypto and digital network technology as part of a broader revolution in how decentralized, permissionless systems can rebuild trust in financial and information systems and commercial transactions. He portrayed the evolution of financial markets from open outcry trading pits to electronic trading to algorithmic trade execution as steps in the modernization of trust. In that light, he said that distributed ledger technology, smart contracts, decentralization, and open source protocols were not fringe innovations, but the next evolution in the automation of commercial trust: transparent, auditable systems where obligations are executed by coded smart contracts and not through the discretion of single siloed institutions.


Not to my surprise, Selig identified crypto not merely as some alternative investment asset class but as the foundation of a new architecture of finance. He said:



As financial markets move on-chain, I believe the United States should serve as the base layer where builders choose to deploy their systems powering this new frontier of finance.3




Selig said that regulators must provide a balanced, pro-innovation framework rather than stymie innovation through enforcement action or other means. He criticized Biden-era regulatory overreach and practices, including debanking, that drove innovators overseas. Selig emphasized the CFTC’s role in enabling US leadership in financial innovation while preserving investor protection. He expressed support for decentralized ecosystems, including transparent, open-source infrastructure such as public blockchains, DeFi, and prediction markets as mechanisms that garner the trust of participants. He noted that the “trust revolution” in financial markets democratizes financial markets by giving anyone with an internet connection the ability to lend, borrow, trade, and verify information on shared public rails.4 He said it was deeply aligned with American values, private property rights, and free market capitalism.5


In coordinated actions timed for the conference, the SEC and CFTC released joint interpretative guidance laying out the application of Federal securities laws to certain crypto assets and transactions.6 The guidance established a classification system or “taxonomy” for digital tokens. The guidance clarified which digital tokens are regulated by the SEC as securities, which may be regulated by the CFTC as commodities, which are regulated by banking regulators as stablecoins and which are unregulated digital tools (such as membership tokens, credentials and domain names) and digital collectables (such as meme coins, game tokens, digital artwork and non-fungible tokens). The notable impact of the release is that most crypto assets are not securities by and of themselves. The guidance also addressed how crypto activities like mining, staking and other matters are treated under law.


The remarkable aspect of the token taxonomy announced by the two chairmen is its voluntary relinquishment of agency jurisdiction over some digital tokens, namely digital tools and collectables. In announcing the tokenization taxonomy, Chairman Paul Atkins said, “The SEC’s persistent failure to provide clarity is over. [The just announced token taxonomy] returns the SEC to its core mission and statutory authority of protecting investors in securities transactions. We are not the Securities and ‘everything’ commission anymore!”7


The token taxonomy, jointly released by the SEC and CFTC, is perhaps the most important piece of US crypto regulatory guidance ever produced. It provides clear and simple categorization of different digital tokens, removing a cloud of regulatory uncertainty that has hung over crypto from the beginning.8 It critically identifies specific cryptos like Bitcoin, Ethereum, Solana, and XRP (all described earlier) as digital commodities under CFTC supervision and not as digital securities under SEC authority.9 In doing so, the guidance brings to an end almost a decade of legal jeopardy for digital asset innovators and the businesses they serviced.


Ryne Miller, a partner at Morrison & Foerster and one of the best of a new breed of digital asset lawyers, captured the significance of a Commissioner-level interpretation, noting, “its practical and persuasive power will shape industry behavior, judicial analysis, and enforcement policy for the foreseeable future—shifting us from regulation-by-enforcement to a rules-based framework.”10


What the taxonomy implicitly concedes is remarkable for twenty-first-century American polity: limits on the regulatory agencies’ own power. It excludes from SEC authority four of five categories of digital assets. It also excludes two categories—“Digital Collectibles” and “Digital Tools”—from oversight by any agency. The latter category includes utility tokens that were aggressively challenged by the SEC under the Gensler administration. As a result, Digital Collectibles and Digital Tools should now be massive categories for American innovation and development without fear of prosecution.


The adoption of the token taxonomy by the SEC and CFTC brings much-needed and long-overdue clarity to the crypto ecosystem. Financial institutions that have remained on the crypto sidelines now will be free to adopt digital network architecture, integrating stablecoins, tokenizing assets, and offering crypto trading, with much more to come. Undoubtedly, future SEC and CFTC administrations could abruptly (although probably not easily) overturn the 2026 joint agency token taxonomy. Thus, there is a need for Congressional legislation to codify the taxonomy into law. Fortunately, that is nearly at hand.







## Clarity



On May 14, 2026, eight years after my celebrated appearance before the Senate Banking Committee calling for a sensible and sound legal framework for digital assets, the same Committee cleared for Senate passage a crypto market structure bill called the Digital Asset Market Clarity Act. The Senate legislation creates a comprehensive crypto market-structure regulatory framework. Among other things, it classifies digital assets into categories and assigns regulatory jurisdiction accordingly; gives the CFTC exclusive authority over cash or “spot” markets for digital commodities; establishes provisional registration regimes for crypto market participants, such as exchanges and brokers; sets up a specific disclosure framework for digital securities offerings; limits yield-bearing uses of stablecoins on unregulated platforms and, finally, prevents the Federal Reserve from issuing a retail-focused CBDC.


The Senate Bill is similar to legislation passed by the House of Representatives in July 2025 led by committee chairman Glenn “GT” Thompson (R-PA-15) and French Hill (R-AR-2).11 Like the House bill, the companion Senate legislation is designed to do precisely what its name suggests: bring digital asset activity into a clear, workable regulatory framework. But clarity is not the same as leniency. The legislation creates new obligations, new supervisory hooks, and new agency rulemakings—while expressly preserving the full suite of authorities that federal law enforcement agencies already wield to pursue money laundering, sanctions evasion, fraud, and scams. This bill does not make it easier for criminals to use crypto. It makes it harder for anyone—criminal or compliant—to operate in the shadows.


The CLARITY Act is definitely not crypto “deregulation.” Rather, it is hundreds of pages of new registration requirements, mandatory disclosures, compliance undertakings, and agency rulemakings that do not exist anywhere in current law. By any honest measure, it is a substantial expansion of regulatory reach—one that responsible market participants have long said they are willing to accept, provided the rules are clear, consistent, and fairly applied.


The CLARITY Act cleared the Banking Committee with a bipartisan vote of 15-9, with two Democrats—Senators Ruben Gallego and Angela Alsobrooks—joining every Republican. The support of the two Democrats was only assured after tangled negotiations the night before and the early morning in the vestibule just behind the Committee dais.12 Notably, 44 amendments proposed by Senator Elizabeth Warren fell, one after another, confirming that the Warrenite insistence that traditional finance be insulated from digital asset innovation has lost its potency in post-Biden Washington.13


Of the major elements of the CLARITY Act that were still being negotiated right up to the Committee vote was an item that would seem to have been addressed during the GENIUS Act negotiations the year before. That is the paying to stablecoin holders interest or “yield” on their holdings. The GENIUS Act bans stablecoin companies like Tether and Circle from sharing yield with customers to avoid direct competition with banking accounts. But there is nothing in GENIUS to stop trading platforms, such as Coinbase that has a revenue sharing partnership with Circle, to offer yield to depositors. In fact, Coinbase is currently paying yield of between 3.5 and 4%, which is more than the average amount of interest paid on bank savings accounts.


Trade associations representing the American banking industry lobbied hard to insert language into the CLARITY Act to prohibit such payment of yield. They argued that it would incentivize bank customers to reduce deposits in favor of stablecoin holdings, leading to a reduction in credit available to consumers, small businesses, and farmers.14 They warned of the potential for an uneven playing field in which stablecoins deposits would not have the same consumer protections as banks.15 The banks’ worries, however, may have been less about deposit flight and more about profits. A Jefferies report found that stablecoins are unlikely to spark a sudden run on US bank deposits but could steadily erode bank earnings as digital dollars gain traction.16 Americans are decidedly in favor of the opportunity, ability, and freedom to earn rewards on stablecoins.17 They believe that the bank lobbyists seek to thwart the Clarity Act out of fear of competition, not because of consumer protection.18


In February 2026, I penned an online op-ed calling for passage of the CLARITY Act and offering a solution to the current Congressional stalemate.19 I argued that a workable compromise was already available if the two sides could get past their talking points. Congress could explicitly enable federally regulated banks—including community banks—to offer yield on payment stablecoins. Banks would gain a clear, federally sanctioned revenue and customer-acquisition opportunity in the stablecoin market, especially important for community banks seeking to remain competitive in a world of mega-banks and scaled payment platforms. Crypto platforms, meanwhile, would retain the incentive structures that benefited their customers and are available under existing law. Congress would get to move market structure legislation forward and create a bill that can pass. And, most importantly, the American consumer would benefit from increased competition and the ability to share in the yield potential of their own money.


A few weeks later, I explained in a podcast that American banks had more to gain by passage of the CLARITY Act than did the crypto industry. Using plain language, I told Scott Melker, the penetrating host of the Wolf of All Streets Podcast, “The banks need this more than crypto. Their general counsels are telling their boards, ‘You can’t invest billions of dollars to build these digital rails unless you’ve got regulatory certainty.’ American banks cannot afford regulatory uncertainty.”20 I warned that if American banks thwart the legislation and sit out crypto innovation, the crypto community will continue to innovate and build and do so offshore, if necessary. I said, “If the banks resist this now, it’s not going to go away. It’s just going to go to Europe. It is going to Asia… and then American banks will say (in a few years), ‘Whoa, our analog, indentity-based, message-based system is no longer working anywhere outside [the United States].”21 America’s banks cannot afford to ignore the new architecture of finance. They needed to invest in it if they wanted to stay relevant in a new era of digital networked finance.


American banks have a well-documented habit of fighting innovation until it overwhelms them—from interstate branch banking and ATMs to electronic clearing, online banking, and mobile payments, usually arriving late and always paying a price for it.22 The Internet of Value will not pause for them to rediscover that lesson. This time, the cost of missing the moment will not be measured in lost market share alone, but in the long-term relevance of the American banking system itself.


The so-called yield issue was not the only sticking point for final Senate passage of the CLARITY Act. There are also political issues. Democrats have proposed an ethics clause to prevent Donald Trump, other senior government officials, and key family members from profiting from a wide range of crypto-related ventures.


The numbers are not in dispute. Trump’s 2025 financial disclosure showed more than $1 billion in crypto income for the year,23 including over $500 million from World Liberty Financial, the venture he co-founded with sons Eric Trump and Donald Trump Jr.,24 and an additional $635 million in royalties from the $TRUMP meme coin.25 That income arrived in the same year his administration ended numerous cases and investigations into digital asset companies opened under the Biden administration and pushed Congress to pass market-structure legislation that would directly shape the value of those same holdings.26


The Democrats’ insistence on an ethics clause, however partisan its packaging, rests on a legitimate point. Government officials with rule-making or enforcement authority over an industry should not hold financial stakes in its outcome. I believe that standard should apply to anyone in that position, regardless of party, even Presidents. I say this with some personal conviction: during my own time in government service, I chose to refrain from any digital asset investing for exactly this reason.


But the honest treatment of this issue cannot stop at the White House gates. Congress has its own long and bipartisan record of members trading on non-public information—a record that predates crypto by decades27 and that neither party has shown much appetite to fix.28 An ethics reform that reaches into the executive branch while leaving Capitol Hill exempt will not read as principle; it will read as selectivity, and it will be treated as such by the public and by the industry alike.29 The honest answer is not “everybody does it, so never mind.” It is that digital assets have simply exposed—with unusual clarity—a gap in federal ethics law that has existed for years and now needs closing across all three branches, not just the one currently under partisan scrutiny.30







## The Final Mile



Both the “yield” and “ethics” issues had proven intractable throughout early 2026. In late March 2026, a deal seemed to emerge on the yield issue that would ban platforms from offering yield “directly or indirectly” on stablecoins. According to Eleanor Terrett and Crypto-in-America, the proposal would apply broadly to digital asset firms and bar anything “economically or functionally equivalent” to interest. Activity-based rewards like loyalty or promotional programs would still be permitted, and regulators would be tasked with defining permissible incentives and anti-evasion rules within a year. According to Terrett, “Crypto policy leaders [were] split over the new legislative text.”31


The breakthrough came on May 1, 2026, when Senators Tillis and Alsobrooks reached a bipartisan compromise prohibiting passive yield on stablecoin balances—the kind of automatic interest a money-market fund pays—while permitting limited “activity-based rewards” tied to specific user actions like making payments, completing transfers, or participating in platform programs. Under the compromise, Circle could not pay a user 4% simply for parking USDC in a wallet, but it could pay a small reward for using USDC to settle a payment, refer a merchant, or participate in an issuer-run loyalty program. Unfortunately, the compromise did not satisfy everyone. On May 9, the three largest US banking trade groups—the Independent Community Bankers of America, the Bank Policy Institute, and the American Bankers Association—formally rejected the Tillis-Alsobrooks compromise, just four days before the scheduled markup. Members of the American Bankers Association reportedly sent more than 8,000 letters to Senate offices criticizing the yield compromise.


Yet, despite the banking lobby’s opposition, the committee moved forward with its bipartisan vote to send the CLARITY Act to the full Senate floor. The May 14 Senate Banking Committee markup was the most important event in US crypto policy since the House passed the CLARITY Act in July 2025, representing the Senate’s first formal committee debate over comprehensive digital asset market structure legislation. The bill was championed by Coinbase, Circle, and Ripple, as well as venture capital firm Andreessen Horowitz, with the White House also pushing for the bill and at times becoming active in negotiations between banks and crypto groups. The bill still has a long road—full Senate passage, reconciliation with the House, and the President’s signature—but the Tillis-Alsobrooks compromise was the essential bipartisan bridge that got it out of committee.


Eight years earlier, I had gone before the Senate Banking Committee and asked Congress to meet a new generation’s hunger for financial freedom with wisdom rather than fear. On May 14, 2026, it finally did.


The long wait was over. The work would go forward. The tower of traditional finance had finally gazed down on the square of crypto with grudging respect and, perhaps, a glimpse of the future.
