# Chapter 14 Booms, Busts, and Clarity




A consideration of technology booms and busts, the critical need for good regulatory structures to support the convergence of crypto and traditional finance







## Railway Booms and Busts



Writing in the late summer of 2025, Peggy Noonan drew on historian David McCullough’s celebrated observation that history imparts “a sense of navigation, a new realization of what we’ve been through and are made of.” 1 Having such a sense of historical proportion is helpful in navigating the current crypto boom.


In the mid-1800s, Europe was seized by a social frenzy of speculative investment in railroads. In the United Kingdom, private firms hatched grandiose construction plans and enlisted government help for licensure and funding, massively overbuilding thousands of kilometers of railway lines with dubious commercial value. When the bubble burst in the late 1840s, railroad companies went bust, leaving debts unsettled and ruining investors.2 A few decades later, on the other side of the Atlantic, so-called railroad barons knit together vast, redundant rail networks while engaging in fraudulent accounting and stock manipulation—fortunes built and lost by financiers with still-famous names like Fisk, Gould and Vanderbilt.3 That boom ended with a gigantic crash in 1893–1894, when about one quarter of all US railroads went bankrupt.4


Yet even after the dizzying speculative bubbles and epic market crashes, no one sought to tear up the rails, which firmly remained in place. Instead, the railroads that survived consolidated and, in time, continued to grow as powerful commercial infrastructures on both sides of the Atlantic. In Europe, they fueled enormous social mobility and the Victorian and Belle Epoch eras of prosperity. In North America, the railroad enabled westward migration and weaved together a set of disconnected regional economies into one North American continental economy, the strength and diversity of which remains unequalled today. In both Europe and America, railroads created greater commercial wealth, social mobility, and democratic expansion than ever before. The speculative railroad bubbles may have been overwrought, driven by a combination of healthy ambition and unhealthy malfeasance, and their collapse certainly destroyed immense value for investors. But none of that undermined the transformative impact of the technology that lay at the heart of all the froth, chaos, and frenzy.


The witticism that “history does not repeat itself, but often rhymes” is an apt image for the course of history’s technological breakthroughs, including the current digital asset revolution. It is entirely legitimate that an increasing number of today’s entrepreneurs and ordinary people are attracted to crypto for no reason other than they want to make money just as they did a century and a half ago with railroads. It is how free market economies work. Talented people turn their attention, try new ideas, take career risk, and attract capital to innovations that they think will generate a strong return, not to activities that cannot.


Entrepreneurs engage in “creative destruction”5 in which their innovations, technologies, and business methods emerge and “destroy” or replace older ones, in the way that automobiles destroyed the horse-and-buggy industry, digital photography mostly eliminated film photography, or streaming services disrupted network television. While it causes short-term disruption and can eliminate jobs in old industries, it ultimately creates new opportunities, industries, economic growth, and forms of wealth. Such wealth creation is jeopardized when the political class forestalls innovation to preserve the industrial status quo.


The historical pattern holds for crypto as surely as it did for railroads. A decade ago, as a CFTC commissioner, I witnessed firsthand the build-up in excitement and fervor of the Initial Coin Offering boom that I mentioned earlier.6 It was a time of incredible innovation and creativity. New projects were proposing novel uses for blockchain technology, from decentralized prediction markets to blockchain-based cloud storage solutions. I also saw firsthand the woeful amount of fraud, fake froth, and manipulation—rug pulls, account hacking, and token hyping7—that sadly are an integral part of crypto innovation. Those responsible include the master manipulators known in crypto circles as “ruggers” who conduct various investment scams, fake trading platforms, phishing expeditions, and Ponzi schemes. Their victims include ordinary citizens fearing that they are missing out on the opportunity to get rich quick.8 It continues today, and I have watched with concern as booms and then busts rock various crypto tokens, at times exacerbated by aggressive “tokenomics” that put retail investors at a disadvantage.9 They make me recall my late parents and the lesson of the de Pisis forgeries—a reminder that things too good to be true often are just that.


The same speculative instinct that fueled the ICO boom and empowered its fraudsters has found a new and more institutionally respectable expression in the growing fashion of digital token treasury strategies. I am concerned about the rising trend of companies raising enormous amounts of money to buy up Bitcoin and other tokens to create digital asset treasuries—a strategy that assumes an ever-rising value of crypto.10 As a passive tactic of converting excess cash into crypto, it is untroubling.11 Yet, for those digital asset treasures that issue mountains of US-dollar-denominated debt to fund crypto purchases, it may be a dangerous proposition in light of crypto’s historical price volatility. According to the respected crypto observer, Nic Carter, “It’s a lot of kindling being built up. There’s going to be a forest fire, and it’s going to be catastrophic,”12 a warning that carries more weight for having an historical precedent less than two decades in the past.


In CryptoDad, I detailed my experience leading up to the great financial crisis of 2008. I heard the assertions that ever-rising housing prices could support extraordinary amounts of consumer debt, the same logic that drives today’s bitcoin treasury strategy—an assumption of perpetually rising asset values with some financial engineering thrown in—what Bank of England Governor Mervyn King memorably called “alchemy” in the form of securitized debt13—underpinning the mortgage-backed securities boom that ended in 2008. Efficient markets and, eventually, economic gravity mean that nothing goes up in value forever. The winners are always those who get out early, and the losers are those left holding the bag. When crypto’s seemingly inexorable price rise inevitably pauses and corrects, the reversal may have broad fallout into the crypto economy and perhaps beyond.


Since hitting a record price of $126,000 in October 2025, Bitcoin has been in a pronounced slide. The broader market for digital assets has lost nearly $2 trillion in value as the manuscript of this book is being finalized in March 2026. Many fear a prolonged “crypto winter,” a cyclical downturn that has tended to arrive every four or so, as it last did in 2022.


The numbers tell the story clearly. According to Raghav Chopra, cofounder of Tephra Digital, the respected digital asset management firm: “In the fourth quarter of 2025 and first quarter of 2026, the digital assets market experienced a meaningful selloff, and Bitcoin declined over 40% from its peak caused by deleveraging, persistently elevated real interest rates, geopolitical tensions, delayed regulatory clarity, and a pronounced cross-asset rotation away from software-related assets and into physical commodities.”14


Yet none of this—not the speculative frenzy, messy and harmful as it has been, nor the current price retrenchment—will undermine the fundamental transformation in finance and money that is underway through crypto innovation. It is ever inescapable that speculation and transformation are opposite but integral parts of the process of emerging technological change. Time and technology continue to march forward. No one ripped up the steel rails after the 1890s crash, and no one is going back to paper checks, branch banking, and three-day wire transfers because of a fall in crypto prices. There will be a great many irrelevant “I told you so’s” in the New York Times.15 The responsible task is to anticipate the future, channel it wisely, and move forward.







## Crypto Investing



The Internet of value is at hand in America. Despite the current price decline, the convergence of traditional finance and crypto architecture is well underway. Following the inauguration of Donald Trump, the GENIUS Act, the joint token taxonomy, and, perhaps, the CLARITY Act, crypto is irreversibly going mainstream. Blockchain and stablecoins are “real” and will be incorporated into the current financial system, according to no less than Jamie Dimon, CEO of JPMorgan Chase.16


Traditional financial institutions have taken unprecedented steps to integrate digital assets into their businesses. Their starting point has been providing retail customers with direct access to cryptocurrency investing, which was previously only available through upstart trading platforms like eToro and Robinhood. For years, banks like JPMorgan and Goldman Sachs provided crypto access only to high-net-worth clients, but the breakthrough occurred in June 2023 when BlackRock, the world’s largest asset manager with a gold-plated reputation, applied to the SEC to list a spot Bitcoin ETF—an exchange-traded fund holding actual bitcoin rather than futures contracts, and buying and selling on stock exchanges like any other share for the first time making cryptocurrency directly accessible to ordinary brokerage accounts. Launched in January 2024, BlackRock’s Bitcoin ETF may be the most profitable product ever offered by the company, generating approximately $245 million in fees and nearing $100 billion in assets under management in just a short period.17 Its incredible success took the financial world by storm.


BlackRock also manages BUIDL—the BlackRock USD Institutional Digital Liquidity Fund—a tokenized portfolio of US Treasury securities and other assets deployable across blockchains and decentralized finance platforms.18 BlackRock is a key partner for Circle, investing its collateral in treasuries.19 Now, BlackRock is going even further, recommending that Bitcoin be included in model portfolios seeking exposure to investments that fall outside the traditional categories of publicly traded stocks, bonds, and cash.20


Following BlackRock’s success—and the even earlier lead of pioneers like Los Angeles-based Wave Digital Assets,21 some of the largest and most consequential institutions in global finance have crossed decisively into the digital asset arena. ARK Invest, Bitwise, CoinShares, Fidelity, Grayscale, Invesco/Galaxy Digital, and Van Eck22 have collectively placed tens of billions of dollars in regulated Bitcoin and Ethereum products within reach of ordinary investors. BNY Mellon and Citi have built the custody and banking infrastructure upon which the crypto ecosystem depends. Franklin Templeton and WisdomTree each offer multiple blockchain-based investment platforms spanning money market funds and various digital assets.23 Even Vanguard—an investment behemoth that once flatly refused to offer its clients exposure to crypto—has begun allowing them to buy into crypto ETFs, a conversion that would have been unthinkable not long ago.24


The pace of institutional commitment has only accelerated. In March 2026, Fidelity asked the SEC to fast-track the integration of trading, custody, and tokenized securities into everyday platforms. The following month, Franklin Templeton launched Franklin Crypto, a dedicated, institutional-grade crypto investment management unit under industry veterans Seth Ginns and my fellow proponent of crypto letters of marque, Chris Perkins.25


The regulatory transformation in Washington—marked by the rescission of SAB 121 that limited banks’ ability to service crypto customers, the new leadership of the various financial regulatory agencies, and the passage of the GENIUS Act—removed the structural barriers that had kept registered investment advisers and broker-dealers on the sidelines, and opened the door to stablecoin integration, tokenization of real-world assets, and on-chain settlement at institutional scale. The firms now engaged in this transformation collectively oversee approximately 22 trillion dollars in assets. What was once dismissed as the province of speculators and technologists has become, with remarkable speed, the defining strategic priority of mainstream American finance.


If Wall Street has been opening its doors to digital assets, Washington has been widening the frame. On May 19, 2026, President Trump signed an executive order directing federal financial regulators to review and streamline rules, guidance, and licensing processes that impede financial technology firms, and requesting the Federal Reserve to evaluate whether non-bank and uninsured firms can gain direct access to Reserve Bank payment accounts and services.26 In the EO, titled “Integrating Financial Technology Innovation into Regulatory Frameworks,” the President stated that the federal government must remove overly burdensome and fragmented regulations that create barriers to entry and that primarily benefit incumbent financial services firms.27


And despite the current sell-off in crypto prices, Chopra believes the longer-term investment case for crypto remains compelling, perhaps “stronger than any other market cycle in the history of the asset class.” He believes that “catalysts for institutional adoption, agentic AI, consumer payments, enterprise use cases, and a regulatory imperative to integrate—and not suppress—blockchain technology in financial markets have all set the stage.” In fact, he anticipates that “employment displacement from AI could cause a $1.5 trillion annual income shock requiring accommodative fiscal and monetary policy generating long-term structural tailwinds for Bitcoin and digital assets.”28







## Convergence



Wall Street’s embrace of crypto investment products—the ETFs, the tokenized funds, the model portfolio allocations—is the most visible sign of a transformation that runs far deeper than a new asset class finding its footing. These offerings are the storefront window. Behind it, something more fundamental is underway: the entire operating architecture of the financial system is being rebuilt on blockchain rails. The institutions that spent years offering their clients exposure to digital assets are now deploying that same technology to rewire the circuitry of global finance itself—how trades are cleared, how collateral is managed, how payments move, and how ownership is recorded. That convergence, still early but accelerating rapidly, is the real story.


In the area of payments, financial service firms are utilizing their own and third-party stablecoins to streamline transaction processes and reduce cost. Institutional trading firms are using crypto technology to accurately and automatically manage and record trading books. And in the area of contract management, firms are employing smart contracts to automatically execute commercial transactions when specific conditions are met, reducing the need for intermediaries. The ongoing adoption of crypto innovation in financial services is expected to continue, driven by the need for improved security and efficiency. As more institutions recognize the benefits, the landscape of financial transactions and asset management may undergo significant transformation.


Broadridge, the financial technology firm whose annual report on the development of distributed ledger technology has become an industry benchmark, captured the mood well. In its 2025 edition, Head of Digital Innovation Horacio Barakat stated, “DLT adoption continues to accelerate and an increasing number of firms are transitioning from pilot projects to live deployments. We are also seeing momentum move to the buy-side, as well as expanding use cases across issuance and custody. These developments underscore the need for scalable infrastructure to support the next phase of market transformation ….”29


A lot of this activity is centered around tokenization. In a celebrated piece in The Economist, BlackRock’s CEO Larry Fink and Chief Operating Officer Rob Kapito discussed how tokenization will modernize market infrastructure and enhance efficiency, transparency, and financial access by connecting traditional and digital finance.30 Tokenization of assets offers major opportunities not just for equity securities, but across many real-world asset classes, most notably US Treasury debt, real estate, private equity, fixed income, commodities, royalties generated from intellectual property, trade receivables, collectibles, insurance-linked securities, carbon/environmental credits, and other instruments. The benefits of tokenization are enabling fractional ownership, improved liquidity, faster settlement, automated payments, and precise provenance of title. Ahead, the authenticity and chain of title of fine art will be immutably recorded on digital ledgers, freeing future heirs like me from having to travel to remote border towns to uncover art forgeries that could have been authenticated with a smart device.


Grayscale, the world’s largest digital asset investment manager, expects rapid growth in asset tokenization over the coming years, facilitated by more mature blockchain technology and improved regulatory clarity. In their view, it would not be surprising to see tokenized assets grow by approximately 1,000% by 2030.31 Goldman Sachs is actively exploring tokenization,32 while JPMorgan has tokenized a private-equity fund in the form of a digital representation of ownership on its fund tokenization platform, Kinexys Fund Flow.33 Securitize and Ondo Finance have emerged as leading platforms for tokenizing a range of securities from US Treasuries and corporate debt to publicly traded equities, and DTCC’s SEC-approved tokenization service—covering the Russell 1000, ETFs, and Treasury securities—signals that the core infrastructure of American capital markets is itself moving on-chain. Even private credit is being put into tokenized form, converting traditional nonbank commercial loans and debt instruments into digital tokens on a blockchain to enhance liquidity, transparency, and operational efficiency.34


The always-on architecture of crypto—indifferent to time zones and trading hours—is now forcing traditional markets to reconsider their fundamental operating assumptions. Both the New York Stock Exchange35 and Nasdaq36 are moving to facilitate around-the-clock trading of tokenized securities. The Chicago Mercantile Exchange (CME) intends to launch 24/7 trading for cryptocurrency futures and options in the second quarter of 2026. The CME’s cross-town Chicago rival, the Cboe, also intends to offer near 24/5 trading in US equities.37


Tokenized bank deposits represent another area of enormous opportunity. By moving deposits onto programmable ledgers, they enable instant 24/7 settlement, automated yield optimization, seamless account portability, and collateral that can be pledged instantly for lending and margin—eliminating the cumbersome multi-step processes that define today’s bank transfers. The market is currently active but so far confined to institutional pilots, with major global banks including BNY, Citigroup, HSBC, JPMorgan, State Street, and US Bank leading the way. Broader retail availability is coming, and with it a new generation of products: time-locked savings, deposit-backed stablecoins, tokenized certificates of deposit, and cheaper cross-border treasury flows.38


As described in Chapter 3, SWIFT, the Belgium-based Society for Worldwide Interbank Financial Telecommunication, provides secure messaging services and standardized messaging formats that banks and financial institutions use to exchange payment instructions, securities, treasury securities, and trade messages worldwide. SWIFT is the epitome of traditional finance or, as it is sometimes dismissively referred to, “TradFi.” SWIFT does not move value; it moves messages. Bank A tells SWIFT to tell Bank B that a payment is coming, and separate systems handle the actual movement of money. The message and the settlement are different events, handled by different infrastructure, often in different time zones. That separation is the defining architectural assumption of contemporary correspondent banking. It’s also the source of most of its cost, delay, and operational risk.


In a tokenized world, that separation collapses. When a tokenized Treasury trades for a stablecoin, the trade and the settlement happen in the same instant. No waiting. No reconciliation. No counterparty risk sitting open overnight. The billions that banks currently spend on post-trade operations, the days of delay that tie up capital, the operational failures that cascade through chains of intermediaries, all of that compresses toward zero.


SWIFT has now unveiled the “Swift Ledger” to mark its evolution from telling banks that money moved to actually moving it. It is not meant to replace correspondent banking, but to complete it, to close the gap between agreeing to a transaction and finishing it, a gap that currently costs the industry billions a year and exposes it to risk every hour that gap stays open. If it works, SWIFT could become the neutral settlement backbone for twenty-first-century tokenized assets, not because SWIFT built the best technology, but because SWIFT is the only entity trusted by every institution that needs to be in the room. And trust is the thing that cannot be engineered from scratch. The SWIFT Ledger could represent legacy financial infrastructure by absorbing the digital network technology that was supposed to replace it. Tower gets the square. Only time will tell.


One area of enormous opportunity for digital asset tokenization is for trading collateral. Tokenizing trading collateral enables faster, cheaper transfers and real-time margining by moving assets onto programmable ledgers. It will loosen collateral liquidity and enable more precise reuse of collateral and the broadening of acceptable collateral types. Combined with smart contracts, tokenized collateral will automate the handling of collateral substitution and release and discounts referred to as “haircuts.” This promises to dramatically improve operational efficiency, auditability, and cross-market settlement across jurisdictions. It also reduces reconciliation and custody friction while creating opportunities for new funding and liquidity products, such as securities lending and tokenized repurchase agreements, potentially transforming the multi-trillion-dollar marketplace by which banks and financial institutions manage their short-term funding needs.39


Assisting the tokenization of collateral will be new network infrastructure to support digital asset collateral verification and intelligibility. Bluprynt, a company founded by Georgetown Law Professor Chris Brummer, is offering structured, machine-readable onchain credentialing of trading collateral. In traditional finance, questions of whether pledged collateral really exists and is not encumbered or pledged elsewhere are answered through a patchwork of legal filings, custodial records, clearing arrangements, repo infrastructure, and credit support documentation. But onchain collateral management requires its own analogous infrastructure of digital wallets, smart contracts, legal wrappers and oracle fees. Bluprynt attempts to fill that gap with its “Proof of Collateral” standard of digital collateral verification system.40


Baton Systems operates a fast, secure platform based on distributed ledger technology for 24-hour settlement of assets and wholesale payments to reduce risk, free trapped capital, and drive improved capital efficiency.41 Nasdaq’s trading platform Calypso is teaming up with cryptocurrency infrastructure provider Talos to launch a tokenized collateral solution and bridge the gap between traditional finance and cryptocurrencies. They estimate that the move will free up more than $35 billion in idle liquidity and enable institutions to streamline off-chain and on-chain trading for faster and cheaper settlements.42 And digital collateral is reaching beyond institutional trading markets. Remarkably, Fannie Mae is preparing to allow home buyers to pledge bitcoin and other cryptocurrencies as part of a down payment—a development that would have seemed fantastical even five years ago.43







## Mainstream Adoption



Tokenization of real-world assets is well underway, moving from pilots to limited live issuances, especially for tokenized treasuries, money market funds, private credit, and short-term fixed income. The process is being driven by asset managers, banks, and stablecoin/payment rails. Tokenization infrastructure has matured with custodians, issuance platforms, distributed ledger networks, oracles, and legal wrappers, while regulators run sandboxes and provide guidance in key jurisdictions. In December 2025, the Canton network announced a groundbreaking initiative to tokenize US government debt in partnership with the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation (DTCC), the primary custodian of tradable securities in the United States and another venerable TradFi institution making enormous strides to adopt and deploy distributed ledger technology.44


Realizing all these benefits will require development of systems for secure custody and interoperable standards, legal recognition of on-chain ownership, reconciliation with off-chain records, and strong investor protections (transparency, valuation, dispute mechanisms). Fortunately, one of the most important prerequisites, regulatory clarity, is finally falling into place. In March 2026, the CFTC staff released some guidance on how crypto assets could be used in trading markets.45 It made clear that some CFTC registrants could indeed treat certain crypto assets, including Bitcoin and payment stablecoins, as margin collateral under specific conditions.46 Current advances in the US Uniform Commercial Code, providing certainty as to the enforcement of ownership rights in digital assets, will help make tokenized collateral a twenty-first-century reality.47


There is one nuance to the debate over tokenization that is necessary to mention at this point. Setting up a tokenization pilot or proof of concept is not necessarily that difficult. The real trick is getting liquidity for resulting tokenized assets, in particular for trading in secondary markets. “Everybody from BlackRock to Goldman, Citi and JPMorgan is saying that tokenization is the future,” according to Nadine Chakar, Managing Director, Global Head of DTCC Digital Assets, in a 2023 interview with Forbes.48 “The problem is interoperability and liquidity,” said Chakar. “Banks team up with company XYZ, do an issuance, and then post a press release. What happens next? Nothing. They become Pet Rocks because they cannot go anywhere.”49


Well, they are not pet rocks anymore. Companies from Coinbase to Kraken and now Robinhood are tokenizing traditional stocks to let users buy and sell them on interoperable blockchains. Tokenized treasuries, like those on BlackRock’s BUIDL platform, are being made available at major DeFi platforms like Uniswap.50 Larry Fink, BlackRock’s legendary CEO even wrote in his 2025 Chairman’s Letter to Investors that “tokenization makes investing much more democratic.” He went on to sing tokenization’s praises, pointing out that tokenization and fractionalization makes it easier for people to get access to novel types of instruments, and lower friction can enhance yields, driving up sources of income. He then went on to point out, “One day, I expect tokenized funds will become as familiar to investors as ETFs.”51


In May 2026, Paxos Securities Settlement Company registered as a clearing agency under SEC Section 17A of the Securities Exchange Act of 1934—the result of seven years of engagement beginning with a 2019 no-action letter.52 The approval makes Paxos the only blockchain-native firm registered to provide clearing and settlement services as a central securities depository in the United States, placing it inside the same regulated post-trade infrastructure layer as the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation. As a registered central securities depository, Paxos can now hold and settle traditional securities trades directly on blockchain rails, providing same-day settlement—a dramatic improvement over the current two-day settlement standard in traditional markets. In short, this is a landmark moment: blockchain technology has been formally admitted into the core plumbing of the US financial system—not as an experiment, but as regulated infrastructure.


If Paxos’ clearing agency registration confirmed that blockchain had earned its place inside the regulated infrastructure of American capital markets, the next announcement made clear that crypto-native firms were not content merely to participate in that infrastructure—they intended to own it. A few weeks before the SEC approved Paxos’ clearing agency registration, Bullish, the institutional-grade digital asset platform and owner of the cryptocurrency media outlet CoinDesk, announced that it would acquire Equiniti—a venerable global transfer agent serving nearly 3,000 public company issuers, 20 million shareholders, and processing $500 billion in annual payments—in a transaction valued at $4.2 billion.53 Transfer agents are among the most unglamorous but essential pieces of capital markets plumbing: they maintain the official shareholder records of public companies, process dividends, manage proxy voting and handle stock issuances and transfers. They sit at the very heart of how ownership of corporate America is recorded and transferred. By acquiring Equiniti, Bullish is not merely buying a financial services firm—it is seizing the ledger itself and rebuilding it on blockchain rails. The combination, in Bullish CEO Tom Farley’s words, delivers “all three elements required to become a powerhouse leading the blockchain era: end-to-end tokenization services, a unified transfer agent ledger, and broad blue-chip issuer relationships.”54


If the NYSE’s partnership with Securitize, DTCC’s SEC-approved tokenization service and Paxos’ clearing agency registration signal that the infrastructure of American capital markets is moving on-chain, then the Bullish–Equiniti combination makes the point unmistakably: the hierarchies of traditional finance are not merely accommodating the new decentralized architecture—they are being rebuilt from within by those who built that architecture in the first place.







## Evolution of Finance



On May 21, 2026, Wall Street Investment Bank Jefferies held its first annual digital assets investor conference in New York. The conference gathered executives from 35 digital asset companies alongside roughly 150 institutional investors and focused less on bitcoin price speculation and more on how blockchain systems are increasingly being integrated into traditional finance.55


In a report published afterward, Jefferies said it expects a surge of crypto-related public listings over the next two years and believes the sector could grow into a $1 trillion public market within five years. Other key findings are that institutional investors are shifting focus from bitcoin price speculation to the integration of blockchain infrastructure into core financial systems, including tokenized money market funds, private credit and settlement networks; traditional financial firms are increasingly partnering with crypto-native providers rather than competing with them; and stablecoins and tokenized payments are cited as key areas of near-term growth. Notably, the report stated: “Investors frequently overestimate the magnitude of tech disruption in the near term and underestimate it over the longer term.”56


In giving the opening address at the Jefferies conference, I said,



Something profound is underway in the global financial system. The once-impenetrable walls separating the tower of traditional finance from the town square of blockchain and digital assets are not merely cracking—they are coming down entirely. The great institutions of American finance have opened their gates, offering crypto products, embracing tokenization, and building the new infrastructure of a digital financial system. Stablecoins, long dismissed as a curiosity, now process tens of trillions of dollars in transaction volume, rivaling the throughput of Visa and PayPal. Real-world assets—from Treasury instruments to real estate—are being placed on-chain, their value unlocked through fractional ownership and near-instantaneous settlement. The architecture of finance itself is being rebuilt.




What we are witnessing is not a disruption of finance—it is its evolution. For years, regulators and incumbents treated the town square of digital assets as an adversary to be contained. I know. I was there. I had to fight to be heard. Full-page letters in the Wall Street Journal demanded I stop. Yet, I kept going. So did many, many others.


Today, with the passage of the GENIUS Act and a more receptive regulatory posture in Washington, the framework for responsible digital finance is finally taking shape. CLARITY is on the horizon. The early walled gardens of blockchain technology are giving way to open networks built for the real world of connected markets. AI is beginning to interface directly with digital asset infrastructure. The vision that so many of us in the public square fought for—a financial system that is open, efficient, transparent, and worthy of the next generation’s trust—is no longer a promise.


It is becoming the architecture of modern finance itself.

# Conclusion: What Lies Ahead




Centralization and decentralization frequently collide in both technological and political revolutions and today’s internet of value revolution will be no different; what matters is building a system that reflects the values of a free society and free people. The time to do so is now




The premise of this book is that crypto is much more than some new alternative investment asset class. It is a whole new digital network architecture of finance, banking and money. In the coming decades, crypto will provide a new foundation for the global financial system built upon a complex blend of digital distributed networks: some permissioned and some permissionless, some private and some fully transparent, some fully decentralized like Bitcoin and operated collaboratively by their users and some others highly centralized and run by global banks, tech firms, and even sovereign governments such as China and the European Union. Undoubtedly, the digital network future—the Internet of Value—will be as complex as the analog network past: a kaleidoscope of different digital networks of value.


This revolution in traditional finance is ultimately more transformative than the first wave of the internet—the Internet of Information—that started in the late twentieth century and changed everything we know about information gathering and social networking. The current wave, an Internet of Value, is doing to material things what the internet of Information did to immaterial things, making them accessible, distributable, and instantly movable across the globe. Those material things include real estate, gold, currencies, debt, stocks, commodities, automobile ownership, software licenses, even things like carbon credits and natural resources, as well as intangibles like personal reputation and identity. In this wave, it will soon be possible to send and receive things of value instantaneously, inexpensively and without intermediation by large, faceless institutions operating as correspondents, validators and recordkeepers. Rather, it will be done directly by digital handshakes on a network of personal computers and smart phones powered by cryptography, tokenization, and shared ledgers. In this internet wave, trading collateral will be precisely tracked and utilized, securities issuers will finally be connected to each and every one of their shareholders and everyday people will send money and other things of value to family members on the other side of the world as easily and directly as they now send a digital photo.


Earlier, we looked at some of the prominent crypto blockchains, major stablecoins and some “killer apps” for this new technology, including prediction markets whose discovery of “unvarnished truth” is a new bulwark of liberty. We considered the mission of the Digital Dollar Project in addressing three essential questions: how to “future-proof” the dollar for this coming world of competing digital networks; how to maintain the dollar’s world reserve currency status and, perhaps most importantly, how to preserve the universal values of economic freedom for which the dollar has historically stood.


We looked at the Biden administration’s resistance to digital network innovation, the possible ideological reasons behind it, and its detrimental impact on US innovation. We also reviewed the second Trump administration’s current crypto initiatives from the easily accomplished ending of bad public policy to the much harder enactment of sound and durable crypto legal frameworks. Through it all, I sought to present an insider’s account of the struggle of crypto—new digital architecture of finance—to emerge from the crossfire of Republicans and Democrats’ different political visions of how to allocate capital to participants in the twenty-first-century digitally networked economy. The outcome of this debate will either empower or inhibit humanity in the digital future of finance and money itself.


As with similar technological transformations such as the nineteenth-century railroad booms in Western Europe and the United States, the Internet of Value will be accompanied by fraud, manipulation, and speculative booms and busts, the rotation of fear and frenzy that frequently accompany technological transformation. The Internet of Value revolution will be no different and will continue whatever market highs and market lows may come. And they will come. Yet, any future crypto market crash will not mean the crypto naysayers have been right all along. However rocky, time will not halt these digital network innovations from delivering broad economic and social change. For some in both the public and private sectors, it will be a huge loss of monetary and social control. For them, market frenzy and investor speculation will be cited as reasons to hold onto power. For many, many others, however, this could be a huge leap forward for human liberty and autonomy.


What matters now is completing the process of enacting sound regulatory frameworks for this new technology as I called for eight years ago in my 2018 “CryptoDad” Senate hearing. It has now been accomplished in the GENIUS Act for stablecoins, albeit imperfectly when it comes to individual privacy. Further well-suited regulatory frameworks are needed for applications of crypto technology in the form of marketplaces for the tokens themselves and applications such as event prediction markets. Fortunately, that work is underway at the administrative level under new and determined leadership at the SEC and the CFTC. What is still needed is legislative action to pass the CLARITY Act. Also required is for the quarrelsome crypto industry to exercise more consistent maturity and seriousness through the leadership of its many fine senior figures, including a new cadre of crypto professionals in law, accountancy, finance and, indeed, politics.


Undoubtedly, crypto innovation by the US private sector is now being encouraged by the Trump administration with a Silicon-Valley-like gusto and pace that is rarely seen in government. Nevertheless, it is important to recall and take stock of the previous administration’s active role in hindering digital network innovation, debanking its participants, and prosecuting rather than rulemaking for its activities. We must remember that misadventure not for partisan reasons, but because attempting to forestall innovation will never work and will always backfire on those who try it. It should never happen again in America.





## Back to the Beginning



Earlier on I shared the remarks I gave in England’s Windsor Park following the 2024 US elections. When I left stately Cumberland Lodge the next day for Heathrow Airport, my driver took a rather picturesque drive along the meandering upper Thames River, west of London. The view from the backseat of the black sedan was of a low and verdant meadow under an early afternoon autumn sun. A signpost said “Runnymede,” a name that resonated for me from my youth reading thrilling stories of Robin Hood and his merry men protecting the downtrodden folks of Nottingham under the reign of evil King John. I knew that Runnymede was the real-life location for the sealing of the Magna Carta, a charter of liberties sealed in 1215 between King John and the powerful English barons.


I saw another sign for the “Magna Carta Memorial.” I asked the driver to slow down and pull in. Outside of the car, the air had the crispness of late autumn and the scent of riverside. I wandered down a gravel path away from the modest parking area toward a broad, still-green meadow. Not far along was a round stone pavilion. Classically designed, the pavilion’s conical roof was held up by eight pillars, signifying eight centuries of the British constitutional order. Inside the pavilion were dozens of inscriptions written in both English and Latin, taken directly from the document it commemorated. Their purpose was to highlight the significance of Magna Carta and its impact on the rule of law and individual liberties.


Magna Carta, or “the great charter,” is a summation of ancient liberties against the sovereign. Some of its key provisions include the right to due process and a fair trial, freedom from arbitrary arrest and imprisonment, the obligation of the king to obtain the consent of a council of barons for taxation, freedom of the English Church from royal interference, and the establishment of a committee of 25 barons to ensure the king’s compliance with the charter.


The document is the starting point for freedom of the citizenry from absolute sovereign power. It sets out familiar rights that we now take for granted—such as the right to trial by a jury of one’s peers, the freedom from being arrested without a formal charge, the protection of one’s personal property from confiscation by the sovereign without compensation, and the antecedent to the famous American colonists’ prohibition on taxation without representation. Magna Carta created the concept of property rights of citizenry and their right to privacy from government surveillance.


Magna Carta is history’s first recorded acknowledgement that there could be and should be limits on sovereign power. Certainly, its rights and protections needed to evolve and expand far beyond a small group of English Barons. And indeed, they did over the coming centuries in the English-speaking world and beyond to provide protections to an increasing number of everyday citizenry. When American yeomanry stood up to British King George at Lexington and Concord and their representatives signed the Declaration of Independence a little over a year later in Philadelphia, it was in the belief that the crown was violating their rights as freemen first set out in Magna Carta.1


Today, Magna Carta is widely accepted as a bedrock constitutional document underpinning modern democracy. Its principles and ideas have endured for centuries, influencing legal systems and constitutions around the world. The document has been referenced and invoked in landmark cases and legislation, including the 1689 English Bill of Rights, the 1792 US Bill of Rights, and the 1948 United Nations Universal Declaration of Human Rights. According to no less than King Charles III, “Magna Carta is cited in at least 160 Supreme Court cases since 1789, not least as the foundation of the principle that executive power is subject to checks and balances.”2 The American Bill of Rights in the US Constitution and its protections of free speech, free press, rights to privacy, and freedom of religion all derive from Magna Carta, as do the constitutions of almost every major contemporary democracy. Magna Carta is a bulwark of human liberty and freedom and a foundational pillar of constitutional democracy. It is part of the deep cultural inheritance of modern civilization that every generation should revere and must defend.


As I exited the quiet pavilion back to the black sedan, I was pleased to read an inscription at the base of the pavilion that its construction and dedication were financed and led by the American Bar Association. I was proud that my chosen profession saw fit to acknowledge its indebtedness to the rule of law established long ago in this very place.








## The Choice Is Ours



In my previous book, CryptoDad: The Fight for the Future of Money, I noted that at the end of World War I, French Prime Minister Georges Clemenceau is reported to have said, “War is too important to be left to the generals.” Similarly, with respect, money and finance are too important to be left to central bankers or politicians.3 That is because money is as much a social construct as it is a government one.4 Its success derives indeed from both the trust and consent of the ordinary citizens who place value in it.5 They—and we—have as much to gain from the digital networking of the world’s financial systems as do governments. But free people also have much to lose if we do not speak up in the discussion over what values will be encoded in digital money and all other forms of digitally networked systems of value. Thus, the important work of the Digital Dollar Project will continue considering how best to “future-proof” the dollar and maintain its reserve currency status in a world of competing digital networks and, perhaps most importantly, to preserve the universal values of economic freedom for which the dollar has historically stood.


What is clear is that the twenty-first century will see a range of digital assets and digital currencies whatever the United States does. Change is coming; it is inevitable. The same may be said for the digital network future. It cannot be forestalled. And it is not a choice between frenzy and advancement, speculation and innovation. It is not a choice between action or nonaction, the future or the past, analog or digital, permissioned or permissionless, stablecoins or CBDC. There will be all of the above.


The real choice is between financial freedom and financial control—and the role of democratic society in driving key design choices and related regulatory frameworks. The question is whether these new technologies, especially the digital networks of value they power, will make societies more open, prosperous, and free. Will they empower or inhibit humanity in the digital future of money?


Democratic societies and free people must reassert their time-tested principles—principles stretching back to Magna Carta—in a world seeking a sound foundation for a new digitally networked future. The choice between freedom and servitude in the digital future is being made now and must be made by us: you and me. Digital networks of value that effectively protect financial privacy in lawful transactions will garner the enduring confidence of free people and those around the world who aspire to live free. The nation that fosters such digital value systems will win the future and the trust and consent of its citizenry.


Now is therefore the time to take steps to ensure that the digital value networks of the twenty-first century are not only on the cutting edge of speed, reliability, and inclusion, but also reflect enduring virtues of free enterprise, free trade, and economic expression against both government and commercial digital surveillance, censorship, and control. The current policy direction of Washington is clear: support for technological innovation to make the United States the crypto capital of the world. It would seem to be both an appropriate and a characteristically American course of conduct. We are an aspirational people. Whether we trace our roots to Europe, Africa, the Asian subcontinent, the Far East or the Western Hemisphere itself, our predecessors generally came to the United States to flee oppression and pursue endeavors equal to their talents and aspirations, free of government dictates and limitations. But to do so, in every instance, required then—and requires today—undaunted courage, sometimes even rugged individualism.


There is every reason for optimism about the road ahead. That is because at its core, this technological revolution of digital networks, this Internet of Value, is one that empowers the individual over the collective. It empowers the decentralized square over the hierarchical tower. Individuals, no matter their origin or wealth, especially young people, have access to knowledge, computing power, and the ability to participate in building the future to a degree never before available in human history. They must strive, indeed, fight to preserve and enhance their liberty against the demonstrable frigidity of statist collectivism in the digital network future of the twenty-first century.







## Militia + Regulars



This book is published on the sestercentennial anniversary of the publication of the Declaration of Independence of the United States. At the outset, I likened British Colonial authority at the time of the Declaration as a venerable hierarchy of military and political power. I described how Paul Revere’s famous midnight ride called to arms a decentralized network of Massachusetts yeoman militia. That decentralized force was the shock troops that first challenged and bloodied the established Colonial hierarchy. But they did not defeat it. That came six years later at Yorktown at the hands of competing hierarchies: George Washington’s well-organized Continental Army and the Expedition Particuliere, Compte de Rochembeau’s French expeditionary force in North America.


What began as a challenge of decentralization, ended in hierarchy. Yet, the victorious power was a hierarchy steeped in the values of decentralization: local government, separation of powers, individual rights and liberties, and freedom of speech, assembly, and private property. In short, the American Revolution was an initial attack on centralized power by a decentralized network, followed by a synthesis of the two.


The American Republic has endured so long—250 years—because it is built upon limits derived from the Magna Carta on the hierarchical power of the US central government. Reflected in those limitations are enduring Enlightenment values of human liberty, chiefly: the rule of law and its eventual equal and impartial application to all citizens; private property essential for individual freedom and democracy; free minds based on the innate dignity of every human being and the desire for self-realization, personal integrity and unconstrained civic evolution; free markets allowing for risk taking in pursuit of prosperity and the inevitable consequences of both abject failure and spectacular success; and the free spirit of human aspiration to live a better life, build a healthier society, and climb higher than the generation before without any claim on the work, effort, or wealth of anyone else.


Reason exercised by a free citizenry, not confiscatory force by governments, is the basis for peace, prosperity, and human advancement. Peggy Noonan again captures the essence, “American free-market capitalism generates—it allows things to be brought to life. Socialism merely distributes what is. And in the end it always relies on lies, the first of which is always that it works. Our [American] way of doing things is unpredictable, chaotic, and can break your heart. People compete, and competition can be painful. People lose. But our system was invented to grow and make room for you. That’s what we have to make sure continues, its fantastic wide-openness.”6







## TradFi + DeFi



Decentralized crypto innovation may be likened to Revere’s loosely organized militia, challenging the established hierarchies of powerful political and commercial interests that for decades have controlled the custody and allocation of capital, value, and money. The battle between these new decentralized networks of value and formidable hierarchies of politics and finance is now being waged, drawing blood and designating its victors and its vanquished. It looked for a time under the Biden administration that the hierarchical forces of traditional finance in Washington and Wall Street had the upper hand in this battle. Encouraged by the second Trump administration, the tide appears to have changed, and many of the old financial hierarchies are yielding and quickly adopting the new innovations.


The question is whether the traditional hierarchies of value custody and allocation will be displaced by decentralized networks or be supplanted by a combination of fast-adopting legacy organizations and entirely new centralized hierarchies that master digital technology and recentralize it among themselves. More likely, however, the digitally networked future of finance will not be a binary choice between decentralized and centralized networks, but result in a third outcome: a future like the past, an uneasy but ever-present and healthy interaction and reciprocation between centralized financial hierarchies and decentralized networks.


In another Revolutionary War battle that precipitated the British retreat to Yorktown and their ultimate defeat, the American militia served a critical role alongside organized Continental troops. At Cowpens, American forces capitalized on the alternative strengths of the irregular militia by integrating them into their tactical planning.7 Recognizing that traditional linear military formations played to the strengths of hierarchically trained British troops, the Americans under Brigadier General Daniel Morgan organized the decentralized militia to engage in a feigned retreat, drawing British troops into a carefully prepared trap.8 The militia skirmished as intended, drawing in the British regiments. Once the British advanced, the disjointed militia faded away as planned. The British troops gave chase headlong into a deadly volley from the disciplined Continental regulars, who held firm and counterattacked the disoriented British forces. Meanwhile, the decentralized militia regrouped and filled in the Continental ranks. The combination of retreat and counterassault, organized ranks, and loose formations led to chaos for the British regulars, who fled in a disorganized and decisive rout.9 It was a decisive American victory—and a model for how disciplined organization and decentralized individual initiative can together defeat a more powerful, but rigid adversary.


If the future of digital networks of value is in fact a synthesis of centralized and decentralized control—then the question, indeed, the task, the duty for all of us is to make sure that the future of digital finance is one that is worthy of free society, within the spirit of the American Revolution and true to New World values of human liberty and financial freedom.







## The Choice Is Now



Each of us has a part to play in ensuring that outcome. The struggle between the tower and the square is as old as human civilization and as new as this minute’s blockchain transaction. What is different today is that the tools of decentralization—cryptography, tokenization, distributed ledgers—have placed in the hands of ordinary men and women a degree of financial autonomy that no previous generation has possessed. The tower will not yield without a fight. It never has. But neither has the square. That is the deepest lesson of Magna Carta, of Lexington and Concord, of Cowpens, and of every generation that chose liberty over the false comfort of centralized control. The Internet of Value is our generation’s square. It is ours to build, ours to defend, and ours to hand forward—worthy of the free people for whom it was made.


Standing in that quiet stone pavilion at Runnymede, surrounded by eight centuries of hard-won constitutional inheritance, I thought of the generations that had kept faith with the liberties first set down in that meadow. They did not do so by standing aside or hindering change. They did so by understanding, in each new age and with each new technology, that freedom is not self-executing. It must be claimed, defended, and re-encoded into whatever systems of power and exchange their era presents. The digital networks of value being built today are the Magna Carta of our time. What values they enshrine—and what values they quietly extinguish—must not be determined by governments alone, nor by central bankers or Silicon Valley entrepreneurs, but by free citizens who understand what is at stake and refuse to look away.


Paul Revere did not ride alone. His midnight cry only had meaning because ordinary men and women in darkened farmhouses chose to rise, take up their arms, and answer. The Internet of Value is sounding its own alarm today—not on horseback along a moonlit road, but across digital networks that span the globe. The question for our generation is the same one that faced the yeomanry of Massachusetts in April 1775: Will we rise to answer?


The values at stake are no less than theirs were—the right to hold property, to transact freely, to live without the surveillance and censorship of overreaching power, whether a sovereign or a corporation. Those values were first written down in a meadow at Runnymede eight centuries ago. They were fought for at Concord Bridge. They are now being encoded—or not—into the digital networks of value that will govern the financial life of the twenty-first century. The choice is ours.


Let us choose wisely, and let us choose freedom.








## Postscript: Erratum



In my earlier book, CryptoDad, I wrote that my mother’s cousin died fighting in World War II at the famous Battle of the Bulge. My brother Michael, an exacting historian, noted that the man’s death came weeks later and pointed me to this 1945 obituary.



Pvt. Eric B. Schwarz, 19 years old, son of Dr. and Mrs. Berthold T. D. Schwarz of 35 Godfrey Road, Upper Montclair, N. J., was killed in action Feb. 18 at Fraulautern, Germany, during the defense of a Saar River bridgehead by the 328th Infantry Regiment of the Twenty-sixth Division. Assigned to the regiment as a replacement only three weeks before his death, Private Schwarz died of chest wounds a few minutes after a mortar shell exploded near his outpost. He had enlisted in the Army in July of last year, after completing pre-medical training at the University of North Carolina. He was a member of the orchestra of the North Carolina Symphony Society, the University of North Carolina Symphony Orchestra and the university band. Private Schwarz was the composer of “The Kiwanis Greeting Song,” and “As the Flag Goes by.” While still in Montclair High School he was selected as a member of the all-State high school orchestra in New Jersey and conducted his own orchestra on Radio Station WAAT in Newark, N. J.10




Eighty-one years later, it is still staggering that a nineteen-year-old classically trained musician on his way to medical school would be killed on the Allied front lines only three weeks after arrival. Fraulautern, where Eric Schwarz fell, sat within the heavily fortified Saar region of the Siegfried Line—the massive German defensive fortification running roughly 390 miles along Germany’s western border, where some of the bitterest fighting of the war’s final months took place. On the Western Front in late 1944 and early 1945, four of five infantrymen were green replacements. They were strangers in all but uniform to the men beside them, individually slotted into war-toughened platoons with no shared history and no bond of trust. They suffered disproportionate casualties.


The American historian Stephen E. Ambrose, prolific author of the best-selling World War II histories Citizen Soldiers and The Victors, voices thoughtful criticism of the US policy of individual soldier replacements. Rather than rotating whole companies or platoons—groups of men who had bonded and developed trust—the American system pulled individual soldiers out of replacement depots and slotted them one by one into depleted front-line units as casualties mounted. My cousin, who left his studies to volunteer in service to his country, was one of those barely trained, but patriotic young men called upon to plug a gap.


I correct the record on the death of Private Eric B. Schwarz to honor his memory and his family’s sacrifice. He was, in one young life, so much of what America produces and then calls to sacrifice: a son, a musician, a future healer, a volunteer, a patriot. His story is also the common story—of the countless American families who have surrendered sons and daughters in defense of human liberty against totalitarian oppression and have been doing so for two and a half centuries. From Lexington and Concord to Fallujah and Operation Epic Fury in Iran, American independence has always rested on such sacrifice. It will continue to do so.

# Acknowledgments



In compiling acknowledgments for my first book, I said that “a writer has the time and space necessary to recall all the worthy people and influences that deserve recognition. … there is no possible excuse for a writer not to thank everyone whose support, encouragement and help contributed to the book and its story.” I certainly followed through by expressing gratitude for several hundred friends, family, and colleagues. I do not believe I missed anyone, not even one single high school teacher who took an interest in my intellectual development, let alone other important friends, mentors, and colleagues along my life’s journey and contributors to that first book.


Having covered so much ground last time around, I want to narrow the scope of personal gratitude to those to whom I owe thanks for producing this book, of which there are quite more than a few.


Specifically, I would like to thank the terrific Wiley team, including Sherri-Anne Curtis, Delainey Henson, Zach Schisgal, and Kevin Harreld, and copy editor Sheryl Nelson. I am grateful to my incomparable literary agent Tim Hays of Hays Media and to journalists Chuck Mackie and Steve Ehrlich, who read and improved early drafts of the manuscript. I thank Nick Davies and Ian Antonoff of Fortier for awesome launch publicity and Katie Bourne and Nina Truman for putting me in the right light. I am especially grateful to art conservator extraordinaire, Linda Crosnier. I thank Jim Harper for our collaboration and letting me tell a bit of his story. I thank my son, Luke, for helping me understand cutting-edge applications of crypto, distributed ledger technology, and AI through his experience as director of the New York FinTech Innovation Lab, senior manager of Technology Innovation Strategy at Accenture and now at Standard Chartered Bank. I’m glad one of us knows what is under the hood.


It takes a village to coordinate the affairs of a wayward sexagenarian eager beaver, and I am thankful for the support and fine service of bankers Tom Duffy, Brenden Squyres, and Ethan Tym, financial advisors John Voigt and Sal Asta, and accountant John Driessen. I am grateful for the service of my friend and bookkeeper, Doug Steele, who keeps my accounts straight; travel planner Mireille McQuade, who gets me to faraway places; drivers Viktor and Karin Ambartsumian, who get me to nearby places; master auto mechanic, Adam Varney, who keeps my old motors running; trainer Joseph Kim, who keeps my own motor running; the communications team of Jonathan Gasthalter and Carissa Felger, who protect my reputation; and my dear friend and personal lawyer, Sal Giampiccolo, who protects me and my family. (Don’t mess.) I am especially grateful to my former executive assistant, Antonetta Chiappone, without whose fluid organizational skills in managing my schedule I could have never have produced this book.


I am forever inspired by the memory of Michael D. Gill and his remarkable wife Kristina, and their children Sean, Brian, and Anika. I thank my fellow directors of the Michael D. Gill Memorial Society, including Kristina and Sean, Eric Pan, Luke Giancarlo, and Neal Kumar. Our mission is to help everyone be like Mike.


I am indebted to my fellow directors and supporters of the Digital Dollar Project, including Charlie Giancarlo, Luke Giancarlo, Daniel Gorfine, Elizabeth Gray, Jennifer Lassiter, Natalia Bailey, Mandy DeFillippo, Dave Treat, Dominic Paolino, Steve Murphy, Laura Jehl, Dan Bernstein, David Kretz, Frank LaSalla, David Malpass, Tom Zschach, Jai Massari, Corey Then, Jennifer O’Rourke, Laura Jehl, Sarah Hammer, and so many others.


I am grateful to the executives and board directors at Nomura, especially Kentaro Okuda, Yutaka Nakajima, Toshiyasu Iiyama, Chris Willcox, Steve Ashley, Sotaro Kato, Satoshi Kawamura, Mitsutaka Kitamura, Vincent Primiano, Eugene Chiulli, Faron Webb, Kanako Taguchi, Yuko Miyawaki, Paricia Mosser, and Nellie Lang. I am especially grateful for the friendship and mentoring of distinguished and celebrated world citizen, Victor Chu. I fondly recall his gallantly hosting the entire Nomura board of directors for dinner on opening night of the 2025/2026 season at Hong Kong’s famous Jockey Club. In the fifth race, I put all my money on a horse that was a 15-to-1 shot to win. The race was neck to neck down the straight and a photo finish. To my delight, the horse, whose name was “Wrote a New Page,” was declared the winner. That evening confirmed that completing this manuscript and publishing this book was my destiny. Thank you, Victor.


I thank many treasured business colleagues, including at Digital Asset, especially Susan Hauser, Yuval Rooz, Eric Saraniecki, Manoj Ramia and Enmet Rios; at Paxos, especially Chad Cascarilla, Walter Hessert and Leighton Dellinger; at Sygnum Bank, including Gabriela Maria Payer, Mathias Imbach, and Thomas Eichenberger; and at 7Ridge, including Carsten Kengeter, Veronica Augustsson, Cris Conte, Vikas Shaw, and Jeff Maron. I thank my former colleagues at Willkie Farr & Gallagher, including Jeff Poss, Matt Feldman, Tom Cerabino, Bill Gump, Ken O’Connor, Bill Stellmach, Conrad Bahlke, and many others, including the members of Willkie Digital Works, A. Kristina Littman, Kari Larsen, Chelsea Pizzola, Tamika Bent, Matt Comstock, Rita Molesworth, and Jenna Fattah. I thank my new colleagues at Patomak Global Partners, especially Robby Greene, Kathy Casey, Pamela Geraghty, Regina Thoele, and Ella O’Malley, and at Jefferies Financial Group, especially Richard Handler, Alex Yavorsky, and Tim Shea for their warm welcome. My further thanks to Harvard Law Professor Hal Scott and his team at the Program on International Financial Systems: John Gulliver, Stefan Gavell and Jon Ondredjko.


I commend the new leadership at the CFTC and SEC, especially Mike Selig, Paul Atkins, Amir Zaidi, Michael Passalacqua and Mel Gunewardena and many White House and Congressional leaders, including Tim Scott, Bill Hagerty, Cynthia Lummis, Kirsten Gillibrand, French Hill, J.T. Thompson, Paul Balzano, Scott Bessent, David Sacks, and Patrick Witt, for their drive and determination to unleash American innovation and free enterprise. Plus Ultra! I am grateful for the collegiality of many fine former CFTC staff leaders and fellow commissioners, including Russ Behnam, Dan Berkovitz, Dawn Stump and Brian Quintenz, as well as cross town peers Hester Peirce and Brian Brooks. My thanks also go to David Wright, Graham Mather, and their fellow leaders of the European Financial Forum. I trust my account accords with their recollection.


I acknowledge many dear friends, colleagues, and intellectual influences: Doc and Ellen Sandor, Jason and Lisa Goggins, Matt and Erica Richardson, Cameron and Tyler Winklevoss, Laurie Katz, Zach Pandl, Brooke Stoddard of Grayscale, Shayne Coplan, Vanessa Grellet, Nicolo Stohr and the CFC team, Don Wilson, Jeff Sprecher, Niall Ferguson, Law Professors Yesha Yadev and Chris Brummer, Chris Dixon, Stu Alderoty and Ali Tager of the NCA, Mike Cagney, Heath Tarbert, Dante Disparte, Yoni Assia, Hedva Bar, Debbie Kahal, Laura Unger, Adriana Ennab, Roshan Robert, Pat LaVecchia, Ed Woodford, Jim Tabacchi, Avichal Garg, Charlie Gasperino, Tom Farley, Scott Melker, Katie Haun, Richard Sandor, Changpeng Zhao, Bruce Tuckman, Sharon Bowen, Lee Schneider, Andy Brown, Jay Schulman (of course), Elaine Asher, Chris Perkins, Justin Slaughter, Justin Browder, Sam Tegel, John D’Agostino, Anthony Pompliano, Michael Piwowar, Paolo Saguato, Chen Arad, Ryo Nozowa, Brad Kimes, Catherine Gu, Ryan Rugg, Cody Carbone, Alan Howard, Micah Green, John Sodergreen, Jan van Eck, Joey Krug, Joseph Lubin, Steve Haft, Asaf Meir, Donna Redel, Matthew Roszak, Perianne Boring, Coleen Sullivan, Skidmore President Marc Connor, and Vanderbilt Law Dean Chris Guthrie.


I thank my dear in-laws: Harry and Jenn Beyel, Brian and Monica Macdonald, and Steve and Lisa Beyel and their terrific kids for their kindness and laughter. See you at Thanksgiving.


I am ever grateful for my dear brothers: Charlie, Mike, and Tim. Our family’s road got rocky midway with many a winding turn since. But we’re still standing, and that is what matters. I love you guys and your beautiful families.


I thank my children Emma, Luke, and Henry, and my daughter-in-law Rachel, and want you to know that you are all such a blessing. I treasure every moment we spend together. I am enormously proud of each of you and love you so much.


Here is where I run out of words that can express the infinite debt of gratitude I owe Regina for supporting me with her sweet love, peace, and care these soon-to-be 40 years together. You made everything possible. I love you with every fiber of my being. I always will.

# About the Author



The Honorable J. Christopher Giancarlo served as the thirteenth chairman of the US Commodity Futures Trading Commission. Considered one of “the most influential individuals in financial regulation,” he also served as a member of the Financial Stability Oversight Council, the President’s Working Group on Financial Markets, and the Executive Board of the International Organization of Securities Commissions.


Giancarlo is a senior strategic advisor to Patomak Global Advisors. He is also a cofounder and chairman of the Digital Dollar Project (digitaldollarproject.org), and a board director, advisor, and investor in numerous public and private technology, digital asset, and financial services companies.


Giancarlo has been recognized by Chambers as a leading US attorney for “Blockchain & Cryptocurrencies,” named a “Top Innovator in Financial Markets” by TabbForum, and ranked among the 10 foremost “Crypto Lawyers” by the American Crypto Association. He was appointed Chevalier in the National Order of Merit by French President Macron in recognition of his expertise in cryptofinance.


Giancarlo holds a Bachelor of Arts from Skidmore College and a Juris Doctor from Vanderbilt University School of Law. His first book, CryptoDad: The Fight for the Future of Money (Wiley, 2022), chronicles his oversight of the world’s first regulated market for Bitcoin derivatives and the digital transformation of financial services.
